#3125. Ring’s new drone patrols your home to keep an eye on things – even upstairs

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Smart Home > Home Security

Alongside the Ring Drone Cam, Ring introduced the Rover Cam for businesses. It investigates disturbances and streams 4K footage.

Today, Ring announced the launch of a new subbrand, Ring Robotics, with two new products: the $249 Ring Drone Cam, an indoor “mobile security camera” drone that shoots 2K video, and the $999 Ring Rover Cam, a security robot for businesses that patrols autonomously and investigates disturbances.

Also: Amazon’s new Echo Studio gives Alexa+ an AI camera to see inside your home

Both devices use Ring’s custom navigation software, “AI Pilot,” to autonomously navigate interior spaces and move between set points along a patrol, rather than being controlled directly by the user. AI Pilot leverages the devices’ cameras, sensors, and vision models, along with cloud processing, to create interior maps, then move the devices between predesignated patrol points such as a side door or a window.

I recently saw both new robots in person at a showcase in New York hosted by Ring’s founder and chief inventor, Jamie Siminoff. They certainly do what they advertise but come with some significant limitations. The drone, for example, can only stay in the air for about 8 minutes before it needs to dock and recharge, while the Ring Rover Cam is slow and deliberate, seemingly ideal for routine surveillance rather than rapid response to an event.

The Ring Drone Cam moves around predetermined patrol points in your home, sending you a live 2K video feed whether you’re just in another room or out of town. The user doesn’t actually fly the drone; instead, it moves around autonomously using Ring’s custom “AI Pilot” navigation technology, leveraging its cameras, sensors, and cloud processing to create a map of its surroundings. This allows it to fly through the air at an appropriate height, going from room to room, even up stairs.

In the Ring app, the user creates patrol points they want the drone to move between — likely a window, doorway, or high-traffic room — creating a set path that is both predictable (so you always know where the drone will go) and relevant, so it’s only monitoring the areas you want it to. But here is where its limitations come into play: It needs a finite path to navigate because its flight time is limited to just five to eight minutes in the air before it has to return to its charging dock.

That makes the dock both the start and end point for the drone’s routine trips, and it’s designed with this in mind. When it goes to land, the drone hovers over the dock and lowers itself down, dropping into place so it can start charging automatically. I asked about its potential to land elsewhere, and Ring told me it would only make an emergency landing outside of its dock in case it didn’t have enough power to get home.

Also: Amazon’s new Echo Dot can turn into a Wi-Fi extender – how it works

There’s one other very important caveat: This thing is loud. There’s no sneaking up on anyone with the Ring Drone Cam, as the entire household is likely to hear it the second it undocks. The four propellers emit a constant, high-pitched whirring while in the air that’s loud enough to announce its presence to anyone in the vicinity: intruder or resident.

During the product briefing, Siminoff insinuated that this was part of its design to dissuade home intruders snooping around your house at night (especially when paired with the drone’s front-facing spotlight), but it’s likely just the result of its engineering, especially considering this is a first-gen product. It’s so loud that it makes me think it’s better suited to navigating the home when no one’s home (although whether or not your pets go bonkers trying to catch it is a whole other issue).

The Ring Drone is designed to work out of the box with the existing Ring ecosystem, including the 2K Video Doorbell and Indoor Cam, and is also compatible with Amazon’s Map View.

The Ring Drone Cam will start shipping on Jan. 27, 2027, and will cost $249.

The other product Ring announced is the Rover Cam, a mobile robot with a 4K camera designed to monitor large spaces like warehouses, offices, or businesses. Ring calls it a “security guard that never clocks out,” but it’s more like a slow-moving droid that goes on set patrol paths. It leverages the same AI Pilot technology as the Ring Drone to map its surroundings and navigate around shelving, furniture, and obstacles in real time.

It’s much quieter than its airborne sibling, traveling at low speeds and offering mobile surveillance instead of wiring every square inch of a large space. If the technology here looks familiar, it’s because it resembles the Amazon Astra, a smaller robot designed for home monitoring but with a very similar build.

The idea here is to deploy the robot to certain areas of a workplace via the app, then get 360-degree views once it’s there. Like the drone, it can also set out on automated routes at select timing intervals — like right after a business closes or before it opens. During working hours, it chugs along with its camera, capturing everything in its view.

Also: The 4 biggest Amazon smart home announcements from today

If another Ring device is triggered, the Rover Cam autonomously goes to investigate the disturbance, providing 4K footage in real time via the app. It also features a periscope camera that can extend to see over obstacles like shelving, counters, or furniture, providing a high-level view if needed. In case you lose it in a large warehouse or office, it has a “light tower” that projects light upward onto the ceiling, notifying users of its location.

Even when docked, it uses motion-sensing technology to start recording the second it senses any kind of movement, just like Ring doorbell cameras. In case of an emergency, the Rover features a siren and red flashing lights to dissuade intruders.

The Ring Rover Cam is available for preorder now and will start shipping on Dec. 16, 2026. It will cost $999.

#3124. Google Pixel Tag Hands-On: The AirTag for Android Fans

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Google’s own tracker adds a few welcome details to the smart tracker field.

When a company comes out with its own version of a product in an established market, there has to be a good reason. Maybe it’s created a design that sets the product apart, or it’s included features that none of the competition has.

If you think I’m talking about Apple and Google, you’d be right – but this isn’t the iPhone Duo muscling into the folding phone fray. This time, the roles are reversed: Google is releasing the Pixel Tag, a small Bluetooth tracker that it hopes will be the Android ecosystem’s answer to Apple’s near-ubiquitous AirTag.

The Pixel Tag is available starting Wednesday for $30 each or $100 in a pack of four. It works with Android phones and uses Google’s Find Hub technology for tracking over distances beyond Bluetooth range. If you use an Android phone and have wanted the capability to track things such as keys, bags, bikes, vehicles and other indispensable items, and you want the tech to come from the company that designed the system, the Pixel Tag is an excellent choice.

It isn’t the first Android-compatible tracker. Motorola introduced its Moto Tag 2 earlier this year, and Tile has been the de facto AirTag alternative for several years. There’s also now a growing ecosystem of other items that tap into Apple’s Find My and Android’s Find Hub networks, ranging from slim credit-card-sized devices like the Rolling Square AirCard Pro and Peak Design’s Beacon Finder Card, to novel approaches like Falcam’s TagBatt, a battery for some Sony and Canon mirrorless cameras that integrates Apple Find My tracking.

Google jumping into this more crowded field tells me the company thinks there’s profit to be made by manufacturing its own hardware and software. It’s also investing in the infrastructure it’s already built out, and not leaving the hardware side to a constellation of other vendors.

Trackers like the Pixel Tag and AirTag can be a surprisingly deflating purchase: You pair them with your phone, put them into something you don’t want to lose track of – then forget about them. They’re usually not tactile the way a phone, watch or laptop is each day. And their value doesn’t kick into action until you think an item has gone missing.

After slinging AirTags into bags and dangling them off keychains, one thing I really appreciate about the Pixel Tag is its thinness. Apple chose a small, round footprint, even keeping the exact design for the second-generation AirTags. Google instead opted for a longer lozenge shape, at 1.8 inches (46.1mm), that is about two-thirds the height of the AirTag at 0.2 inch (5.4mm).

Google sent a Bellroy leather keychain as a sample accessory for review, and paired with the Pixel Tag, the two are a fine size for a pocket or clipped to an inside bag pocket. I thought the longer design would get in the way, but it’s not bad at all.

The Pixel Tag uses a CR2032 coin-sized battery that should last about a year (the same as the AirTag). It also has an IP67 rating, which is durable in 1 meter of water for up to 30 minutes.

Another departure from the AirTag is a physical button, making the Pixel Tag even more tactile. When you’re using Find Hub on your phone to locate it by sound, press once to stop the chirps it makes. Or, if you have the tag in hand (on a keychain, for example) and need to find your phone, press twice to ring the phone – it makes a pleasant sing-songy chime that gets louder and more intense as it continues, so I’m confident it’ll be easy to locate, even among couch cushions.

Speaking of the chimes, credit to the Google sound designers who came up with the Pixel Tag’s tune. It has three levels of loudness you can set, and seems plenty audible enough when you’re spelunking the living room for it. I measured it at 80 decibels, which when compared to the second-generation AirTag volume, is 7 less than the 87 decibels of Apple’s tracker.

One more nice touch compared to the AirTag: the Find Hub app shows the tag’s battery status. If that gets to Low or Very Low, the app sends you notifications every two weeks until the battery is replaced. After Apple showed battery statuses on AirTags in early versions of the Find My app, the company changed the Find My app so it only indicates battery when it’s low and needs to be replaced.

The best feature of either tracker is the ability to locate its exact location – not which building or room it’s in, but to tell if the item fell behind the couch or was accidentally dropped in the driveway. (Yes, I’m making up these examples from personal experience.)

Precision Finding uses a combination of Bluetooth 6.0 (via a feature called Channel Sounding) and the ultra-wideband chips inside the tag and the phone to directionally find the tracker when it’s within Bluetooth range. You hold the phone upright, perpendicular to the ground for the best connection. Then you wander your space, watching the indicator change as you get closer to the tag.

On the Pixel Tag, that indication is a wobbly circle shape that fills in as you get closer; when you’re on top of the tracker, the shape is fully filled. The Find Hub app also uses the camera and UWB chip to include an estimated distance in feet and inches (or meters and centimeters). When you’re close enough, the tracking shape becomes a directional arrow to point you the right way. UWB is compatible with the Pixel 8 Pro, Pixel 8 Fold and newer Pixel phones.

In my testing, the Find Nearby feature performed fairly well. Find Hub using a Pixel 11 Pro XL connected from within about 25 feet, including on multiple levels of a house when I was above or below it.

Perhaps because I was raised on science-fiction devices like the trackers in Aliens, though, I expected more consistent readings. There were times when both I and the tag remained stationary. The distance estimates would fluctuate by several feet.

Since I’ve had this review unit for only a couple of days, I haven’t yet had a chance to send it off with someone else and see if I can track it remotely using the Find Hub network. When nearby Android devices detect the Pixel Tag’s presence, they should securely and privately relay the tracker’s location to Google’s servers. (Apple’s Find My network operates the same way.) I’ll update this article once I’ve taken the tag on such an adventure.

Tracking all your stuff is invaluable – I learned this first-hand earlier this year when I left a small bag containing my passport in a hotel room, and was able to convince the hotel that the bag was still on the premises. But unfortunately, smart tags are being used to track people without their knowledge.

Google is taking the same approach as Apple to help thwart this type of tracking. If a tag is separated from the phone it’s paired with (or the phone of someone else you’ve chosen to share the tag location with), and the tag is moved, its chime activates.

The idea is that you’ll hear an unfamiliar chirp and investigate. It has an NFC chip that displays its information when you tap it using the NFC built into a phone.

#3122. 5 apps I need Siri AI to work with now that it’s open to third-party developers

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Smart Home > Smart Assistants

With the latest betas for iOS and other platforms, Siri AI is now available to all developers for full integration into their apps. Here are the ones I hope will jump on board.

One of the many benefits of the new Siri AI is its ability to work directly with apps on your device via a feature known as App Intents. Currently, this skill includes many of Apple’s own built-in apps and some third-party ones. But with the latest public and developer betas for iOS 27.3, the company is expanding support to all third-party developers.

Also: Ready for a Siri AI agent? Apple under pressure to upgrade

With App Intents, you’re able to ask Siri to tackle a variety of actions among various built-in and third-party apps. On a basic level, you can tell it to perform tasks such as getting directions, playing music, scheduling meetings, sending messages, adding reminders, and finding photos.

On a more advanced level, you can ask it to do things like organize your inbox, edit a photo, and analyze and help you with your current screen. You could also tell it to combine several actions in one shot, work across multiple apps, and engage in a natural back-and-forth conversation.

Introduced in 2022 with iOS 16, the App Intents skill does play well with classic Siri, no Apple Intelligence required. This applies not just to built-in apps but also to third-party apps. However, classic Siri lacks the AI smarts and advanced skills of the latest version.

Siri AI fully supports most built-in apps, including Calendar, Files, Finder, Journal, Mail, Maps, Messages, Music, Notes, Photos, Preview, Reminders, Safari, Shortcuts, and Weather. It does support some third-party apps, but its more advanced features are limited or unavailable.

That’s the gap the new developer betas aim to close. With iOS 27.3, iPadOS 27.3, MacOS 27.3, and VisionOS 27.3, all developers will be able to use Siri AI to fully support and integrate with their apps. For example, instead of manually opening an app like DoorDash or Grubhub to check or change a food order, you can tell Siri to do it for you.

Also: The Siri AI waitlist is gone in iOS 27: How to try the beta now

“Today’s preview allows any app to bring its content and actions directly into Siri AI by using App Intents, making it easier for all apps to tap into Siri AI’s newest capabilities, including personal context understanding, on-screen awareness, natural language, and more,” Apple said in a news release for developers. “So people can just ask for help in their own words, and Siri AI carries out any custom action you’ve built, from checking a delivery to booking a reservation to placing an order.”

As I use many third-party apps on my iPhone and iPad, both personally and professionally, I’m looking forward to enhanced support for the ones I rely on. With that in mind, here are five apps for which I’d welcome the new and full Siri AI integration.

I often use Amazon on my iPhone and iPad to shop for and purchase items, review my wishlist, and check the status of products I’ve ordered. To do any of those things, I have to open the app and manually go to the right screens. How convenient it would be if I could tell Siri to handle those tasks for me.

“Siri, search for the 512GB 2026 iPad mini on Amazon and add it to my shopping cart.”

“Siri, review my Amazon wishlist for the luggage I recently added and tell me the latest prices.”

“Siri, check the delivery status of the Alexa photo frame I just ordered on Amazon.”

Right now, Siri can’t do any of those things. But if Siri AI and App intents are fully integrated with Amazon, I may just be able to accomplish those tasks in the future.

IMDB is my go-to app for getting info on movies, TV shows, performers, directors, and more. After I open the app and run a search, I often want specific details, such as the rating and runtime of a particular film or TV episode.

Also: AI completely changed how I develop iPhone apps

To simplify that process, I’ve love to be able to ask Siri: “Siri, search IMDB for the 1935 movie ‘The Bride of Frankenstein,’ give me the running time, tell me where I can stream it, and add it to my watchlist.” If Siri could accomplish all that at some point, it would get a round of applause from me.

I borrow and read a lot of e-books through my local library systems, and I use the handy Libby app for that. Here, I can search different libraries for books by title, author, or subject. I can then borrow a book and read it through the Libby app or, more typically, through the Amazon Kindle app. For this, I have to manually open the app and search among the various libraries for the book I want.

Also: I lost 100 pounds in 7 months: How a smart scale and Apple Watch helped

Instead, I’d like to ask Siri to do that for me. I might say: “Siri, look in Libby for books about Alan Turing, give me the titles of the ones you find, and tell me which ones are currently available to borrow.”

My wife and I are big theater buffs, and we see a lot of plays on Broadway when we’re in New York and on the West End when we’re in London. Often, I buy tickets through the TodayTix service and app. For this, I’ll open the app, set the location to New York or London, look for a specific play, and then try to find seats for a certain day and time.

If TodayTix is ever fully supported by Siri AI and App Intents, I might be able to ask Siri to track down the right seats for me. I could say: “Siri, look for the play ‘Rent’ in London, give me the available performances for Dec. 5 through 9, and let me know which seats are accessible in the first 10 rows.”

As my wife and I often travel and dine out, Yelp is another app I frequently use. Here, I also have to manually launch the app and run searches for restaurants and other places. I then have to try to filter or sort the results based on location, price, and ratings.

Also: 3 handy iPhone features that make my life easier – even doing laundry

Asking Siri to tackle those tasks would certainly be a time saver. I could ask it to “find the closest Italian restaurants using Yelp and show me ones within a one-mile radius that have earned four stars or higher.”

With App Intents, Siri AI can already handle advanced, complex, multi-step tasks for built-in apps. With all developers now able to take advantage of this same support, I’m expecting a lot of apps to jump on board. Let’s hope my favorites end up on the list.

With such a healthy crop to choose from, I'm shouting out the three new movies on Tubi that I'm watching this month. These three phenomenal movies are ones I think the critics got right, followed by one I think they got dead wrong. Whether you're in the mood for a Tom Hanks/Leonardo DiCaprio action caper or a cozy autumnal oasis of nostalgia, there's something for everyone's taste on this list. You can put away your wallet and skip the endless scrolling, because these fantastic movies are available to stream completely free.

The words "cat-and-mouse chase" get thrown around a lot these days, but credit where credit is due to THE best example in modern movie history. "Catch Me If You Can" is one of those movies that always had me sat down whenever it came on cable growing up. The high-stakes chase between DiCaprio's lovable schiester Frank Abagnale Jr. and Tom Hanks' crochety FBI agent Carl Hanratty is addictive to watch unfold, even when you already know where all the cards land.

A brilliant con artist, Frank hops around the world bluffing his way into one industry after another to keep the cops off his scent. Fake it till you make it has rarely been taken this literally. He poses as a pilot, doctor, and lawyer — and all before his 19th birthday — with enough charisma that no one ever thinks to call his bluff. That is, until the FBI catches wind, led by Hanks playing against type as the buttoned-up straight man bureaucrat on Frank's heels. It's Leo at his peak putting on the charm — what's not to love?

Watch "Catch Me if You Can" free on Tubi (with ads) now

With the phenomenal "Wyldwood" still in theaters (seriously! go watch it!), I'd be remiss not to shout out Laika's first hit "Coraline," the ground-breaking "baby's first horror movie" that put the stop-motion studio on the map. Based on Neil Gaiman's terrifying children's novel of the same name, "Coraline" perfectly captures how terrifying being a kid can be. This peak mall goth Y2K adventure also sees the return of "Nightmare Before Christmas" director Henry Selick.

Coraline (voiced by Dakota Fanning) is a precocious kid bored out of her skull in her family's lame new home in the Pacific Northwest. When her neighbors give her a doll that leads her to a secret door, she discovers an alternate world that mirrors her own — only better. At least at first. Coraline is delighted by her Other Mother and new (if slightly off) friends, but when they offer to let her stay there forever on the condition that she has buttons sewn over her eyes, Coraline does everything she can to escape her new living nightmare.

You bet your ass an animated movie is the #1 ranked movie on this list. I think most war movies suck and this is one of maybe like three I can recommend with my full chest. "Waltz with Bashir" blends documentary with fantasy in a meditative reflection on the ways trauma affects our memory. All gorgeously animated in a style reminiscent of "Watchmen" and "Maus" mixed with whatever good soup Gerard Way's got cooking up in there.

Written, produced, and directed by Ari Folman, it follows Folman after a friend tells him of a recurring dream about their time serving in the Israeli army in the 1982 Lebanon War. Folman starts seeing images in his own mind that he can't quite piece together, and to hunt down answers, starts interviewing others, as shown through dreamlike visuals overlaid with one of the most effective musical scores of the decades. It's hard to explain why this movie is a shot in the heart, but now that it's streaming free for Pete's sake, I can't recommend "Waltz with Bashir" enough. It's well worth your time.

I didn't think it was possible to get queasier than Stanley Tucci's Caesar Flickerman, and I was mistaken. This prequel to "The Hunger Games" film series jumps back to the early days of Panem, long before Katniss Everdeen sparked the flames of revolution.

Tom Blyth ("Scott and Sid," "Benediction") stars as a teenage Snow (played by "Donald Sterling" in the previous movies), who's falling into all the pitfalls of adolescence just as the 10th annual Hunger Games kick off. Snow is reluctantly assigned to mentor Lucy Gray Baird (Rachel Zegler) from District 12. As the last hope for the once-proud Snow family, failing isn't an option. His hunger for power sets off a chain of events that culminates in him becoming the tyrannical monarch of Panem. While this movie got a middling response from critics, it remains one of the most faithful adaptions of Suzanne Collins' work to date.

#3119. Reddit launches AI-powered Reviews feature – with 10,000 products to start

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Reddit is adding AI-generated product reviews that compile real user sentiment from across communities. Here's how they work.

If you regularly turn to Reddit for product advice, your experience is about to change.

Reddit announced a new feature on Tuesday called Reddit Reviews, which takes conversations from subreddits across the site and compiles them into AI-generated, in-depth product summaries. The feature works much like Google AI Overviews and is likely an attempt to better compete with them.

When you search for a product on Reddit, you’ll see an “easy-to-digest format” that has a “what people are saying” recap. A visual breakdown will show whether conversations are generally positive, mixed, or negative, as well as common sentiments among users. If you want to go deeper, Reddit says you can browse comments about a product, apply topic filters, read original user discussions, ask follow-up questions, or explore other commonly compared products.

In an example shared by Reddit, a user searches for “Best headphones 2026.” The search returns several results with tags like “Very positive,” “Positive,” and “Mixed.” Clicking on one expands to a review that says, “Reddit likes the XL224’s fantastic battery life, powerful bass, and strong sound quality, while the steep price and fit on smaller heads draws criticism.”

“A mix of generative AI and other in-house technology” is used to identify themes and summarize what people are saying, a representative explained, and added that “the technologies and models we use may evolve over time to best support the experience.”

Perhaps the most important part is that Reddit Reviews are shaped by users, and companies can’t pay to have product features. For a community sensitive to commercialization, this is a big deal.

My first concern was about bots or other fake reviews, but a Reddit representative said they’re taking that into consideration. “Platform-wide,” the representative said, “we are labeling apps and prompting accounts we detect to be running automations to either verify there’s a human behind the username or be labeled as an app.” In this context, an app is a registered API client that’s developed to interact with Reddit (including posting, commenting, upvoting, and scraping). In short, the system will label known bots.

Also: Ring’s new drone patrols your home to keep watch – even upstairsSpecifically for reviews, a “review conversation” will appear that describes a person’s experience with a product. Reddit also has “defined rules,” it says, “to help our LLMs identify these review conversations based on the surrounding conversation for context.”

You’ll start to see Reddit Reviews over the next few weeks in the US on iOS, Android, and browser versions of the site. Around 10,000 products across electronics, health and beauty, home and garden, and fashion and apparel will be available to start.

I find myself frequently adding “reddit” to Google searches for everything from the best restaurants in a city to the best headphones for my kids, so these overviews are definitely intriguing. It’s not easy right now to wade through numerous threads, so if this does make it easier to get true user sentiment, it could be a big plus.

#3118. Google Fi’s ‘Super Network’ Hops Networks to Keep Your Pixel Phone Connected

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Automatic network switching for Pixel phones could mean fewer mobile dead zones.

Even in areas that seem abundant with overlapping cellular coverage, phones sometimes sputter when you’re looking up information or trying to get directions when your carrier’s signal gets weak. To avoid those annoying interruptions, Google Fi Wireless is rolling out what it’s calling a “super network” feature. It automatically switches your phone between two 5G networks to maintain the best connection.

The new capability works only on Google’s phones, starting with the Pixel 9 and newer Pixel models.

The super network is included free for existing Google Fi customers on all of its plans: Flexible, Unlimited Essentials, Unlimited Standard and Unlimited Premium.

Google says that it’s using “two top 5G networks,” but the company is refusing to reveal which networks those are in the US. Traditionally Google Fi runs on T-Mobile’s network.

The network switching also applies when you’re traveling internationally, and is included in the Unlimited Premium and Flexible plans.

According to a source at Google, the phone will use two eSIM slots to switch between networks, but it will appear to only use a single eSIM on the customer’s phone. A fine-print disclaimer notes that any other eSIMs on your device must be disabled, which is worth knowing for anyone that already uses multiple carriers on a single device.

In addition to the super network news, Google said its Wi-Fi Auto Connect Plus feature is available in millions of more locations across the US. If the phone is in an area where cellular is spotty and a compatible Wi-Fi network is nearby, it automatically makes an encrypted connection if the Wi-Fi network is stronger.

Wi-Fi Auto Connect Plus is available on Pixel phones, but is also becoming available on Samsung phones running One UI 9 or later and “select modern Motorola phones.”

#3116. I drove the Nissan Armada and Dodge Durango for a week — here’s the sporty 3-row SUV I'd actually buy

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Our weekly newsletter is expertly crafted to immerse you in the world of streaming. Stay updated on the latest releases and our top recommendations across your favorite streaming platforms.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

They prioritize driving performance over everything

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I’ve said it countless times this year: If there’s one type of vehicle I’ve driven more than anything else, it’s the 3-row family hauler. When you’re looking for maximum passenger capacity without skimping on comfort, full-size SUVs certainly fit the bill — and the Nissan Armada and Dodge Durango are no exception.

Unlike mainstream models like the Chevrolet Tahoe or GMC Yukon Denali that prioritize plush comfort over driving dynamics — often making them feel like lumbering giants — these two are far from it. Instead, these heavyweights prove that family practicality and raw enthusiasm can genuinely coexist.

The $55,225 as-tested 2026 Dodge Durango R/T 392 AWD Launch Edition delivers pure old-school muscle with a 475-hp 6.4-liter HEMI V8 engine that sounds genuinely imposing. That looks like a steal next to the $83,735 as-tested price of the 2026 Nissan Armada Nismo, but the Nismo counters with a twin-turbo V6 pushing 460 horsepower, sharper styling, and enough high-tech amenities to make a case for its steeper sticker.

After testing each one for a week, only one earns my recommendation.

2026 Dodge Durango R/T 392 AWD Launch Edition

NISMO-Tuned 3.5L Twin-Turbo V6 Engine (4WD)

17 mpg city / 16 mpg highway / 19 mpg combined

13 mpg city / 19 mpg highway / 15 mpg combined

Frankly, neither design captured my attention at first — but one of them eventually grew on me. Even though they compete in the full-size, 3-row segment, they take entirely different styling routes.

The 2026 Dodge Durango R/T 392 Launch Edition leans hard into classic muscle swagger — reminiscent of the Dodge Charger — with its scooped performance hood, functional air intakes, bold Brembo brake calipers, and a rounded profile that still looks surprisingly menacing. It catches plenty of glances thanks to that aggressive stance, but it lacks the visual flair to truly sell me on its familiar look.

Between the two, the 2026 Nissan Armada Nismo gets my nod. Despite its noticeably more upright, boxy proportions, it delivers far more drama and curb appeal. Nissan gave it the full NISMO treatment, outfitting the SUV with massive 22-inch machined alloy wheels, an aggressive front fascia and grille, sculpted side steps with flared fenders, and full-width LED taillights. The sharp two-tone premium paint finish seals the deal, transforming this traditionally square full-size SUV into an imposing showstopper worthy of a cameo in a Fast & Furious flick.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

2026 Dodge Durango R/T 392 AWD Launch Edition

Nobody shops for performance-tuned, three-row SUVs with the intent of saving on fuel. However, one of these gas-guzzlers still proved friendlier at the pump over the long haul.

The 2026 Dodge Durango R/T 392 Launch Edition surprised me most on dedicated highway runs, achieving a best mark of 24.2 mpg and handily overdelivering on its 19-mpg highway rating. On short-distance trips, I averaged 21.6 mpg — equally impressive given its 13-mpg city EPA estimate. Overall, it managed 18.1 mpg across 237.9 total miles of mixed driving.

In comparison, the 2026 Nissan Armada Nismo made a much stronger case for real-world consistency. While its best recorded long-distance run of 23.9 mpg fell just shy of the Dodge, its twin-turbo V6 and 9-speed automatic worked far more efficiently across everyday traffic. Over the course of 302 miles of mixed driving, the Armada Nismo delivered an average of 19.9 mpg — not only beating its official 17-mpg combined EPA rating, but also comfortably edging out the Durango by nearly two full miles per gallon overall. When you’re feeding a sport-tuned hauler week in and week out, that spread makes a noticeable difference.

When SUVs are slapped with performance badges, driving dynamics are what make them special over conventional haulers. While the Armada managed to surprise me in a big way, the Durango takes the crown here.

Nissan deserves immense credit for defying expectations given the Armada's boxy proportions, which initially made me apprehensive about driving it on crowded highways. It packs a genuinely strong 3.5-liter twin-turbo V6 pushing 460 horsepower and 516 lb-ft of torque, delivering instant thrust and effortless passing power.

It's also surprisingly easy to drive for its size, thanks to an ultra-responsive steering wheel that feels very light and loose. If that’s not enough, its adaptive electronic air suspension and great shocks make cruising over bumpy roads a breeze.

Yet for all the Armada’s composure, it can’t match the raw power and exhilarating feel of the 2026 Dodge Durango R/T 392 Launch Edition. Backed by a 475-horsepower 6.4-liter HEMI V8 under the hood, it completely transforms the driving experience.

Between the thunderous exhaust roar, immediate throttle response, and quick Brembo stopping power, the Durango delivers a far more thrilling ride every time I put the pedal to the metal. Honestly, it handles like a muscle car — giving enthusiasts the genuine excitement that almost every other three-row hauler lacks.

Both SUVs deliver great accommodations when it comes to passenger capacity, but one takes interior luxury and space to another level. What I love about both full-size haulers is how they offer second-row captain's chairs paired with a convenient center console for cupholders and armrest storage.

In the Dodge Durango R/T 392, second-row passengers have 38.6 inches of legroom to stretch out comfortably, while the third-row bench yields 33.5 inches. Even though adults can fit back there, my knees practically touch the seatbacks in front of me. Finally, there’s a cavernous 17.2 cubic feet of cargo space behind the third row that easily swallowed my electric scooter without needing to wedge it at an angle.

The one big downside for the Durango is that it lacks lane-centering assist when adaptive cruise control is engaged — meaning, it won't actively keep you centered between the lines.

The 2026 Nissan Armada Nismo simply offers more cabin volume to comfortably seat adults while pampering them with genuine luxury. Thanks to its taller cabin, legroom feels far more generous in the second row with 39.2 inches of space, while the third row provides 32.9 inches. The Armada also delivers significantly more cargo capacity behind the rear seats with 20.4 cubic feet of trunk space.

Between its plush, red-and-black trimmed NISMO suede seats, whisper-quiet cabin insulation, and ProPILOT Assist for hands-free highway cruising, the Armada makes long distance drices effortless no matter where you sit. That said, its lane centering isn't always spot-on when activated, but the overall luxury leap is impossible to ignore.

On paper, the pricing advantage leans heavily toward the Durango, but it's important to look at what each model actually delivers before jumping to conclusions.

Dodge’s value proposition is centered squarely on performance, yet this bargain full-size SUV still includes a respectable set of amenities. For example, you get a single-pane sunroof, a Harman Kardon sound system, and a standard backup camera. Still, I’m not as impressed with the dashboard layout, which leans far too conventional and dated.

By contrast, the 2026 Nissan Armada Nismo commands a steep $83,735 as-tested price that’s nearly $28,500 higher than its rival. That’s a significant stretch, but it buys a modern suite of luxury and tech features: an expansive panoramic moonroof, a high-fidelity 12-speaker Klipsch premium audio system, heated front seats, and a sophisticated 3D Enhanced Intelligent Around View Monitor that makes navigating tight parking spaces a breeze.

While the Armada undoubtedly delivers the more modern, tech-rich experience, the Durango's $55,225 as-tested price makes it an untouchable performance bargain in the three-row space.

I can’t overstate the price disparity between these two sport-tuned SUVs, with one costing nearly $28,500 more than the other. At their core, however, both deliver large family-hauling utility without forcing you to completely give up your passion for driving.

Without question, the 2026 Nissan Armada Nismo is the superior all-around modern SUV for everyday living. Between its sharp two-tone finish, plush suede seats, massive passenger volume, whisper-quiet cabin, and cutting-edge tech suite, it feels every bit like an $84,000 flagship that justifies its price tag. If your budget allows, the Armada won't disappoint with its effortless acceleration and smooth, composed ride — making it tough to beat.

2026 Dodge Durango R/T 392 AWD Launch Edition

Yet when it comes to the vehicle I’d actually spend my own money on, the 2026 Dodge Durango R/T 392 AWD Launch Edition gets my vote, despite coming up short on paper once all the category scores are tallied. Sure, the Durango's interior aesthetics lack the modern flair of its rival, but it unapologetically prioritizes genuine muscle car performance with three rows of seats.

With an as-tested price of $55,225, it isn’t just competitive — it's an untouchable performance bargain in a segment otherwise dominated by $60K+ land yachts.

#3115. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The ongoing earnings season, investor concerns about the durability of AI demand and spending, and geopolitical risks are key factors that have been contributing to stock market volatility in recent trading sessions.

In this scenario, investors seeking steady income can consider adding dividend stocks to their portfolios. Recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help them pick attractive dividend stocks that are backed by solid cash flows to support consistent payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips is this week's first dividend pick. With a dividend of 84 cents per share (annualized dividend of $3.36 per share), COP offers a dividend yield of 3%. The company is scheduled to announce its second-quarter results on Aug. 6.

Ahead of second-quarter results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin reiterated a buy rating on COP stock with a price target of $183. Despite the pressure on oil prices from an increase in OPEC production quota, the analyst finds ConocoPhillips and Shell stocks appealing as the earnings season approaches. He cited their operational visibility and resilience as factors backing their appeal.

The 5-star analyst expects ConocoPhillips to meet its production guidance of 2.2 million barrels of oil equivalent per day at the mid-point. He expects lower Waha natural gas prices in the Permian Basin to be offset by stronger Brent crude premiums. Margolin expects capital expenditure to remain within COP's prior guided range of $12.2 billion annualized, with no significant impact on spending on the Northfield East project in Qatar despite the Strait of Hormuz disruption.

Overall, Margolin expects COP to generate about $3.5 billion in free cash flow (before working capital) and earnings per share of $2.94. He expects continued strength in COP's free cash flow and regular dividend growth through the completion of the Willow project in 2028/2029. Prior to the Willow project coming online, the analyst expects free cash flow to grow by about $2 billion in 2027 and 2028, assuming Brent crude averages around $60 per barrel.

"COP's track record of capital efficiency and strong Permian well productivity underpins its ability to pursue long-cycle developments," said Margolin.

Margolin ranks No. 457 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 70% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.3%. See ConocoPhillips Financials on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer is a limited partnership that operates 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. With a quarterly cash distribution of 33.75 cents per common unit ($1.35 per unit on an annualized basis), ET offers a yield of 6.8%.

Heading into Energy Transfer's Q2 earnings on Aug. 4, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith reaffirmed a buy rating on ET stock with a price target of $23. The analyst noted that his adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization estimate of $4.46 billion is 1% below the Street's consensus of $4.49 billion.

The 5-star analyst noted that Energy Transfer has slightly outperformed Enterprise Products Partners recently. However, it still trades at a relative discount of 19% compared to EPD, which is below its historical discount range of 17%-20%. Smith believes that ET stock could be re-rated higher if the company provides a clearer long-term strategy for natural gas growth.

Furthermore, Smith expects the current energy market to support a stronger outlook for natural gas liquids and crude oil. "The current energy macro backdrop positions ET to benefit in all three commodities," said the analyst.

He expects Energy Transfer's adjusted EBITDA to grow at a 4.8% compound annual growth rate in 2027-2030, which is 1%-3% above Wall Street's expectations. In fact, Smith sees the possibility of additional upside if ET announces more natural gas projects. He added that investors will await details on final investment decisions on new natural gas projects and any clues about additional projects in the pipeline. The analyst noted that ET has announced new gas projects consistently in recent quarters.

Smith ranks No. 550 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.4%. See Energy Transfer Statistics on TipRanks.

Finally, let's look at energy giant Chevron, which is scheduled to announce its second-quarter results on July 31. Last month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12, CVX offers a dividend yield of 3.92%.

Ahead of Q2 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on Chevron stock and lowered his price target to $216 from $236. Byrne expects the company to report adjusted EPS of about $5.86 per share, nearly 9% above the Street's expectations.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the challenges seen in Chevron's upstream business in the first quarter due to the disruption at the Tengizchevroil joint venture in Kazakhstan, Storm Fern downtime, and the Middle East conflict have largely been resolved. Consequently, Byrne expects production to recover in the second quarter to about 4,033 mboepd. He expects the upstream business to generate adjusted earnings of about $8.1 billion in Q2 2026.

Meanwhile, Byrne expects Chevron to generate downstream adjusted earnings of about $4.4 billion in Q2, with strength in both domestic and international markets. The downstream business benefited from higher crack spreads and strong refining performance.

Additionally, the analyst expects Chevron to generate $18.2 billion in cash flow from operations (before working capital changes), driven by stronger earnings and about $2.2 billion in dividends from affiliated companies. Unlike the first quarter, Chevron is not expected to make a TCO loan repayment in Q2, providing an additional boost to cash flow.

Byrne ranks No. 409 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 56% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.5%. See Chevron Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#3114. Done with Debian Linux distros? Try Fedora next – 7 reasons why

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

After using a Debian-based distribution, you may find yourself longing for something different. I know exactly what you should try next.

Back when I was constantly distro-hopping, I found it important to have a “what’s next” mentality. That way, I could plan accordingly, instead of jumping in blind. I could prepare myself for the installation, the package management system, the desktop, and other necessary bits and pieces.

Also: Fedora Everything let me build the Linux desktop I actually want

But more than anything, I always found it best to know which type of distribution should be next.

You wouldn’t want to jump from Ubuntu to, say, Gentoo, as the leap in required skills is considerable. You also wouldn’t want to jump from Ubuntu to Debian, since Ubuntu is based on Debian, making it a lateral move. From my experience, when coming from an Ubuntu-based distribution, the best next distro is one based on Fedora.

This statement wouldn’t hold water five years ago because Fedora often required work to get it running properly. Now, Fedora requires no work or effort to get it working out of the box. Hardware support is on par with Ubuntu, modern boot processes are supported, it has an excellent first-boot experience, Nvidia driver support is out of the box, the touchpad works out of the box, suspend and resume work flawlessly, it ships with a newer kernel, it includes built-in support for modern display stacks, and it’s clean and bloat-free.

Like many Linux distributions, Fedora lets you choose your desktop, but it does so better by offering dedicated spins for each. You’ll find Fedora spins for KDE Plasma, Xfce, Cinnamon, Mate-Compiz, i3, LXQT, LXDE, SOAS, Sway, Budgie, Miracle, Plasma Mobile, and COSMIC. Instead of manually installing your desktop of choice, just download the Fedora spin with your desired desktop.

Also: Fedora 44 made me forget I was using Linux – in the best way

Yes, there’s a Debian-based distribution for every desktop, but knowing you can just go to the Fedora Project Spins page and quickly find what you’re looking for makes the process easy. As well, Ubuntu has its own “flavors” (such as Kubuntu, Lubuntu, etc.), so coming from an Ubuntu flavor will make total sense.

When using a Debian-based distribution, if you install/manage software from the command line, you’re used to APT, such as sudo apt install quillmark. Did you know that Fedora’s DNF works in the same way? That’s right: sudo dnf install quillmark will get you the same results as sudo apt install quillmark (so long as the app is found in the Fedora repositories).

The same holds true for removing software. One difference, however, is that when system software is upgraded, you’ll have to reboot a Fedora-based distribution for the upgrades to take effect. The only time you have to reboot on a Debian-based distro upgrade is if the kernel is upgraded in the process.

If you’re using Ubuntu as your Debian-based distribution, you’ll find that it includes a universal package manager, just as Fedora does. On Debian, the package manager is Snap, while on Fedora it’s Flatpak. While these two are different, they do have some similarities, such as app installation.

Also: 5 of my favorite Linux distros for AI – and why

With Snap packages, the installation goes something like this: sudo snap install quillmark. On Fedora, that command would be flatpak install quillmark. You’ll notice that sudo is not used with Flatpak; the reason for this is that Flatpak apps are typically installed on a per-user basis. Yes, you can install them system-wide, but that defeats the purpose of sandboxing.

Both Fedora and Ubuntu are secure, even if they arrive at that security by different routes. For example, Ubuntu has AppArmor and Fedora has SELinux. However, both of these tools manage Mandatory Access Controls — just in different ways. The good news is that you’ll probably never have to touch either system.

Also: Ubuntu 26.04 vs. Fedora 44: After years of testing both, here’s my verdict

Also, both Debian- and Fedora-based distributions tend to ship with a GUI frontend for the firewall. If you don’t find one installed, you can install one through your package manager. It is important to know that the Fedora firewall manager is considerably more powerful than Ubuntu’s, which also means it has a steeper learning curve. The good news is that, out of the box, you probably won’t have to make many changes to the firewall, other than ensuring it’s enabled.

Once upon a time, I would have debated such a statement, but Fedora is now on par with Ubuntu in terms of user-friendliness, especially if you choose one of the more user-friendly spins (such as KDE Fedora, Cinnamon, or MATE-Compiz). Fedora also has a System Settings app similar to Ubuntu’s; each one lets you manage nearly every aspect of the computer, so there’s no need to use the command line with Fedora (unless you want to).

With Debian, the release cycle is every 2 years, and support is 5 years. With Fedora, the release cycle is every 6 months, and support is 13 months. If you’re using an Ubuntu-based distribution (which is based on Debian), the release cycle is the same as Fedora, but there are two versions: LTS and interim. LTS support is considerably longer than interim releases: LTS releases receive 5 years of free support, while interim releases receive 9 months.

Also: My favorite Linux desktop environment for laptops surprised even me

In the end, the Fedora release/support cycle is faster and simpler.

Because both development teams focus on building a user-friendly distribution out of the box, migrating from a Debian-based to a Fedora-based distribution is seamless. When making such a transition, aside from a few minor differences, you’ll feel like you never left your Debian-based distro, especially if you choose a Fedora Spin that includes the same desktop you’ve been using.

#3112. Your FitBit Versa and Sense smartwatches will lose some features this month – what to know

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Time's running out for some Versa and Sense models. After Nov. 28, you can no longer install or update third-party apps or clockfaces on these Google smartwatches.

If you have a Fitbit Versa or Fitbit Sense smartwatch, you’ll soon be unable to update the third-party apps on these devices. Google announced on Monday an update to its older lineup of devices that may signify the beginning of obsolescence. Users can no longer install or update third-party apps or clocks on these Fitbit smartwatches, beginning on Nov. 28.

Also: Google’s Fitbit Air is a $99 screenless wearable that I can actually take seriously

The apps will continue to work after that date, but if users delete any of these apps, they will not be able to reinstall them. The changes affect the Fitbit Sense and Fitbit Sense 2, as well as the Fitbit Versa, Fitbit Versa Lite, Fitbit Versa 2, Fitbit Versa 3, and Fitbit Versa 4.

Google noted that users will still have access to Fitbit- and Google-developed apps and clocks, and that they should update to the latest app versions to keep those apps functioning properly.

The Nov. 28 support cut-off comes right on the heels of this season’s Black Friday sales. (Google’s Pixel Watch 5 just got a $100 discount at Amazon.)

So the smartwatches aren’t dead — they can still perform the same primary functions, like recording workouts or receiving or responding to messages and calls. Still, the news is a downer for Versa and Sense users who’ve been using their smartwatches and the many third-party apps on them for anywhere from two to eight years now.

All signs point to these devices being slowly phased out in favor of Google’s latest lineup of fitness trackers and smartwatches.

Also: Google’s new Fitbit Edge solves my biggest gripe with the Pixel Watch

Within the past few years, Google has unveiled an impressive new lineup of health trackers, including the screenless Fitbit Air, the screened Fitbit Edge, and the Pixel Watch 5. For those looking to ditch their Versa or Sense, a few affordable options exist in the ecosystem to take their place.

The earliest and most recent Fitbit Versa models were launched in 2018 and 2022, respectively, and the first and last Fitbit Sense models launched in 2020 and 2022, respectively — making the oldest of these devices eight years old.

Apple says the update provides bug fixes and security updates for issues on your iPhone, two of which may lead to memory corruption on your device.

#3110. Prediction: Here's What the Nasdaq Will Do in the Last 2 Months of 2026

The Nasdaq recently fell into correction territory.

The Nasdaq Composite has had ups and downs this year, but in recent times, the downs have won out. The tech-heavy benchmark fell into correction territory. Why has the index slipped? A variety of elements have represented headwinds this year. Investors have worried about rising inflation and the potential interest rate increases put into place to contain it.

And the biggest concern of all might be regarding the future of artificial intelligence (AI). Investors question whether big tech's spending on new data centers and equipment will be worth the effort -- or if the revenue opportunity will fall short. All of this has put pressure on tech stocks and, therefore, the Nasdaq, in 2026.

On top of this, Democrats, who have been cautious regarding AI, scored majorities in both the House of Representatives and the Senate in the U.S. midterm elections last week. This reinforced investors' concerns about regulation and limitations on data center growth.

Against this backdrop, you might be wondering what the Nasdaq will do in the last two months of 2026. Here's my prediction.

First, let's take a look at the Nasdaq and tech story so far. As mentioned, technology companies are heavily weighted in the benchmark, so if they are having a good or bad day, the Nasdaq will reflect that. Apple, Nvidia, and Microsoft lead the Nasdaq-100, the index of the largest non-financial companies traded on Nasdaq, with weightings of more than 10% each.

Over the past three years, as the AI boom progressed, the Nasdaq Composite advanced, gaining more than 100% until the recent dip. This is as AI companies reported strong revenue growth and investors got excited about the current and future earnings opportunities. AI was seen as the next big thing in technology, and investors wanted to bet on it early to maximize their gains.

Stocks climbed so much, however, that valuations started to look lofty. And investors began to worry about the return on investment for tech giants paying for the AI infrastructure build-out. Amazon, Alphabet, Microsoft, and Meta Platforms pledged to spend more than $700 billion this year alone. This, along with higher inflation and a tightening of the credit market, has periodically weighed on the performance of tech stocks. And just recently, the Democrats' victory stirred up concern about a potential slowdown in AI growth.

Now, here's my prediction for the last two months of the year. While this month may be a difficult one for the Nasdaq, I predict that the index will advance in December and post a double-digit gain for the year. We aren't far from such a gain now, with the increase being more than 8% from the start of the year through Nov. 9.

History shows that the index has climbed four times in the November-December period over the past six years, and the end of the year is generally known as being a strong period for stocks. The S&P 500 often benefits from a "Santa Claus" rally, with the index finishing positive almost 80% of the time during that period, according to Britannica Money. (That period is the last five trading days of December and the first two days of January.)

So, history supports my prediction, but I'm not basing my ideas on that alone. I also think the Nasdaq may climb because the long-term AI growth story hasn't changed. Even if it faces certain headwinds or a slowdown, quality, well-established tech companies are likely to generate significant growth from their AI investments and products over the long run.

Meanwhile, some of these companies are trading at interesting valuations right now due to the recent decline. Investors are likely to recognize this and may decide to add some of these players to their portfolios at the end of the year -- to prepare for a potential rebound next year.

And that could help the Nasdaq, after falling into correction territory, advance in the last weeks of the year.

#3108. Google’s new AI tool just proved Signal encryption really works – here’s how

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

With the aim of securing other vital systems, Google DeepMind plans to open-source its AlphaProof Code.

Researchers at Google DeepMind used AI to formally verify portions of Signal‘s underlying code — meaning they’ve mathematically proven that at least parts of the encrypted messaging app’s code do what they say, without errors. That’s reassuring given how many vulnerabilities AI systems have discovered in software lately, and how many people’s communications are protected by Signal’s cryptography. WhatsApp and Google Messages use the same encryption.

Also: How AI security failures are endangering your supply chain

DeepMind used AlphaProof Code, “a novel agentic framework for formal verification at unprecedented depth that combines human expertise with AI agents,” the researchers said. They added that formal verification “is the highest standard of security assurance,” but, without automation at scale like AI systems, difficult and time-consuming.

AI companies like OpenAI have been on a math-breakthrough tear as of late, in some cases solving nearly century-old math problems with AI. By computing at such vast scale, AI models can rapidly accelerate much of the manual work mathematicians do.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, ZDNET’s parent company, filed an April 2025 lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

Using AlphaProof Code and building on earlier verification work by the Signal Foundation, DeepMind researchers divided the verification task into two camps. Humans worked with agents to identify what they needed to prove, and multiple agents running Gemini Carbon, the company’s latest model, then executed it. The agents also audited the proof and “highlighted areas that needed a second look by a human — just 1% of the codebase, a massive time savings,” the researchers wrote.

Also: After the vibe-coding rush comes the debugging hangover

For good measure, researchers tested AlphaProof Code’s ability to distinguish correct from flawed code by showing it two previously fixed Signal bugs, for which it produced correct proofs.

“Our work marks the first time AI has been used to formally verify a widely deployed cryptographic protocol at this depth,” the researchers wrote. “It’s also the first time the implementation of a widely deployed protocol like this has been formally verified by any method, with or without AI.”

Normally, researchers need to manually develop a proof to verify software, which takes much longer. That labor-intensive barrier has made formal verification a distant promised land, until now. The DeepMind team’s verification took a month to do what might normally take experts years.

DeepMind did not verify all of Signal’s code, but noted that this test is a step toward improved security infrastructure at a time when AI is disrupting the entire cybersecurity space.

Also: How to encrypt emails in Gmail, Outlook, and more

The breakthrough reiterates how deeply AI is both a massive risk for cybersecurity and an equally powerful fix. The year 2026 has been especially marked by AI-related security incidents, many of which involve agents. At the same time, companies like Anthropic, Google, and OpenAI are developing models geared specifically toward cybersecurity, including Anthropic’s Claude Mythos.

Also: How to easily encrypt your files on an Android phone

DeepMind did not share any plans to release its findings as a consumer product, though the researchers are open-sourcing their code to help others with formal verification efforts. “Verifying Signal’s core cryptography lays the groundwork to secure other vital systems, from medical device firmware to power grid software,” DeepMind added

#3106. Is KKR a Buy After Its Latest Earnings Report?

The financial company posted an impressive jump in adjusted earnings.

One of the most seasoned and distinctive financial companies on the scene, KKR (KKR +2.81%) won some positive attention from investors early in the week. That's because the company started it off by publishing a quarterly earnings report that easily beat analyst estimates. KKR has always been a prominent company in its niche, and in the wake of earnings, it was a popular one. That doesn't necessarily mean it's a buy, however, so let's try to evaluate its performance on that basis.

While KKR topped consensus estimates -- more on that in a moment -- it actually saw a year-over-year decline in total revenue under generally accepted accounting principles (GAAP) during the third quarter. This fell to $4.56 billion from $5.53 billion in the same frame of 2025. The drop was due to a slide in the business that comprises much of the company's take, insurance; this declined to slightly over $2.8 billion from the year-ago tally of $3.78 billion. KKR's total assets under management (AUM), meanwhile, rose by 11% to $804 billion.

The other arm of the company's operations, asset management and strategic holdings, rose during the period. The increase was modest, however, with only incremental growth to $1.76 billion.

Non-GAAP (adjusted) net income rose despite the revenue slide. It improved by a very sturdy 29% to $1.6 billion, or $1.78 per share. Much of this derived from a 27% jump in fee-related earnings to $1.3 billion.

With the caveat that the financials of asset managers and (to a lesser extent) insurers can be tricky to estimate, KKR's fundamentals were well above the pundit consensus numbers. On average, analysts tracking the company's stock were modeling only $2.69 billion for revenue, and $1.59 per share for adjusted net profit.

Essentially, KKR is a business anchored by steady revenue earners, namely the insurance operations and the fees and related revenue derived from its considerable asset management activities. The company balances this with its far more up-and-down investment activity, as it directly invests in a variety of assets to exit them at a profit for itself and its limited partners. When these exits are successful, the revenue generated can be substantial.

KKR has also been deeply involved in the capital markets for decades, and because it operates in both equity and debt verticals, there's a built-in hedge in this business.

The company is a financial sector powerhouse, and despite the ultimately up-and-down nature of its business, it usually manages to net a relatively high-margin profit. But all in all, it thrives when markets and companies thrive, and the economy may be heading into more challenging times. Since inflation persists, there's scope for fresh Federal Reserve interest rate hikes. Higher rates make the financing that powers KKR's deals more expensive and less available (therefore not as lucrative in the aggregate).

To me, all this means that KKR is a sharp operator, but one whose business model has a higher-than-average level of inherent volatility. I'd flag it as a buy only for investors with a long-term horizon who don't mind an occasionally bumpy ride along the way.

#3105. Google Introduces 3 More Top-Level Domain Names, Including .Eat and .Fly

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Prospective website owners can now register domains geared toward food, travel and local bands.

We’re trained to type .com almost on autopilot, but more top-level domains are coming. You’ll soon see websites ending in .eat, .fly and .here. It’s a new way for restaurants, travel agencies and other service providers to customize their online presence.

Google successfully filed these domain types with ICANN, the Internet Corporation for Assigned Names and Numbers, back in 2012, but only released the new registry options to the public on Tuesday.

There’s always a concern that someone will steal a business’ domain name, a process known as cybersquatting. To prevent new top-level domains from being registered and ransomed at high prices, Google’s partnered registrars offered .eat, .fly and .here websites to registered trademark holders ahead of a wider rollout. This closed access period lasted from Sept. 1 to Nov. 9.

Google’s blog post about the new domains revealed several big businesses that have already secured new web addresses during the trademark holder selloff. Wingstop bought up wingstop.eat, while agriculture and food-tech company Clayton Farms secured the america.eat address. Caddy, Canary and Voltair are some of the businesses with new .fly domains.

The most interesting assortment of companies have tied down .here addresses. In its blog, Google describes .here domains as denoting businesses “where people get things done.” They utilize fairly common words that people might search for. Real-estate app Zillow owns rent.here and sell.here, while bloodtest.here is owned by SiPhox Health, a home blood-testing service.

A number of Google-partnered registrars are now accepting registrations for the three new domain types.

There’s currently a one-time early registration fee associated with registering the new domain names, though it is diminishing and will completely disappear on Nov. 17. After that, any currently unused .eat, .fly and .here names can be registered and maintained at a fixed annual rate, just like any other type of domain.

A representative for Google didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment.

#3104. All your Apple devices need to be patched ASAP – here’s why

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Security updates for iPhones, iPads, Macs, Apple Watches, and Vision Pro just rolled out.

On Tuesday, Apple released security patches for iOS, iPadOS, MacOS, WatchOS, and VisionOS, which address wireless security flaws in Bluetooth pairing, AirPlay, and Messages.

According to Apple’s security notes, vulnerabilities can allow attackers to establish a Bluetooth connection without user interaction, bypass AirPlay connection rate limits, and join an iMessage group chat and receive its messages. Around half of these flaws were found internally by Apple, while the others were submitted by researchers.

You should update your devices now, as several generations are affected across device categories. The following software versions include patches:

Apple’s security notes list a fix for Messages to prevent attackers from remotely joining an iMessage group chat and reading its messages. According to Apple, “an authorization issue was addressed with improved validation” to fix the issue.

There was likely a flaw in Apple’s iMessage authorization logic that allowed an unauthorized user to inject themselves into a group chat roster without permission from an existing member.

Also: Your iPhone can spot scams for you with iOS 27

Another issue in Messages could allow an authorized added member to access messages within a group chat that were sent before they joined. According to Apple, the code that caused this issue has been removed.

One security fix across devices and software versions addresses an issue where a malicious actor could establish a Bluetooth connection to your device without user interaction.

Apple was not specific about which kind of Bluetooth attack this fix addresses; however, it’s reminiscent of Bluetooth Low Energy Re-Pairing vulnerabilities found earlier this year by French researchers.

This study explains how bad actors can exploit vulnerabilities in Bluetooth Low Energy pairing logic to execute one-click or zero-click impersonation and man-in-the-middle attacks against unsuspecting devices, regardless of Bluetooth version or security level.

Also: Beware of Bluetooth sniffers: Your favorite gadgets could expose you

The study was published in January of this year, and the researchers reported initial findings to Apple, which “[appeared] to have hardened the re-pairing logic in MacOS/iOS 26,” according to the study. It’s possible that Apple internally found another vulnerability in the same Bluetooth pairing logic, given how many devices across Bluetooth versions are affected.

According to Apple, this issue was fixed with “improved checks,” likely meaning strengthened verification steps during pairing.

Another vulnerability pertains to AirPlay, allowing an attacker within Bluetooth range or on the same network to bypass the rate limit on AirPlay pairing code attempts. This issue also affects iOS, iPadOS, MacOS, VisionOS, and WatchOS.

This vulnerability is similar to the AirBorne attack method. This report, published in 2025 by Oligo, describes how zero-click remote code execution, user-interaction bypass, denial-of-service, man-in-the-middle, and other techniques can be used to take control of a device that supports AirPlay.

However, the current security issue is likely less serious or far-reaching and was discovered internally.

For this issue, when you pair a device with an AirPlay receiver, such as an Apple TV, AirPlay-enabled TV, or Mac, it will prompt you for a security PIN to verify your device. You can only input an incorrect PIN a certain number of times before you’re denied pairing for a period of time.

Also: The new Apple TV 4K is always listening for ‘Hey Siri’

A vulnerability in this defense mechanism can allow an attacker within Bluetooth range of, or on the same network as, your device to use a brute-force attack, bypassing the mechanism that limits the number of incorrect PIN attempts, and eventually gain access to your device. There are only so many PIN combinations.

According to Apple, “a logic issue was addressed with improved state management” to fix this problem.
