#3102. Half of New Games on Steam Are Now Made With AI. How Does This Reflect the Gaming Industry?

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Most of those games are unpopular, with 10 or fewer reviews from gamers.

Steam, the digital marketplace for PC games from game studio Valve, has hit a major milestone, sparking pushback from parts of the gaming community: Approximately 50% of new Steam games in October were made with the help of AI.

This underscores a growing trend in gaming, where more games are made with some form of AI assistance — specifically, generative AI, which developers have used to generate code, art assets, voice lines and more. Developers are adopting the technology in increasing numbers.

The numbers come from a Reddit user who goes by Ploz, and who used Steam’s publicly available data to see how many newly released games relied on AI in some capacity. This was possible because of Valve’s AI disclosure policy for newly released games, which requires developers to state whether they used AI in making their game. The disclosure is part of the storefront’s publicly accessible records, allowing anyone to view it.

A Valve representative did not respond to a request for comment.

The data is a snapshot of the tidal wave of AI-made games flooding storefronts, most prominently Steam. But crucially, Valve has chosen not to limit this flood, washing its hands of the problem and leaving gamers to fend for themselves in finding quality games. Developers had already been struggling to get their games noticed, and Valve’s limited curation seems completely unprepared for the firehose of AI-made games churned out on an increasing scale.

Valve implemented the AI disclosure policy in January 2024 to increase developer transparency. At the time, only 1.4% of newly released games used AI. This increased to 13.4% by the end of 2024, then to 24% by the end of 2025. It now sits at 49.9%.

There are some caveats. The AI disclosure is only required for games that use AI to create player-facing content, such as artwork, sound, character dialogue and localization. The 49.9% figure is specific to these types of games and doesn’t account for developers who use AI for behind-the-scenes tasks such as coding and bug fixes. A Google Cloud survey conducted in August 2025 showed that 87% of developers use AI for backend work, which doesn’t require disclosure on Steam.

So it’s likely that the number of games on Steam built with AI assistance is greater than 50%. Ploz notes that the only way users can avoid AI-generated games is to manually check for AI disclosures.

According to Ploz, the most common use of AI in game development is for artwork, which was mentioned in 63% of disclosures. Text and translations gobbled up 30%, while music (21%), code (16%) and voices (7%) rounded out the list. Many developers had more than one of those areas named in their disclosure, and about a quarter of developers said they were using AI for their Steam store pages and marketing.

About 3% of developers used AI directly as a game mechanic. One such example is a driving simulator called Teach My Little Sister How to Drive. Developers use AI in the game to interpret a player’s voice commands and generate the sister’s responses. To keep a playable demo of the game available to the public, the development team, Easy Fox, is paying $1,000 every day just to cover these AI operating costs — an expense so high that it was forced to take out a loan simply to keep the demo running.

For development, there are loads of options. The Unity 6 engine came out in October 2024 and includes AI code assistance, AI asset generation and third-party integration with Claude Code, Gemini and Grok. Anthropic’s Claude is a fairly popular option among smaller developers, since it supports the ability to vibe code whole games, with ChatGPT, Gemini and GitHub Copilot as other options.

Many of the games being released with the AI disclosure on Steam can be classified as slop — a derogatory term gamers have applied liberally to titles that heavily lean on generative AI for development and look derivative of other popular games. According to Ploz, most AI-enabled games are small: Around 47% of new games with fewer than 10 reviews have an AI disclosure. Among developers who released more than 10 games in a year, 68% of those games included the disclosure.

Games with AI disclosures received fewer positive reviews on average than those without. And among all newly released games with more than 1,000 reviews, only 17% include an AI disclosure. That means gamers are still gravitating toward games without an AI disclosure and pushing back against games that use the technology.

Developers seem aware of this. CNET reached out to two dozen well-known developers, including Epic Games, EA, Activision Blizzard, Ubisoft, PlayStation Studios and many others. All of them declined to comment.

Bigger studios may be using AI in their development, but it’s hard to tell the scale. There’s no such thing as an AI audit yet, and the industry seems to be relying on voluntary disclosure for now. Steam’s disclosure policy has outed smaller developers who are either worried about violating the policy and being taken off the digital storefront, or aren’t worried about the negative effects of disclosure.

A lot of dominoes had to fall for this to happen. The availability of AI tools and the increasing ease of use are definite contributors. Subreddits like r/AIGameDev have plenty of examples of people putting together games with just a few days or weeks of vibe coding, which is a lot shorter than the standard video game development cycle, which can stretch over years depending on the scale, production level and graphics quality. This practice has exploded over the last couple of years, resulting in a lot of games getting launched that are made mostly or entirely with AI.

Many of these games end up on Steam, which doesn’t have a policy limiting AI-developed games.

“Digital storefronts like Steam have already struggled to moderate low-effort and poor-quality games, as well as copycats stealing other games’ ideas,” said CNET Managing Editor David Lumb.

Valve openly admits its role in this, stating that the problem definitely exists but that the company doesn’t “want to be gatekeepers telling developers which tools they should or shouldn’t use.”

It takes a similar stance on AI cloning, with Valve’s Tom Giardino telling developers that “you must continue to grow your game by adding features, personality and creativity that stem from your own perspective as a developer.”

These policies essentially allow any AI-developed game or clone to reach the Valve storefront without pushback, which certainly contributes to the flood of newly released games with some form of player-facing AI involvement in their development.

Rival store Epic Games has a similar stance, allowing basically any game that wants to be there. The company declined to comment further about this, but CEO Tom Sweeney is an avid supporter of AI in video game development, stating that it was “perfectly logical” for developers to rely on AI to handle the mundane grunt work that takes up most of a developer’s time.

While Steam and Epic Games tend to take a more lax attitude toward AI slop, other storefronts like Itch.io are not. Itch.io has a whole policy against AI slop and the company has willingly, and at times gleefully, deleted terabytes of AI slop from its storefront. It also requires developers to disclose if they used AI but takes a much more consumer-friendly approach by letting users filter those out of search results if they don’t want to see them.

Some measures are being taken to address the proliferation of AI slop, but none will entirely stop the problem. Cloning has been a problem for many years. Mobile gamers of a certain vintage may remember the flock of Flappy Bird clones that appeared on app stores after the original game went down, and those weren’t even made with AI.

AI makes cloning much easier and faster, and some AI companies are addressing it. Anthropic recently changed Claude Opus 5.5 to prevent it from generating copies of existing games. Anthropic engineering leader Addy Osmani posted on X that using Claude to copy games infringes on intellectual property rights and violates Anthropic’s policy.

However, during CNET’s testing, we found we could still make games resembling popular titles, so cloning remains possible with a little creativity.

“This has come up over and over on the subreddit r/GameDev, and there’s no real solution if Steam and other digital storefronts don’t stem the flood of low-quality AI games drowning out the labors of love that entertain and innovate,” Lumb said.

Many developers openly oppose AI and refuse to use it in their own work. However, as the Steam stats show, that crowd’s output appears to be getting smaller and smaller every year amid the unending stream of AI games.

#3101. I found a hidden Android tool that solves all my tiny text troubles

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > Android

When the print on your Android display is too small to read, and the pinch-to-zoom feature isn't working as expected, try this.

How many times have you been on your phone, wanted to read something (a piece of news, a website, etc.), only to find the text is too small and every effort to zoom in doesn’t work?

Also: 7 Android accessibility features that can make your life easier

This happens quite often when I’m viewing content in the Google News app — especially in the morning, when my eyes are still in “wake up” mode. It can be frustrating. And it’s not only text. Sometimes there’s an image I need to enlarge, but for whatever reason, pinch-to-zoom isn’t working on that particular screen.

Fortunately, there’s an option you can enable to ensure you can always zoom in on your Android device, no matter what’s on the screen. The option is found in Accessibility and is aptly called Magnification. With this feature enabled, you can quickly zoom in on the screen to enlarge any displayed content. It doesn’t matter what’s on the screen or what app is showing the image, page, or text — Magnification will magnify it.

This iteration of the feature has been available since Android 15. I found it by accident while poking around the Settings page. Since then, I’ve used it quite a bit, and it’s eased my morning frustration with reading news sites.

The Magnification feature is quite simple: once enabled, a magnifier shortcut appears on the screen. Tap that button, and whatever is on your screen will increase in size. It’s really that easy. One caveat: As long as the Magnification shortcut is enabled, the button remains on your screen. You can move the button up and down or to the left or right side of the display, but if you want the feature enabled, the button must remain.

The good news, however, is that the button does fade a bit, so it’s not glaring and in your face. You can still see it, but it gets just enough transparency that it doesn’t stand out all that much.

Also: Android is getting my favorite iOS tracking feature

Open Settings by dragging the Notification Shade down twice, then tapping the gear icon (or tapping Settings in the App Drawer). Where this is located depends on the Android version you’re using and your device. For example, on my Pixel 9 Pro, it’s in Settings > Accessibility > Magnification. On a RedMagic 12 Pro, it’s in Settings > Features > Accessibility. If you don’t find it right away, open Settings and search for Magnification.

Once you’ve found the Magnification option, tap the On/Off slider until it’s in the On position, and the Magnifier button will appear on your screen. Tap the button, and whatever is on the display will enlarge enough that you shouldn’t have any trouble reading it.

Here’s a little tip: After tapping the Magnification button, whatever’s been enlarged can be scrolled vertically, but not horizontally. Fortunately, there’s a way to ensure that you can read everything on the page.

Also: I use Google Magnifier to read tiny print without my glasses

Tap the gear icon at the bottom right of the display to open the options pop-up. On this page, tap the “Magnifier size” icon that’s second from the right. This enables a movable magnifier you can place where you need it (kind of like a traditional magnifying glass). You can also change the size of the movable magnifier (by tapping one of the other “Magnifier size” buttons) as well as the size of the text when magnified (by dragging the bottom slider).

Also: RedMagic 12 Pro is the fastest Android phone I’ve ever seen

This feature has alleviated the early-morning frustration of having to squint (even with my glasses on) to read certain news sites. Give it a try and see if it doesn’t make your Android life a bit clearer.

#3100. GE Vernova's Orders Jumped 96%. The Number Hiding Underneath Is Electrification's 9% Decline.

GE Vernova's third-quarter 2026 results were strong, but is the 9% decline in electrification orders a problem hiding in plain sight?

GE Vernova's (GEV -3.90%) third-quarter 2026 sales rose 27%, and its earnings rose 193% year over year. It is hard to complain about that performance, but the quarter wasn't all good news. For example, while orders rose 96% across the company, its electrification division saw orders fall by 9%. Don't get too worked up about it; there's a reason, and, in the end, it probably isn't a big deal. Here's why.

GE Vernova makes the turbines that generate electricity. The build-out of AI data centers currently taking place has dramatically increased electricity demand. And, in turn, that has increased the demand for the turbines that GE Vernova makes. At this point, the company's backlog is up to a massive $197 billion. That suggests this industrial giant has plenty of room for growth ahead, even if new order growth slows down.

A slowdown may be on some investors' minds as they look at the 9% drop in orders in the company's electrification division. But some perspective is needed to properly assess that figure. For starters, the big story right now is power, where GE Vernova generated $21.8 billion in orders. Electrification's $5.4 billion and wind's $2.1 billion are less meaningful to the overall story. And while electrification's $5.4 billion was down compared to the prior year, the division is still generating new orders.

But there's still more to the story. The year-over-year drop in orders is due to the third quarter of 2025 benefiting from unusually large orders. In fact, in the third quarter of 2026, the book-to-bill ratio was 1.4x for this division. That means it added more orders than it could fill, which is a sign of strength on the orders front. So, when you look more closely, electrification's 9% decline in orders doesn't seem like a particularly big deal for GE Vernova.

There is a lot going on in the world right now, from the political to the technological. Investors are worried about the future, and any bit of bad news could feel extra troubling. GE Vernova's third quarter was good, with a slight negative hiding underneath it. Don't overreact to the negative. Indeed, few quarters are 100% positive, and the negative was there for a good reason (an unusually strong third quarter for electrification in 2025) and is unlikely to be a lingering or material problem over the long term (highlighted by the division's 1.4x book-to-bill ratio).

#3099. Need more AI in Creator Studio? Apple may charge you for it

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

With its new $50 AI usage packes, Apple is the latest company to introduce paid model for AI access.

There are plenty of AI subscriptions out there, but Apple’s AI features are among the few still accessible by simply purchasing a device. Now, Apple is shifting its business model by offering a one-time $50 purchase option to grant server-based AI capacity in some apps, starting with Creator Studio apps.

Apple is introducing a one-time purchase option for AI Usage Packs via app updates to Creator Studio. If you want expanded access to AI features in Final Cut Pro, Pixelmator Pro, the iWork Suite, and Logic Pro, you’ll need to pay.

Also: ChatGPT Plus vs. Google AI Pro: Two $20 plans, very different perks

The $50 fee applies to extra usage blocks and kicks in when you exceed predetermined limits in these apps. Instead of a recurring fee, Apple is hoping the one-time fee for intelligence usage packs will help the company offset some of the cost of AI. Users can buy a pack for Logic Pro now, and the other apps are expected to get it soon.

“Usage Packs may offer the option to prepay for the use of certain AI-based Services and represent the amount you have paid in advance for use of the respective Services,” according to Apple.

Apple’s one-time payment breaks away from what most companies are doing. Most companies offering AI tools try to offset the steep cost of running large language models (LLMs) by charging users for paid subscriptions. For example, Samsung is rumored to be planning such a subscription for Galaxy AI usage, including access to features that are currently free.

Also: The best tablets of 2026: Lab-tested recommendations

The Creator Studio apps are the only ones getting the Usage Packs for now. The legal terms for intelligence Usage Packs explain that these packs would be available in “AI apps,” which include not only Final Cut Pro, Compressor, Logic Pro, MainStage, Pixelmator Pro, Motion, but also Pages, Keynote, Numbers, Freeform, and other apps.

You’re not required to purchase the Usage Packs to enjoy the core functionality of the apps in the Creator Studio. However, the fact that the Usage Packs are to be applied to popular apps like Pages and Freeform begs the question of whether Apple plans to put a paywall on features that are currently free.

Some of the features for which you may be required to purchase Usage Packs once you exceed your limits include the generation of images, shapes, presentations, and presenter notes, as well as some assistant features. Usage limits reset each month.

Also: Alexa Tablet 12 Pro review: Forget everything you knew about Amazon tablets

You can view your usage status for each of the applicable apps on your iPhone or iPad by tapping on More (or the button with three dots), then Intelligence Features, and Show Usage Status. On a Mac, it’s available by accessing Intelligence Features from the menu bar of the app you’re using, then clicking on Show Usage Status.

Intelligence usage packs expire after 12 months, but they roll over each month if left unused.

#3098. We’ve Folded the iPhone Duo Almost 400,000 Times. How Much More Can It Take?

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Our Fold-a-Phone machine folds Apple's iPhone Duo 900 times every hour to test the durability of its hinge.

We’re now a third of the way through November, and the machine in CNET’s lab continues to work overtime testing Apple’s first folding phone, the $1,999 iPhone Duo. We purchased this poor phone more than two weeks ago, and since then its only job has been to bend and flatten, bend and flatten, until it breaks.

So how many times can we fold the iPhone Duo? As of 3 p.m. on Tuesday, Nov. 10, we’re at 390,000 folds, and by tomorrow we’ll reach 400,000 folds.

I oversee hardware and software testing as director of lab operations in our lab in Louisville, Kentucky. Over the last week, I’ve seen a lot of chatter about the sturdiness of the Duo’s folding hinge and its potential to fail or crack. I’ve periodically examined the Duo we’re testing in our machine, and so far, I haven’t seen any signs of damage. And the crease isn’t any more visible than it was when we took it out of the box. If that changes, I’ll let you know here and on our livestream.

In the meantime, let me explain what we’re doing. My team and I designed and built a proprietary machine to test foldable phones like Apple’s and its Samsung rival, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 (which we’ve also started testing). I’ve worked on the methodology and build of this testing rig for months in anticipation of Apple’s long-rumored foldable iPhone.

We even named our machine to match its purpose: Meet the Fold-a-Phone.

Now, my team of engineers and testing experts will watch the iPhone Duo open and close, open and close, to rigorously and systematically monitor the durability of Apple’s hinge and two screens.

After all, repeatedly folding a phone with bare hands can only get you so far in understanding the complex forces at play between the magnetics that snap the screen shut and the all-critical hinge.

CNET’s Senior Technology Reporter, Abrar Al-Heeti, has done a beautiful job capturing the iPhone Duo’s pros and cons in her smart, thorough review. Our Fold-a-Phone machine will add a deep data foundation to complement her real-world experience.

Since Apple hasn’t publicly shared claims of the iPhone Duo’s foldable lifespan, CNET’s Fold-a-Phone machine is poised to provide a wealth of valuable data about Apple’s first foldable phone and competing models in the foldable market.

And yes, you’d better believe we will use our treasure trove of in-house data to share status updates and comparisons during our ongoing testing window with the Duo.

Join CNET’s iPhone Duo livestream on YouTube weekdays from 1 p.m. to 5 p.m. ET, where you can follow along live and watch the Fold-a-Phone do its thing to a Duo.

The importance of this moment is hard to overstate. Apple’s appearance on the foldable smartfold frontier lags Samsung by seven years (the first Galaxy Fold went on sale in the US in September 2019). Motorola and other phone-makers have long experimented with foldable phones in every shape and size, including a trifold accordion foldable phone we first demoed in 2019.

CNET’s mobile experts, including Al-Heeti and Editor at Large Andrew Lanxon, have discussed the legitimizing effect of the iPhone Duo’s existence on the entire foldable phone landscape, which has previously felt like a new-wave fantasy rather than a mainstream riptide.

In many ways, Apple’s newfound competition with existing foldable phones completes the portfolio. That is precisely why we’re building the Fold-a-Phone now, and why our proprietary machine, methodology and test data will enrich our collective understanding of the capabilities – and yes, limitations – of foldable phones as our true next-generation mobile device.

Most current foldable phones are rated for 200,000 to 500,000 folds. As such, we knew we needed to create a robust and adaptable machine that could accommodate book- and flip-style foldable phones of any size, dimension and hinge orientation. (Note: The Fold-a-Phone was not built for trifold phones, which present a unique element to the engineering challenge.)

Moreover, we wanted to test the phone the way people would actually use it. No one just sits around opening and closing their foldable phone at top speed all day. Rather, you open it naturally, spend a few minutes watching a video, browsing a web page, or using an app, close it, and then put it in your pocket. This may happen dozens of times per day.

Mimicking this usage on the Fold-a-Phone limits undue stress on the phone hinge and avoids generating excess heat compared to a test built for speed.

We determined that a cadence of roughly 4 seconds could sidestep technical hurdles while also being reasonably fast. The foldable phone takes 1 second to open, 1 second to fully open, 1 second to close and 1 second to fully close. This cycle counts as a single completed fold. Then, the cycle repeats until either the hinge or the screen gives out.

In conceptualizing and building the Fold-a-Phone, our team has used standard, off-the-shelf components to simplify the machine’s development. The essential design consists of two armatures, distinctly designed to perform the opening and closing movements with both force and grace.

An industrial-grade stepper motor drives each armature, with the host software (running on a connected computer) deterministically adjusting the torque to complete each step of the process while also protecting the phone. If any of the several proximity sensors fail to detect the armature in the correct position, the machine stops immediately.

We also monitor the motors’ power usage to track how well the foldable phone opens and closes over time. We have set up cameras to capture images and video of the test from multiple angles, and we will analyze regular status updates from the computer to determine the current status of the phone and the test.

To ensure we never miss a major development, the entire rig is connected to an EcoFlow Delta 3 Plus power station to guard against electricity outages, and all the output data is saved to multiple locations.

To secure each phone to the machine, we use our CNET Lab Test-winning 3D printer, the Prusa Core One, to 3D-print a custom “sled” that matches each phone’s exact dimensions. We secure the phone to the sled with heavy-duty double-sided tape (yes, it works!) and then screw the sled onto the machine.

There’s always room for improvement. We’re constantly looking for ways to make the machine run more smoothly and quietly without disrupting the folding rhythm.

Apple’s presence is already changing the game and could very well spur innovation in ways we can’t predict today. But until then, our Fold-a-Phone machine will continue to test the iPhone Duo and its competitors, and CNET’s writers will report on how well these individual hinged devices stand up to the challenge – or fold under pressure.

#3097. New Windows 11 update fixes File Explorer menu mess – and patches an exploited flaw

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Windows > Windows 11

With the November Patch Tuesday rollout, you can finally customize your right-click menu in Windows 11. It fixes 1,094 security flaws, too.

Windows 11 users who’ve struggled to navigate and use the right-click menu in File Explorer now finally have some relief. With the November Patch Tuesday Windows update, you’re now able to customize the menu to add and remove specific commands and apps so that only your favorites appear.

As usual, the monthly update is mandatory, which means it should automatically download and install on your PC. To check, head to Settings and select Windows Update. If the latest update — labeled “2026-11 Security Update (KB5132061) (26300.9974)” -– pops up as installed with a pending restart, just reboot your computer. Otherwise, click the button to check for updates and allow it to download and install.

Also: You can change Windows settings directly from a search now

One caveat: Microsoft’s monthly updates fully support Windows 11 26H2 and 25H2. But support for 24H2 ended last month, at least for the Home and Pro editions. Windows 11 24H2 Enterprise and Education will continue to get monthly security updates, though not regular non-security preview updates, while Windows 11 23H2 Enterprise and Education will receive no more updates after this month.

If you use Windows 11 Home or Pro, be sure you upgrade your system to 26H2 to continue receiving the necessary support and updates.

First and foremost in the latest update is the new customizable right-click menu.

The context-sensitive right-click menu is a handy way to quickly access key settings, features, and apps, both in File Explorer and on the desktop. But there’s always been a flaw in its basic design. The more apps you install, the more cluttered and disorganized the menu becomes. That was especially problematic in previous versions of Windows. The old-style, classic menu would grow so long that it would be difficult to navigate and let you find the item you wanted.

With Windows 11, Microsoft introduced a more modern and pared-down menu. But this version can also get crowded. Plus, it may not display every command you need, so you often have to revert to the older classic menu. You’re then stuck dealing with two separate versions of the same menu, leading to an even more confusing and cumbersome experience.

Also: Windows 11 can run better on PCs with 8GB now, Microsoft says

The ideal solution is a menu that you can customize yourself, and that’s what we now have, at least that’s what is now rolling out. Microsoft calls this one a gradual rollout, which means you may or may not get it right away.

To see if and how this is available, open File Explorer and right-click on a folder or file. If the new menu has reached your PC, you’ll see an entry for Customize menu. Select that to view all the available options for tweaking it. Alternatively, head to Settings, select Personalization, and then click the entry for Context menu.

There, you’re able to turn certain file actions on or off, such as Send to, Print, Create shortcut, Copy as path, and Rotate image. You can also choose which apps appear all the time, either in the main menu or in a submenu. Plus, additional options let you decide where certain commands and actions appear and whether you can still access the old-style menu.

Though the customizable right-click menu is the star of the update, Microsoft has added other surprises as it strives to make Windows more reliable and user-friendly.

Also gradually rolling out, Cloud rebuild is a new recovery option that lets you reinstall Windows 11 via cloud-based system images and drivers downloaded from Windows Update. This tool can help you recover Windows when it won’t load and you don’t have a USB drive or your own custom image. The downside is that your files won’t survive. But if you regularly back them up as you should, you can restore them after Windows is back in business.

To start Cloud rebuild, go to Settings, select System, click Recovery, and then select Rebuild PC. Your PC will then restart into the Windows Recovery Environment (WinRE) to kick off the cloud rebuild. If Windows can load, your computer should automatically enter WinRE mode, where you can then choose the cloud rebuild. You should need to make sure your PC is online via a wired or wireless connection.

The November update also enhances or fixes other Windows features and apps.

And that then brings us to the security fixes.

This month’s update resolves 1,094 security vulnerabilities, according to Microsoft, with 63 of them rated critical and one already exploited in the wild. That’s fewer than October’s record of 1,400 patches. But it still represents a hefty number as Microsoft and other vendors continue to rely on AI to find and fix security holes.

Also: Windows finally gets a built-in internet speed test tool – how to try it

Though labeled Moderate, the exploited flaw points to a serious spoofing vulnerability in Microsoft Outlook. Here, a remote attacker who sends you a malicious email and convinces you to open it could cause Outlook to connect to a network controlled by that attacker. In this case, though, you would not receive any warning. Further, the exploit might reveal your username and password to the sender, potentially allowing them to impersonate you.

“An attacker sends a specially crafted message file and gets the user to open it,” Jack Bicer, Director of Vulnerability Research at patch management provider Action1, said in an advisory.

“Outlook then connects to a network location the attacker controls, but it skips the usual warning about external content,” Bicer explained further. “That quiet connection can leak the user’s NTLMv2 hash, a scrambled form of their login secret, which the attacker can use to sign in as that person. It affects Microsoft 365 Apps for Enterprise and Office LTSC 2021 and 2024. Attackers are already exploiting it, it needs only one click on an email file, and it hits Outlook, which nearly every organization runs.”

#3096. Top Wall Street analysts back these 3 stocks for their long-term growth potential

Major indexes remain volatile as investors assess key earnings releases and geopolitical tensions in the Middle East. Investors with a long-term horizon should look beyond the ongoing noise and focus on stocks that are well-positioned to generate strong returns.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist stocks with robust growth opportunities. These experts conduct an in-depth analysis of all the macro and micro factors affecting a company, focusing on its long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

CrowdStrike Holdings is a cloud-native cybersecurity provider. The company recently announced an expanded partnership with Schwarz Digits to bring its Falcon platform to European enterprises.

After hosting several European investor meetings with the CrowdStrike CFO, Stifel analyst Adam Borg reiterated a buy rating on CRWD stock and increased his price target to $230 from $220.

The five-star analyst said that the meetings increased his confidence about CrowdStrike being an "important, well-positioned cybersecurity platform vendor that is an AI beneficiary given its expansive portfolio, unique data set, and ability to both secure AI and use AI to improve security."

Among the key takeaways, Borg noted that AI is driving a major shift in cybersecurity demand as it presents software vulnerabilities that can be exploited by less sophisticated hackers with capabilities that were once limited to government-backed hacking groups. This has led to companies making cybersecurity a top priority, driving customer interest in CrowdStrike's new AI Detection & Response solution and its broader security platform.

Borg added that AI-driven demand is expanding CrowdStrike's pipeline and supporting confidence in the company's higher fiscal 2027 net new annual recurring revenue guidance issued last quarter. Overall, Borg believes that CrowdStrike has multiple growth drivers that can help maintain revenue growth in at least the high-teens and drive improved profitability in the years ahead.

Borg ranks No. 651 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See CrowdStrike Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving on to AST SpaceMobile, a company building a global cellular broadband network in space. Recently, Piper Sandler analyst Alexander Potter initiated coverage of space-related stocks. While the analyst assigned AST SpaceMobile a buy rating with a price target of $100, he has a neutral stance on rocket builders SpaceX and Rocket Lab due to valuation concerns.

"We prefer ASTS, due to a more palatable valuation, and a clearer path to EBITDA upside," said Potter.

The five-star analyst highlighted that AST SpaceMobile builds satellites that connect directly to smartphones, ensuring network connectivity for users everywhere and enabling applications like video calls, streaming, and gaming. Potter added that ASTS' technology helps Mobile Network Operators, or MNOs, expand coverage and offer add-on services to travelers and users in remote locations.

Potter ranks No. 708 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 48% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.2%. See AST SpaceMobile Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

Finally, let's look at Broadcom, a provider of custom AI chips and networking solutions. Recently, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on Broadcom stock with a price target of $502.

The five-star analyst is surprised by the underperformance of AVGO stock so far this year compared to its peers, especially given continued momentum in AI-driven demand. Moore cited concerns that competitor MediaTek could gain market share from Broadcom in Google's tensor processing unit chip business and investor preference for faster-growing AI chip names as reasons for the underperformance.

While Moore acknowledged that MediaTek's role in Google's TPU business is growing, he contends that it is unlikely to disrupt Broadcom's position significantly. He expects Broadcom to continue to be the major TPU supplier, with about 80% market share. Overall, Moore believes that concerns about a decline in AVGO's market share to 50% or its eventual replacement seem premature.

"AVGO should retain majority share, sustain strong AI growth, and remain a core compute winner, close behind NVDA," said Moore.

The analyst explained that his bullish outlook on Broadcom is based on the company's solid advantage in high-bandwidth memory supply, chip packaging capabilities, and large-scale production. Moore also highlighted AVGO's dominance in AI ASIC chips, strong networking business, and new customer wins.

Moore ranks No. 148 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 25.5%. See Broadcom Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

#3095. Swarmer: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Swarmer's shift to selling complete defense systems directly to governments rests on combat-tested technology already used in Ukraine.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, Swarmer (SWMR -2.23%) announced a pivot from selling business-to-business (B2B) software to corporate drone makers to manufacturing end-to-end defense hardware directly for government buyers. Controlling both physical hardware and autonomous software enables the business to capture higher contract values across ground vehicles, quadcopters, and air defense systems. Three things management said stand out.

Owning both physical defense hardware and autonomous software lets the company sell complete combat kits directly to government agencies. This reduces reliance on third-party drone manufacturers to bring its technology to market.

Acquiring Ratel Robotics brings unmanned ground vehicles (UGVs), which are land-based robotic combat vehicles, and an existing $86 million government contract backlog into the business. Ratel generated $32.5 million in revenue during the third quarter of 2026 alone.

A memorandum of understanding (MOU), which is a preliminary commercial agreement, with Vectus Air Defense outlines substantial purchase intent for complete air defense systems. Each system carries an estimated manufacturer's suggested retail price (MSRP) between $5 million and $6 million.

Manufacturing capacity is expanding each quarter to fulfill heavy government demand, positioning the company to transition from small-scale software support toward large-scale hardware delivery.

Field-tested technology, integrated hardware-software architecture, and direct government relationships provide structural defense advantages as leadership monitors production expansion and contract fulfillment.

#3092. Gaining Financial Control Requires More Than Just a High Income

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Income is one factor to feeling financially in control, but your approach to managing your money is crucial, too.

Earning more money can boost your feeling of financial control.

Nearly 9 in 10 (89%) Americans with a household income of $100k-plus say they feel in control of their day-to-day finances compared with 62% of Americans with a household income of less than $50k, according to NerdWallet’s October 2026 Financial Resilience Index survey conducted by The Harris Poll.

But getting a raise or a higher-paying job isn’t a surefire way to feel in control. Pairing your paycheck with good financial habits is a more robust approach.

Nearly 4 in 5 (78%) Americans feel in control of their day-to-day finances, according to the survey. That’s up from 73% in September.

There’s no single external measure for what makes someone feel in control of their finances. If you swap financial positions with someone else who feels in control, for example, there’s no guarantee you would feel the same way.

That said, a person who lives comfortably with what they have and who has the ability to withstand unexpected expenses has a good chance of feeling in control.

Americans with lower household incomes are much more likely to feel a lack of control in their day-to-day finances compared with those with higher household incomes.

That may seem intuitive. Someone with a high income is more likely to be able to cut back on luxuries, such as dining or vacations, when the costs of groceries and healthcare rise. Someone whose income is lower might not have expenses to cut.

However, that narrative is an overgeneralization.

If feeling in control of your finances depended entirely on having a high income, you might expect to see a much higher share of Americans with low incomes who feel they aren’t in control of their day-to-day finances.

But in fact, the majority (62%) of Americans with a household income of less than $50k do feel in control of their day-to-day finances. At the other end of the income spectrum, it’s clear that money doesn’t automatically result in feeling in control: About 1 in 10 Americans (11%) with a household income of $100k+ say they don’t feel this.

A high income alone won’t offset spending more than you earn, and it isn’t a substitute for an emergency fund. Good financial habits are an important component of financial control, too. They may look like:

Preparing for an emergency: Car repairs, medical bills, broken appliances: There are plenty of expensive emergencies that can pop up, even if you’re doing everything right. One-third (33%) of Americans don’t have enough cash on hand to cover an unexpected $1,000 expense if it popped up this month. Those who have an emergency fund can avoid relying on credit.

Managing debt: Monthly expenses that exceed income indicate a shaky financial footing — not great for developing a feeling of control. Nearly 2 in 5 Americans (38%) say they’ll have to rely on credit to manage expenses in October. Managing debt goes beyond paying your credit card bill each month. For example, if you get the maximum car loan or mortgage that lenders approve, a large portion of your paycheck is committed to debt payments. Keeping your overall debt load low gives you flexibility if your situation changes in the future.

Avoiding expensive mistakes: Saving money takes time. Spending it can happen quickly. A separate recent NerdWallet study found that 3 in 5 Americans (60%) say they’ve spent money on something expensive and later regretted it. Luxury items, gambling and vehicles topped the list of regrettable purchases. Pump the brakes on avoidable expensive purchases. For any big buy, take a day or two to mull over your options, especially if you have outstanding debt or if your emergency fund is low. Considering spending decisions carefully is another way to feel more in control.

The feeling of control is likely to emerge over time. Build momentum by making progress on one tangible financial goal — saving money or paying down debt, for example — before adding another.

Yes, Americans across a range of incomes report feeling in control financially. But it would be glib to suggest that this demonstrates that the feeling of financial control can be flicked on like a light switch, if only people tried harder.

There are plenty of reasons why the road to feeling in control can be arduous. Family obligations, medical concerns, affordability issues and limited opportunities can all present real obstacles, even for those who strive to practice sound financial habits along the way.

If you’re struggling and need assistance, start with these resources:

Call 211 or visit 211.org. They’ll help connect you to the relevant resources in your area.

#3091. Apple’s iPod Turns 25: Here’s the Song Catalog That Rode Wit You

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

On this day in 2001, Apple's iconic MP3 player was released. And now, an Apple Music easter egg lets you experience the nostalgic sound and scroll of the gadget's click wheel.

Back in 2001, some of the top songs included Nelly’s Ride Wit Me, Fallin’ by Alicia Keys and Ms. Jackson by Outkast. When people wanted to listen to those songs but weren’t near a car stereo or their home audio equipment, most carried music with them on skip-prone portable CD players, boomboxes or maybe even a homemade mix cassette.

Sure, some early adopters were already carrying a small collection of songs on a portable MP3 player (MP3s: the new-new thing!) like the Diamond Rio or Creative’s Nomad Jukebox. But these weren’t common, and you didn’t see them that often. There was no ubiquitous sign yet that the times were changing musically in a digital direction.

Enter Apple in November 2001 with its original iPod, a small hard drive encased in smooth white plastic that could stash between 1,000 and 2,000 songs, a sum that seemed godly at the time.

Who would ever need to listen to 1,000 songs (or have access to that many) at their fingertips? What mortal would dare?

The iPod, which was announced on Oct. 23, 2001, and released on Nov. 10, 2001, had a starting price of $400. It heralded the smartphone era before smartphones were a thing, making portable electronics more tactile, user-friendly and information-forward. The white earbuds that were bundled with the device were the beacon of that era. Before long, it seemed everyone had white cables coming out of their ears.

For the 25th anniversary of Apple’s game-changing music player, the device’s creators are getting nostalgic about its silver anniversary. On Apple Music’s website, you can find at its 404 Not Found page a playful mockup of the OG iPod with its pleasing-to-use scroll wheel and a selection of songs you might have had in your iPod at some time or another.

CNET writers and editors had a good time playing around with the fully functional iPod online, sharing favorite hits from its music library and enjoying some nostalgia ASMR.

We also looked back at CNET’s earlier write-ups, including one from CNET’s former Editor-in-Chief Connie Guglielmo, explaining what it was like to watch Steve Jobs introduce the iPod. There’s also a great 2021 piece by CNET’s Scott Stein on the iPod’s evolution, and a commentary by CNET’s Ty Pendlebury on how the iPod captured and held the throne for so long, surviving music streaming and rival players.

Read more: iPhone Duo Review: Late to the Foldable Party, but It Steals the Show

If the iPod can be primarily credited to one person — leading a team of many — it’s widely acknowledged to be Tony Fadell, who came to Apple as a contractor and went on to head one of its most successful product divisions. These days, Fadell is thinking a lot about the future of AI assistant devices, but he took time on his LinkedIn to acknowledge the iPod anniversary this week.

“We took it from approval to shipping in less than eight months,” he wrote. “There wasn’t time for endless refinements. We had to make decisions, trust our experience and stay focused on one clear promise: 1,000 songs in your pocket.”

While Fadell says that the iPod took a few generations to really take off and eventually come in iterations like the Nano and the Touch, some music fans who still love the original iPod design have been using the device for decades. If you have any hard drive-based gadget that lasts that long, count yourself lucky.

In addition to those old enough to remember what a Hoobastank is, iPods are also getting popular again with younger generations on a nostalgia kick, many of whom would rather carry around a separate physical device for their music than stream their Spotify accounts on their phone for the millionth time. Axios reports that iPods and MP3s are back in a big way in a trend called “friction-maxxing.” You don’t have to use that term to indulge, we promise.

Getting into the hobby isn’t cheap: Working iPod models can cost several hundred dollars on eBay. And one that’s never been opened? Try $25,000 to $60,000.

A cheaper route may be to keep the familiar outside trappings but replace the guts with a new motherboard. A group called Crowd Supply promises in a YouTube video a new way to bring nonworking iPods back to life. It hasn’t launched yet, but you can keep an eye on the project at Crowd Supply’s website. Other tinkerers have had years and years to work up hundreds of iPod hacks; you can find many of them on Hackaday.

Alternatively, it may be the function that a modern MP3 enthusiast seeks, not necessarily the Apple branding or hardware. Other companies, such as Fosi Audio, have their own takes on the modern music player, like the $80 DAP Neo. CNET has a list of the best MP3 players of 2026, with devices like the Shanling M0s and the Android-based HiBy R4 coming out on top.

Lastly, if you want to relive the iPod glory days, you don’t necessarily need new (or old) hardware at all; your phone can do it with iOS apps such as RetroPod, My Classic – Retro Console or, if you’re feeling saucy on Android, ClassiPod.

#3090. Is Nvidia a Bad-News Buy or a Stock to Avoid?

Nvidia has been a key winner in the AI boom.

Nvidia (NVDA -5.47%) stock has been one of the major winners of the artificial intelligence (AI) revolution. This is because the tech giant has built an AI empire, offering the world's fastest AI chips, known as graphics processing units (GPUs), and a full portfolio of supporting products and services. AI customers have snapped up Nvidia's chips, with demand often exceeding supply as the company launches new updates.

All of this has fueled mind-boggling earnings growth, with revenue and profit rising in the double and triple digits. Investors recognized this early in the AI boom, and this helped the stock soar 1,100% from the start of 2023 through the end of last year. This year has been a rockier one for Nvidia and other AI powerhouses as investors turned more cautious on the space. They worried about higher inflation in the U.S. and questioned whether data center spending would continue at current levels.

And just this week, particularly bad news concerning Nvidia emerged. Nvidia opened an investigation into chief Jensen Huang's relationship with a person U.S. intelligence agencies are calling a Chinese spy. Against this backdrop, is Nvidia a bad-news buy or a stock to avoid? Let's find out.

First, let's take a step back and consider Huang's role at Nvidia. He founded the company more than 30 years ago, and over time, he has been at the helm, guiding its direction. Huang made a major bet on AI about a decade ago, deciding to shift Nvidia's GPU focus to serving that market. Clearly, it was a winning decision. And since that time, he has become the face of AI development, speaking around the globe about the technology today and its future potential.

Huang also has lobbied to bring Nvidia's chips back to China after a U.S. export ban. He won that battle, with the U.S. permitting the export of certain chips, but China hasn't yet fully accepted imports. Nvidia has only exported a small number of chips there this year. But this situation brought the U.S./China AI race to the forefront.

All of this demonstrates something big: Huang is the driving force at Nvidia, a leader whose name is known around the world and well beyond tech circles. That suggests trouble for him could equal trouble for the company.

Now, let's consider the latest news. Nvidia on Nov. 8 said it is launching "an independent investigation in light of recent reporting that founder and CEO Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort."

Huang, in an email to staff obtained by CNBC, said that he is "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security."

The company also said in a statement to CNBC that Huang is committed to leading Nvidia "without interruption."

Nvidia's stock declined 4.2% in the following trading session, leaving the stock trading at 19x forward earnings estimates -- a level that's dirt cheap considering the company's track record of growth throughout the AI boom and prospects down the road.

Still, the current investigation introduces an element of uncertainty. So you may be wondering if you should pick up shares of Nvidia after this recent bad news or if you should avoid the stock.

The major risk the company faces is the possibility that Huang could step down or be forced out. Since he is a visionary who has driven the company for decades, and Nvidia hasn't set out a succession plan, investors could frown on such a scenario -- and that could weigh on stock performance. Another risk is that lawmakers might favor more limits regarding chip exports to China or increased regulation, moves that may weigh on future growth.

The decision to buy or avoid Nvidia right now depends on your comfort with risk. If you're a cautious investor, you might favor waiting for the results of the investigation before getting in on the stock. Though Huang intends to remain at the helm and the board has expressed confidence in him, uncertainty will linger until the investigation is complete. And this could hurt the stock in the near term. Any possible departure by Huang would also be bad news.

That said, the current turmoil doesn't change the demand present in the AI market or the quality of Nvidia's chips and innovation. Aggressive investors with a well-diversified portfolio might consider picking up a few shares of Nvidia at today's dirt cheap levels and holding on as the storm passes.

#3088. The New Adobe Firefly AI Is All About Video Editing

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Adobe's AI platform is doing all the things now, not just beginners' basics.

Adobe has been on a journey for years to turn itself into an AI behemoth, and its latest round of Firefly AI updates brings its dreams one step closer to fruition. At Adobe Max on Tuesday, the company announced a slew of Firefly AI updates focused on building out its video-editing capabilities.

Firefly is getting an upgraded video editor. Like other creative AI companies, Adobe has been building up its AI video capabilities over the past year, with video models made both in-house and by third-party AI companies. Now, the new interface incorporates the Firefly AI assistant, which is in beta, in a bigger way. There’s a dedicated ideation panel and more ways to storyboard ideas.

It’s a more feature-packed alternative to beginner or everyday video editors like CapCut, without overloading creators with all the features of professional-grade Adobe Premiere. Part of that push is integrating agentic AI, or agents that can handle editing tasks for you autonomously. And there are a lot of AI agents coming to Adobe’s programs.

That beginner-to-intermediate design is, well, by design — Adobe is aiming to lure in folks without decades of video-editing know-how with its generative AI features. It fits with Adobe’s larger goals; it wants to build Firefly for video and image editors the same way it built Photoshop — to be the “career partner” for creators.

Another new project is Firefly Design, an AI-heavy version of a graphic design program that incorporates print, motion graphics and 3D. The intelligence assistant is a big part of the interface, where you can use text prompts to come up with variations of different scenes.

There are a few new Firefly AI image tools, and their names describe their uses well: relight, retouch and edit object. These are more precise controls, keeping with Adobe’s goal of giving AI enthusiasts more ability to edit their video content. There are also new quick actions, or shortcuts, including a video thumbnail generator that’s based on your video.

And if you remember Project Graph from last year’s Adobe Max, it’s back in a public preview as Firefly Graph. This is a node-based editing system, where you can create individual “nodes” and connect them to create an entire system, or ecosystem, of content. It won’t be for everyone, but it’s aimed at creative teams who want to visualize large workflows.

#3087. Greg Abel's Berkshire Hathaway Owns 37% of This Company. Here's Why the Stock Is Also a Buy for Retail Investors.

Sirius XM has performed well in 2026, and it has a deal in place that could generate even more meaningful revenue in the years ahead.

Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA +2.60%) (BRKB +1.41%) CEO Greg Abel's tenure is being defined by aggressive moves, with one of the most recent being the addition of more shares of Sirius XM Holdings (SIRI -1.32%) to the company's portfolio.

As revealed in a Form 4 filing, from Oct. 30 to Nov. 3, Berkshire purchased approximately $27 million worth of Sirius XM shares. By Berkshire's standards, that may sound small, with Sirius XM's weight in its portfolio at only 1.1%.

That said, the bigger vote of confidence from Abel's company is that it now owns 37.4% of the satellite radio provider. And as we head into 2027, investors may also want to consider Berkshire's moves and add the stock, or more of it, to their own portfolios.

Berkshire Hathaway CEO Greg Abel. Image source: Getty Images.

Over the last five years, Sirius XM has faced increased competition, a declining customer base, rising content costs, and a difficult market for acquiring new customers.

Heading into 2027 and beyond, while the stock price might not climb more than 60% each year, there's a reason Sirius XM could continue to be a long-term winner.

In April, Sirius XM struck a deal with Alphabet to become the exclusive advertising representative of YouTube's audio ad inventory in the United States. While YouTube was essentially just a platform to upload videos in its early days, it's become a more comprehensive destination for entertainment, ranging from live sports to podcasts to music. And it's a huge market for advertisers to reach. "According to a study from SiriusXM Media and Edison Research, there are more than 212 million monthly listeners in the U.S. engaging in audio-first content or environments on YouTube," Sirius said in a press release.

This deal won't be massively transformative right away, but by 2029, Deutsche Bank analysts estimate the partnership could generate $2 billion in incremental annual revenue for Sirius. For a company that generated $8.5 billion in 2025, that's meaningful.

If Sirius XM can set up other deals like this, it could ultimately become a profitable toll collector in the audio advertising space rather than competing in the crowded audio entertainment sector.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates went up today, but they're still in a much better place than this time last month.

Mortgage rates are higher today, but only by a little. And they're still hovering below 7%, which is much better than what we saw around this time last month.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked up to 6.98% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is two basis points higher than Friday and five basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Mortgage rates have more room to rise, as a stronger-than-expected jobs report reinforced expectations for a Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

As for what’s next: Tomorrow’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially nudge mortgage rates in either direction. To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the video.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds are watching the October Consumer Price Index, which comes out on Tuesday. CPI is one of two key measures of inflation, which the Federal Reserve aims to keep in check relative to the strength of the labor market. (The Fed looks more closely at a different inflation measure, the Personal Consumption Expenditures Index, which comes out Nov. 25.)

Right now, the labor market is looking pretty darn strong: The October jobs report, released Friday from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, showed U.S. employers added 165,000 jobs in October, far more than economists expected, and unemployment edged down to 4.1%.

Raising the federal funds rate is the Fed’s main tool for bringing inflation down, but rate hikes are only on the table when the labor market is strong enough to handle it. Considering Friday’s jobs data, it’s looking fairly likely that the Fed will approve a rate hike when the committee meets Dec. 8-9. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but an anticipated rate hike can put upward pressure on mortgage rates, which respond to investors’ expectations for the economy and Fed policy.

Still, a jump in mortgage rates isn’t a sure thing, and market sentiment could shift over the next month. October’s inflation data will capture the effect of the Fed’s last rate hike, which came in the middle of September. If that worked as intended and inflation shows signs of slowing, mortgage rates might remain around 7%.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.48% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3085. NASA Is Accepting Astronaut Applications for the First Time in 2 Years

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

If you have the right stuff, you could be on the next moon mission or end up on Mars.

This type of job posting happens once in a blue moon.  If you’ve always dreamed of becoming an astronaut, NASA just made space for those who meet its qualifications. On Saturday, the agency announced that it’s seeking the next class of astronauts who will journey to the moon, build the planned 2032 Moon Base and help create the systems that will enable the first Mars mission.

NASA last accepted applications in 2024. Over 8,000 people applied, but only 10 astronaut candidates were chosen. Since the 1960s, 370 people have been selected to train as astronaut candidates, and just 32 are currently in NASA’s astronaut corps.

If you want to journey into space with NASA, you have to be a US citizen, ideally with a master’s degree in a STEM field.

In addition to completing an application package that includes a long-form resume and five references, you will also have to pass NASA’s long-duration astronaut flight physical and show strong skills in communication, teamwork and leadership.

If you don’t have a master’s degree, you can meet that requirement with at least two years of work toward a doctoral program in a STEM field, completion of a medical degree or completion or enrollment in a nationally recognized Test Pilot School program.

You also must have at least three years of related professional experience. If Test Pilot School is your only advanced technical degree, you must also have a bachelor’s degree in a STEM field.

If you’re in residency training as a medical doctor, that could count toward the professional experience requirement, but training would have to be complete by July 2027.

If you’re a pilot, you could meet the professional experience requirement with 750 hours of pilot-in-command time in a high-performance jet aircraft; 1,500 total flight hours, with 750 pilot-in-command hours, in other fixed-wing aircraft; or 1,000 total flight hours, with 300 pilot-in-command hours, in a rotary-wing aircraft.

Yes, there is a job posting for becoming an astronaut. USAJOBS, the federal government’s official employment website, states that you’ll be reporting for duty at the Lyndon B. Johnson Space Center in Houston.

It also pays to become an astronaut: $164,395 per year.

Unsurprisingly, 76% or more travel is required — or what NASA calls “extensive travel.” Kind of the point of the job, though, no?

If you meet NASA’s requirements, you have until Dec. 3 at 11:59 p.m. ET to apply.

If accepted, you’ll spend about two years in training, learning robotics, leadership, lunar geology, spacewalking and more.

After completing training, you’ll become eligible for a spaceflight assignment. Until then, you’ll be an active astronaut corps member with astronaut office responsibilities. That’s more exciting than it sounds, as it could involve supporting fellow astronauts in space or helping develop a new spacecraft.

#3084. Warhammer Survivors Isn’t Much More Than a Vampire Survivors Reskin, Which Is (Mostly) OK

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Though it passes up the opportunity to move the genre into new territory, the game is a tasty, if familiar, piece of gaming junk food.

When I first heard that developers Auroch Digital and Vampire Survivors creator Luca “Poncle” Galante were working together on Warhammer Survivors, I was thrilled. A studio of Warhammer lore fiends collaborating with a solo dev who birthed a genre seemed like a recipe for success.

But having now played through most of the game’s sci-fi battles and a smattering of fantasy incursions, I think your enjoyment of this game probably hinges entirely on how many modern bullet heaven games you’ve played. If you’re looking for something that meaningfully changes the formula, you won’t find it here.

While Vampire Survivors should be respected and lauded for laying the foundations for an entirely new type of video game, it has been surpassed by newer projects that have fundamentally changed the fledgling bullet heaven genre for the better. Newer games have implemented incredibly robust progression systems, more objective-based gameplay and entirely new camera perspectives.

There’s no problem with Survivors being knocked off the pedestal it built – every developer should learn from their peers, and the oldest games will never be the most polished games (even if they stand the test of time as cult classics).

The issue is that Warhammer Survivors just feels like a massive, reskinned expansion of Poncle’s original game. Despite a striking visual identity and plenty of Warhammer-themed weapons and items, this game never escapes the shadow of Vampire Survivors, which may diminish the enjoyment of genre enthusiasts.

Auroch Digital’s stab at a bullet heaven game isn’t unique, and its thrills wear out sooner than one might expect, but I think that’s mostly OK. It’s full of charming, vibrant Warhammer-themed pixel art, and that feels like what the true appeal of Warhammer Survivors just might be. This is the type of video game junk food that keeps you up until 5 a.m. every once in a while, completely destroying your sleep schedule. It won’t go down as one of the bullet heaven greats, and it doesn’t need to.

Vampire Survivors veterans will have a sense of deja vu from the pixel art-styling and increasing enemy clusters. There’s some extra content and quality-of-life features bolted on here, but you’ll instantly know what you’re getting into.

When players start a run, they’re dropped into iconic locations from a top-down perspective in order to purge the area of ravenous xenos and chaos cultists. The onslaught of enemies grows increasingly fearsome as time wears on, with larger and more dangerous enemies joining the fray. As players dodge and defeat the alien threats, though, they provide experience points and items that exponentially ramp up player power.

Once you start getting your hands on an expanded arsenal of weaponry, it becomes obvious why Auroch Digital was the right choice for this project – it’s a development studio full of folks who obviously love the twin worlds of Warhammer, and that passion can be found in every nook and cranny of Warhammer Survivors.

The game’s visual style is striking, which is something I’ve come to expect from any Auroch Digital game – Warhammer Boltgun has richly detailed pixel art, and the development experience from those boomer shooters has translated extremely well to Warhammer Survivors’ 8-bit arcade trappings. The cost of entry is worth it just to see massive tyranid aliens and chaos space marines rendered in all their retro glory.

And the space marine power fantasy thrives here as players wield some of the most iconic space marine weapons, including gory chainswords, hard-hitting boltguns and face-melting flamers. What’s truly special, though, is how many of the game’s weapons and passive power-ups pay homage to the series’ tabletop roots – I was chuffed to see explosive D6 dice and mini figure glue, which both became a regular part of my screen-clearing builds.

One truly unique feature allows players to collect allies that wield weapons, instead of just building out a one-man arsenal. These allies trail behind the main character, which alters how players may approach hordes of enemies.

Certain characters unlock massive buffs if they continue adding allies to the party, which introduces another way to scale up damage very quickly. This doesn’t change how account-wide progression works, but it’s a new play style that shakes up the Vampire Survivors formula.

But it was when I discovered my favorite playable character in Warhammer Survivors that I started really noticing the game’s biggest weakness. I found myself enjoying the Sister of Battle’s gameplay, since she gets to wield a holy shield that repels enemies and damages evildoers who get too close with nearly constant pulses of light. As I continued to invest in this play style, I realized that this character was a nearly one-to-one re-creation of my Vampire Survivors main (Poe Ratcho, with his stinky demon-killing garlic).

It almost feels as though Auroch Digital was afraid to deviate too far from Poncle’s original vision for a bullet heaven game. The characters and weapons feel like reskins; the upgrade shop looks nearly identical, and even the in-game war chests that elite enemies drop have the same opening animation as the loot boxes from Vampire Survivors.

Warhammer Survivors is well-built, but often feels like a massive DLC for Vampire Survivors rather than a standalone game.

There’s plenty of meat on Warhammer Survivors’ bones. The Warhammer 40,000 and Age of Sigmar universes have their own progression tracks, with unique maps, characters and secrets to unlock. Survivors’ scale is remarkable – this game is probably twice as large as Vampire Survivors was when it launched from Early Access, and it could get even bigger with post-launch updates and future DLC.

If you love Warhammer and have never played a bullet heaven game before, Warhammer Survivors will likely ensnare you the same way Vampire Survivors did the rest of us in 2022. Figuring out effective weapon combinations, slaying thousands of oncoming foes and watching loot fly out of treasure chests with all the pomp and circumstance of a digital slot machine are all still surefire ways to flood your brain with dopamine.

But if you’ve delved deep into this genre and have enjoyed the immense mechanical advancements of later games, I suspect you’ll find Warhammer Survivors a bit lacking.

At its core, this is a game centered around achieving increasingly large damage numbers to defeat foes that continue to get faster and stronger until you’re overwhelmed – it’s a simple premise that can only be mastered through repetition. Yet Poncle’s award-winning formula started wearing thin for me this time around, and I say that as someone who believes the world is richer for having more action-packed Warhammer games and good Vampire Survivors clones in it.

Too many elements feel rehashed and reused (characters and weapon drops are the worst offenders), and Warhammer Survivors very quickly begins to feel like a carbon copy of a game I’d already played. Sure, it’s copying from an excellent game. But no difficulty spike or mystery is novel enough to remove my sense of fatigue, and I was left wishing there was some additional layer of progression or mechanical depth to keep me engaged between runs.

Warhammer Survivors currently commands an even higher price tag ($8) than its predecessor. Auroch Digital is making a big ask, especially since this game doesn’t benefit from Vampire Survivors’ multiple years of free content updates and DLC. Warhammer Survivors isn’t quite as expensive as its contemporary competitors, but it also lacks their innovations.

As it stands, I think this is a solid game – but as a pricey reskin, I wouldn’t recommend it over similar games like Vampire Hunters and Megabonk, unless you’re a Warhammer fan just looking for more ways to mow through enemies.

Warhammer Survivors is available now on PC, Nintendo Switch, Xbox Series X/S, PlayStation 5, iOS and Android for $8 and is included within Game Pass subscriptions.

#3083. Resideo: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Management is betting on branded hardware over third-party OEM work, a shift that could reshape margins for years to come.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, Resideo (REZI -2.99%) reported total revenue of $727 million, growing 3% year over year despite broader housing market headwinds. The company's post-spin focus as a stand-alone product business demonstrates how pricing power in core sales channels drives profitability. Three things management said stand out.

Resideo is scaling back its original equipment manufacturer (OEM) security business, in which it manufactures products for third parties that compete with its own brand at lower margins. Expanding higher-margin core sales channels protects profitability over the long term.

Management uses cost discipline and higher-priced product launches to expand margins. The resulting cash flows straight back into research and development (R&D), creating a self-sustaining growth cycle for its core home safety and heating, ventilation, and air conditioning (HVAC) products.

Following the August spin-off of ADI Distribution (ADI), its former security distribution arm, Resideo directed immediate capital allocation toward balance sheet health. Generating cash while shrinking debt lowers interest expense and improves financial flexibility.

Core channel momentum remains steady as product pipelines deliver. Management is countering input cost inflation through price increases and site consolidations while expanding software capabilities.

Software platform investments and core hardware focus support future expansion. Operational efficiency gains help fund engineering work.
