#3082. Reddit’s New AI-Powered Reviews Are Already Controversial

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Tech > Services & Software > Online > Online Services

The platform's test of automated product review summaries is sparking concern that advertising will replace human advice.

Reddit announced on Tuesday that it’s testing Reviews, an AI-powered shopping feature that aggregates user comments into product summaries, commerce links, and pros-and-cons breakdowns. In a blog post, the company said it wants to organically use discussions on the platform, from conversations about skin care treatments or coffee to headphones, to help people “understand what a product is really like and feel confident deciding whether it’s right for them.”

The Reviews feature promises to make product research easier on a site that’s increasingly focused on shopping recommendations. But the announcement immediately rankled some Reddit users who are concerned it will degrade authentic human-to-human discussion and advice. Many Reddit moderators, or “mods,” tasked with keeping order of communities called subreddits, believe it’ll be hard to distinguish real opinions from straight-up advertising.

According to Reddit, the upcoming feature will surface product reviews in search results and through highlighted product mentions in comment threads, regardless of a group’s rules preventing the promotion of products by users or brands.

The test launch of Reviews, which was previewed in February and focused on electronics, will start with about 10,000 products across electronics, health and beauty, home and garden, and fashion and apparel. If the experiment works, Reddit plans to expand the feature outside the US and also increase the range of categories and products.

In a Reddit community for the moderators who enforce platform and subreddit rules, the new AI-powered shopping feature was greeted with skepticism and criticism. Writing in modnews, ProudnotLoud dubbed early tests “an ad-ridden, distracting mess” that made reading threads nearly impossible.

For moderators who work to keep commercial spam out of their subreddits, the feature poses an existential threat. User nerdshark warned that communities are already being actively targeted by stealth corporate campaigns, arguing that forcing Reviews into these spaces “does vulnerable communities a huge disservice,” especially without an opt-out mechanism.

Some Reddit admins have pushed back against the backlash, clarifying that the embedded product links are search shortcuts rather than paid placements. Yet users remain unconvinced. As CouncilofStrongs countered: “From a user experience, it is an ad.”

Reddit has also recently been testing advertising in search results, which could be a way for brands to reach Reddit users who click a product name even if they aren’t paying for placement in Reddit Reviews.

Given the amount of sites that focus heavily on human-centered reviews (you’re reading one of them), Reddit’s intent to aggregate product information through AI summaries could be seen as another way for traditional media publishers to have their content used without compensation.

But Reddit maintains the feature is purely informational. In a statement to CNET, a Reddit spokesperson said that Reviews relies exclusively on internal platform content and “does not source, scrape or republish third-party content.” The company said that because each review links to the original discussion, users on the platform “can explore the context and go deeper. “

Reddit also said that brands can’t buy ads to get into Reddit Reviews. “What appears in Reddit Reviews is shaped by Reddit communities, not paid placement,” the spokesperson said.

However, that reliance on organic Reddit activity brings its own vulnerabilities. One challenge Reddit may face with Reviews is trying to keep its AI focused on authentic human-written reviews versus posts that might come from marketers, coordinated astroturfing or AI-generated spam. Because the platform operates on anonymity, it’s facing an uphill battle with a massive influx of AI bots that could try to game content in Reviews.

The new feature comes alongside Reddit’s recent changes to the platform to detect spam or other manipulated content and keep bots out, including discontinuing support for RSS feeds and limiting access to its content API. The company is also restricting Old Reddit, a previous design version of the platform that has fewer ads.

A Reddit spokesperson told CNET that the platform has “the least amount of AI slop relative to other[s]” and that it is taking “numerous steps to prevent and enforce against inauthentic content.”

But while Reddit insists that Reviews will be centered on personal experience and uncorrupted by advertising dollars, many are worried the feature could blur the line between community research and automated e-commerce.

#3081. 1 Unstoppable AI Stock to Buy Hand Over Fist Before the Next Leg Higher

Iren's 70% crash does not align with its strengthening fundamentals. The mismatch won't provide an asymmetric opportunity forever.

Iren (IREN -7.85%) looks like a formidable AI stock to buy. Good stocks do not go down forever, and Iren has managed to improve its fundamentals despite dropping more than 70% from its all-time high. Recent earnings confirmed the bullish thesis, and broader neocloud trends have added fuel to the long-term picture.

Iren's business model involves building AI data centers, supplying infrastructure and cloud platforms, and renting out megawatts to hyperscalers and enterprises. As megawatts become more valuable, Iren's potential annual recurring revenue increases, and the company's recent earnings report hinted at this potential.

AI cloud revenue almost tripled sequentially to $200 million, which is a small slice of Iren's $20.3 billion revenue backlog. Operating annual recurring revenue has just crossed $1 billion, and the company intends to reach $4 billion by the end of January.

Notably, Iren secured a five-year deal at $22 million per megawatt and landed a short-term deal at $40 million per megawatt. Both of those figures are much higher than its landmark deal with Microsoft last year, which came in at $9.7 million per megawatt.

The value of a megawatt has more than doubled in less than one year. If that momentum continues, Iren will end up with far more lucrative deals in 2027. Leadership has also been very intentional about not rushing to sign new deals, which has proven to be the right call with each passing day.

Although October featured plenty of talk about neoclouds having less access to financing and being stuck with high interest rates, those issues don't concern Iren. Even the shareholder dilution concerns have faded after the company laid out its plan to raise capital.

Prepayments do the bulk of the work. The prepayment under the five-year deal, valuing each megawatt at $22 million, was sufficient to cover 90% of the associated GPU capital expenditures. That doesn't even include GPU financing, and Iren also has the flexibility to borrow against its data centers in the future.

Iren has no issue with raising the amount of funds it needs. This was a major bearish concern multiple months ago, especially stock dilution. Even though Iren has addressed this issue and strengthened its financial position, the growth stock remains well below its all-time highs. This mismatch is part of the reason Iren looks enticing at current levels.

As Iren delivers more megawatts and reports profitable quarters, funding its expansion will become even easier.

Although Iren has been quietly making more deals, it didn't add any additional sites this quarter, resulting in a 5.8-gigawatt portfolio. However, the company did unveil more than 35 gigawatts in its development pipeline. It demonstrates how quickly the tech company can add more gigawatts to its pipeline, but the conversion from developmental gigawatts to delivered power is not straightforward.

While Iren knows how to build data centers on time and on budget, the company acknowledged that its development projects are "speculative" and that a "significant portion" of them may not secure power. In other words, a large chunk of them will fall through.

Iren doesn't need all 35 of those gigawatts online to become a juggernaut. Even if it just adds 10 gigawatts from this development pipeline, Iren ends up with roughly 15.8 gigawatts. At $20 million per megawatt, that translates into more than $300 billion in annual recurring revenue.

Iren is at the center of the artificial intelligence boom. Neoclouds like Iren and Nebius (NBIS -5.27%) may be just as important as AI chipmakers in scaling out this new technology.

Need a NYT Mini Crossword answer today? We’ve got every answer for the Mini Crossword on Wednesday, Nov. 11.

Need the answers for today's NYT Mini Crossword?

Need some help with today’s Mini Crossword? The diverse mix of answers includes everything from pizza toppings to Supreme Court justices. Read on for all the answers.

The Mini Crossword is a nice break from tougher puzzles and takes only a fraction of the time needed to solve the vaunted full-size New York Times Crossword.

CNET publishes daily answers and hints for Wordle, The New York Times Mini Crossword, Connections, Connections: Sports Edition and Strands puzzles.

1A clue: Animal whose prominent feature makes it an unofficial mascot of WednesdayAnswer: CAMEL

Chase's hotel and resort program, The Edit, gives some cardholders the chance to book stays at spectacular hotels.

Many premium credit cards have their own travel portals where you can earn and redeem your points. They also have a curated list of hotels with which they partner to offer elevated benefits to their cardholders who book directly through their program.

The Edit is Chase’s program, and similar to the Fine Hotels & Resorts program from American Express, travelers need to book a prepaid reservation directly through a Chase Travel channel and pay with their Chase card to receive the benefits.

What benefits do you receive when you book through the program? Well, they can be substantial.

At nearly 1,400 hotels and resorts, The Edit customers will enjoy:

If the hotel is an all-inclusive property, guests would instead receive a $200 property credit to use for experiences like spa treatments. While stays at hotels in The Edit Boutique Collection do include the other perks, those stays are not eligible for a room upgrade or early check-in/late checkout considerations.

The tradeoff of using The Edit program is that it does not necessarily always have the lowest rate. Sometimes you can find a lower rate directly with the hotel or another website. But, when you consider the list of benefits above, any differential in price can more than outweigh the extra cost.

While it belongs to Chase, The Edit is not open to all Chase cardholders. It is a dedicated perk for primary cardholders and authorized users of the Chase Sapphire Reserve® and J.P. Morgan Reserve cards, as well as primary Sapphire Reserve for Business℠ cardholders.

Even better is that these eligible Chase cards come with a total of $500 in credits that can be used at The Edit hotels each year. They are divided into two $250 credits that each must be used for at least a two-night stay. And when paying for the rest of your stay through The Edit, cardholders earn 8x Chase Ultimate Rewards® points per dollar spent. In addition, hotel stays booked through The Edit and labeled as “Loyalty Plus” are eligible to also earn points with any associated hotel loyalty program.

These are some of our favorite hotels in the program where you can take advantage of your annual $500 credits as well as earn significant Chase points for a prepaid stay.

#3078. Want Apple Intelligence for Your Home Security Camera? You’ll Need These 4 Things

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Apple Intelligence offers smart AI features for compatible cameras, but you’ll need to play by Apple’s rules. Here’s all the requirements.

Senior Editor / Home Security and Smart Home

Senior Editor / Home Security and Smart Home

In the past months Apple has unveiled a host of new Apple Intelligence and Siri AI features, with more on the way. And some features immediately caught my attention because they can enable security cameras on Apple devices in all-new ways. The most interesting option even adds video descriptions, essentially using AI to “read” your security clips and give you texts about what’s happening in them, so you don’t have to view them yourself.

It’s a nifty trick, one that I’ve seen come to Google Nest, Arlo, Ring, Blink and other home security camera companies in the past year or two. Now it’s Apple’s turn, but enabling this AI feature takes more work this time around. Apple wants to keep users inside its ecosystem, so there are more requirements than usual for smart video descriptions and similar capabilities. Let’s go through exactly what you’ll need.

Apple Intelligence doesn’t run on just any device. Your first requirement is an iPhone or iPad that can handle it. If you recently downloaded iOS 27 or iPadOS 27 and received an update about Apple Intelligence now being on your device, you should be good to go.

Specifically, you’ll need an iPhone 15 Pro, iPhone 15 Pro Max or iPhone 16 model or later to run these features. If you prefer using an iPad, you’ll need the iPad Mini (A17 Pro or later) or an iPad model with the M1 chip or later.

Before you start using security features with Apple Intelligence, make sure everything is updated – you’ll want the latest iOS if you don’t have it yet.

Smart home devices that work with Apple Home typically need a hub to pair with, and security cameras using Apple Intelligence are no exception. That means you’ll need another Apple device that can act as a hub, and you’ve got several choices.

Apple hubs include the second-gen HomePod, the HomePod Mini and Mini 2 or an Apple TV 4K model. Apple briefly gave some iPads the ability to become home hubs but then stopped, so an iPad won’t be sufficient here.

You’ll need a security camera that works well with Apple Home, but that’s easier said than done. If you can wait, Apple’s own security camera line, made in partnership with LG, should be here by the end of 2026, and should have the best results.

However, those Apple cams will be very expensive, so I understand if you want to look for a more affordable option. Not many security cams currently work with Apple, but one of the best brands is Aqara, like this Aqara G3650 camera hub ($140) I’m testing right now. Aqara models go down to $40, so there’s plenty of room to save here.

Note that, at least for the base subscription, I’ve seen reports that AI features like video descriptions only work with one camera at a time, so getting a whole fleet of cams won’t make much difference here. And speaking of subscriptions, there’s one final piece you’ll want.

Apple Intelligence features may require an advanced iCloud subscription.

You’ll want an iCloud Plus subscription to enable the full suite of Siri AI capabilities and avoid running into daily limits. But to be clear, even if you don’t use Siri AI, these security Apple Intelligence features will require at least a 2TB plan, which starts at $10 per month. That’s not surprising – video descriptions like this typically come with a subscription requirement, up to $20 per month in some cases.

Now you’ve got everything you need for Apple Intelligence security features. To get started, just go to Apple Home, go to Home Settings and make sure Apple Intelligence is toggled on. Then you should be able to choose an added camera to turn on video descriptions.

#3077. Forget the Market's Hottest Stocks. This Boring Dividend Stock Is Where I'd Put $1,000 Before 2027.

The market environment for the foreseeable future could be very different than it's been in the recent past.

After last week's sizable stumble, artificial intelligence stocks seem to be stabilizing, perhaps regrouping for a recovery. The AI trade appears to be back on.

Still, I'm not overly interested in adding more exposure to the AI sliver of the market while it's discounted. Last week's sell-off shows some vulnerability on this front, and perhaps for most of the overall stock market now that it's about as expensive as it's been in many years. We may be moving into a period where a surer thing is the smarter thing, including reliable income.

To this end, while it's admittedly boring, if I had an extra $1,000 to put to work right now, I'd use it to take on a position in the mundane grocery store chain The Kroger Company (KR +0.87%), plugging into its just-mediocre forward-looking yield of 2.3%. This often-ignored outfit has a very bullish secret.

You almost certainly know the company, perhaps better than you realize. While most of its nearly 2,800 grocery stores peppered across the United States operate under the Kroger banner, the company is also parent to Fred Meyer, Ralphs, Pick 'n Save, Marianos, and King Soopers, just to name a few. There's a good chance you've shopped at a Kroger-owned store without even realizing it, contributing to last year's revenue growth of 2.9% to $147.6 billion, more than $4.9 billion of which was turned into operating profit. That's not huge growth or a particularly wide profit margin rate, although by grocery store standards, it is.

This company delivers more shareholder value growth than it seems like it should be able to, however. While its top-line growth is usually only in the low single digits, over the course of the past 10 years, Kroger's dividend has grown by an average of 12.5%, from $0.12 then to $0.39 per share now. That's enormous dividend growth for any industry.

Granted, stock buybacks helped -- a lot. Since 2016, the grocer's outstanding share count has been dialed back from 959 million to 608 million as of the middle of this year, helping its annual earnings improve from $2.06 per share then to $4.85 per share now.

That's far from being a misleading metric, though. The Kroger Company earned enough real accessible cash during this stretch to fund these generous stock repurchases and pay its dividends.

This might put things in their proper perspective with this boring dividend stock: Had you made a $10,000 investment in KR stock 10 years ago and reinvested any dividends it dished out along the way, today your position would be worth $26,870, with most of that gain coming from -- surprisingly enough -- the stock's price appreciation rather than dividends. Your position would also be generating more than $600 worth of annual dividend income, by the way, to do with whatever you wish.

Then there's the simple fact that Kroger sells products that people must purchase over and over again, regardless of their price.

Obviously, there's no guarantee that the next 10 years will be as fruitful for Kroger's shareholders as the past 10 have been. In fact, there's a reasonably good chance they won't be. The grocer's considerably bigger now than it was then, so opportunities for future growth are (relatively) smaller in size and fewer in number. The stock's price has also nearly doubled during this time, dampening the benefit of its stock buybacks. Be sure to keep any expectations in check.

Still, while past performance is no guarantee of future results, the past is a reasonably good indication of how well a company functions, which points to the plausible future. All the attributes that made KR such a high-performance ticker for the past decade are still in place, and likely to continue growing this stock's value, even if at a slower pace.

More than anything, though, I'm starting to think the market environment is shifting toward a slower-moving one that's less charged by AI enthusiasm, making cash-generating names like Kroger all the more valuable. I don't want to simply shed all of my more aggressive growth holdings here. However, with interest rates already high and still rising, I do think it's time to prepare for a bit more economic lethargy. That means more reliable -- and growing -- cash flow, since the returns on other types of investments may be on the verge of weakening.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

#3074. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 dividend stocks for passive income

The uncertainty in the Middle East and concerns about the durability of the artificial intelligence boom and high spending continue to weigh on investor sentiment. Investors can consider strengthening their portfolios by adding dividend stocks.

Tracking top Wall Street analysts can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts provide useful insights after thorough analysis of a company's financials and growth prospects.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first dividend pick is natural gas producer Expand Energy, which recently announced the $1.25 billion acquisition of Twin Eagle Holdings, a private asset-backed natural gas marketing and optimization business. The deal is expected to accelerate EXE's marketing and commercial goals.

Meanwhile, Expand Energy impressed investors with solid second-quarter results. It repurchased shares worth $530 million in Q2 2026 and announced an additional $1 billion of buyback authorization. Expand declared a dividend of nearly 58 cents per share, payable on Sept. 3. At an annualized dividend per share of $2.30, EXE stock offers a dividend yield of 2.5%.

Following the Q2 print, Wolfe Research analyst Doug Leggate reiterated a buy rating on EXE stock and raised his price target to $114 from $110. The five-star analyst noted that Expand delivered market-beating adjusted earnings per share and earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization, driven by better gas realizations and lower gathering, processing, and transportation expenses.

"Solid 2Q26 with in-line guidance keeps our focus on what it can control, walking the line between options to return value to s/holders," said Leggate.

As Expand Energy continues to weigh options to enhance shareholder returns, Leggate believes that net debt reduction is the best choice. He highlighted that the company repaid $1.3 billion of gross debt in April, supported by seasonally higher cash flows in the first quarter. EXE ended Q2 2026 with $3.1 billion in net debt. Leggate views buybacks as the second-best option and mergers and acquisitions as the third, with the Twin Eagle deal expected to lower Expand's breakeven by about 7 cents from about $2.80/Mcf.

Leggate ranks No. 807 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.1%. See Expand Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Moving on to SM Energy, an operator of oil and gas assets across four leading U.S. shale basins: the Permian Basin, DJ Basin, South Texas, and Uinta Basin. SM offers a quarterly dividend of 22 cents per share, or an annualized dividend of 88 cents per share, representing a yield of about 2.7%.

Following a preliminary update on Q2 metrics, Roth analyst Leo Mariani reaffirmed a buy rating on SM Energy stock and raised his price target to $34 from $32. The analyst cited higher oil prices and his optimism about a strong second-quarter report as the reasons for the upgraded price target.

Commenting on the key insights from the Q2 preliminary update, Mariani noted strong oil and gas price realizations in the quarter. However, the update reflected a slightly larger cash hedging loss of $220 million from derivatives, compared to Roth's estimate of about $211 million.

Meanwhile, the five-star analyst raised his cash flow per share estimate by 3%, citing a strong Q2 2026 pricing update. Mariani added that he expects SM Energy to report strong Q2 2026 results on Aug. 5, with oil production of 237,650 barrels of oil per day, which is around 1.5% above the Street's estimate. He expects capital expenditure of $820 million, in line with consensus.

"We rate SM Energy a Buy based on its reasonable returns of capital to shareholders, its discounted valuation vs. peers, and its upside potential in the Austin Chalk and Uinta plays," said Mariani.

Mariani ranks No. 65 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 27%. See SM Energy Financials on TipRanks.

Oilfield services provider SLB, formerly known as Schlumberger, reported better-than-expected Q2 earnings. The company attributed its Q2 performance to sequential growth across international markets, mainly led by offshore activity in Latin America, Europe and Africa, as well as Asia, helping offset disruptions in the Middle East due to the U.S.-Iran conflict.

SLB announced a quarterly cash dividend of almost 30 cents per share, payable on Oct. 8. At an annualized dividend per share of $1.18, SLB's yield stands at 2.4%.

Reacting to the Q2 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reaffirmed a buy rating on SLB stock with a price target of $62. "Given SLB's position within international OFS, we reiterate our Buy rating and see forward activity expectations as a driver of earnings," said the analyst.

Within Goldman's oilfield services coverage, Mehta is optimistic about SLB as he expects the company to benefit from a recovery in international activity. The five-star analyst projects international revenue growth of about 10% between 2026 and 2027, driven by SLB's exposure to incremental intervention activity and a recovery in Middle East activity. He also expects international revenue to gain from higher offshore activity and a potential ramp-up of exploration activity across several geographies.

Furthermore, Mehta's bullish stance is backed by long-term growth prospects of SLB's data center business. He noted that the company expects the annual run-rate revenue of this business to exceed $2 billion by the end of 2027, with the potential to grow the customer base and products. Mehta also expects SLB to deliver strong free cash flow and shareholder returns this year, driven by higher activity and unique business lines.

VA mortgage borrowers are quietly navigating a knowledge gap. Here’s how to close it.

If your understanding of a VA home loan starts and ends with “it’s for the military,” you’re not alone. For many eligible veterans and service members, the intricate workings (and benefits) of this U.S. government-backed mortgage are obscured behind lackluster guidance.

While there are classes and resources available, the military is broadly wary of steering members in a specific direction, says Alex Rodino, a real estate agent and U.S. Army veteran who served 10 years as an infantry officer and military advisor. “A lot of the course material is just very generic,” he says.

And that’s not a surprise. Considering the sheer size and scope of the military, providing personalized advice on VA loans to all its members would be no easy task.

That leaves real estate agents and lenders as the last line of defense, which is where a big part of the knowledge gap exists. Rodino purchased his first home in 2020 with a VA loan and says he didn’t like how ignorant he felt during the process.

“The reason I actually got my real estate license to begin with was because of that experience I had,” Rodino says. “Looking back on it, I had a lot of questions that didn't really get answered.”

But how can VA borrowers navigate this knowledge gap without having to become mortgage experts in their spare time? Here are some of the most important things to know when considering a VA loan, according to the financial experts and borrowers who have lived it.

“My mortgage broker said, hey, you might qualify for this, look it up,” says Elizabeth Valente, a real estate investor and former U.S. Army officer who bought her first home using a VA loan in 2021. When it came to the application process, she felt crucial information wasn’t readily available to her or fully explained.

In hindsight, Valente says, working with an agent or lender who had detailed VA loan experience would have been beneficial. Not only would it help untangle the specific paperwork and appraisal requirements, but that expertise offers an important layer of support for military borrowers.

“For some processes in the military, it's very hand-holding,” Valente says. “I'll tell you what to wear, what to bring, when to be there.” But that’s not always true when transitioning back to civilian life, and parsing specifics of a loan process can be challenging. “I don't think a typical Realtor would understand what that person's going through,” Valente says.

Finding professionals with the right expertise involves asking questions — and lots of them.

“If I'm the smartest person in the room, we have an issue,” Valente says. “Don't be afraid to tell someone if something doesn't make sense or if you don't understand the numbers.”

Before agreeing to work with a lender or mortgage broker, ask how their process works, what companies they work with, and how familiar they are with VA loans. Treat the initial conversation like an interview — you’re hiring them, not the other way around.

Knowing where to find your Certificate of Eligibility (COE) and how much you might be eligible to borrow is the responsibility of the person applying, Valente says. “All that information — it is available in the [VA] website but you really have to dig for it.”

And this information isn’t recreational reading — your COE lists your "basic entitlement," which is a crucial figure in VA lending. If you have full basic entitlement, the U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) generally guarantees 25% of the loan, which in most cases lets you put 0% down.

Why? With a quarter of the loan guaranteed by the VA, the lender is more protected and thus more willing to forgo a down payment.

So how do you know if you have full basic entitlement? Look for the figure $36,000 on your COE. This dollar amount represents full entitlement. Technically, basic entitlement is for loans up to $144,000, but then your bonus entitlement kicks in and you can go higher. The VA doesn’t set loan limits when you have full entitlement — though your lender will still decide how much you can borrow based on your income, debts, credit and the home’s appraised value.

Let’s say you want to buy a home in Fayetteville, North Carolina, and you need a $200,000 loan. In order to put no money down, your entitlement needs to cover at least 25% of the loan amount — in this example, that’s $50,000. After you purchase the home, your COE will show your basic entitlement as $0 and the entitlement charged to a previous loan as $50,000.

“Serving in the military is a unique investing opportunity that most people don't get,” Rodino says. “I wish someone had told me to buy a house when I got to my first duty station.”

If he’d known it was possible or understood how, Rodino says he would have been able to buy in 2017, move in 2020, rent out his first home and then repeat the process. “I'd have three by now and the first one would probably be paid off.”

This particular plan is partly based on the unique schedule of many military members. “Generally, you're moving anywhere from two to four to six years,” Rodino says. Owning multiple properties also leverages two big (and often misunderstood) perks of the VA loan: It’s possible to have more than one active loan at the same time, and after one year, you may be able to convert your primary residence to a rental.

Let’s go back to Fayetteville, North Carolina. After two years, you decide to rent out the home because you’re moving to Bell County, Texas. The home you want to buy in Bell County is $300,000, and in order to make no down payment, you need the VA to guarantee 25% of the loan ($75,000).

Because you no longer have full basic entitlement — remember, you already have an active VA loan elsewhere — your entitlement is capped at 25% of the conforming loan limit in the county where you plan to buy, minus what’s already being used on your first loan ($50,000 in our Fayetteville example).

Now for the math: The current conforming loan limit in Bell County is $832,750. Multiply this figure by 25% and subtract the $50,000 already in use: You have $158,187.50 of entitlement available to you — plenty to guarantee the $75,000 needed on the new loan.

If all of this sounds overwhelming, you’re not wrong. There are resources out there to help you figure all of this out, Rodino says. “The problem is, they're not always practical or they're not always apparent.”

The VA has a website that can walk you through entitlement calculations, but you should also ask your lender, broker or agent. If you’re working with experienced folks, they’ll be able to explain these details and help you get the most out of the VA’s guarantees.

The key takeaway is this: While each VA loan must initially be for a primary residence, borrowers can leverage their entitlement to build an investment portfolio without needing to cough up large down payments.

But these examples don’t consider all of the factors that go into approvals. Like most mortgages, your income, debts and assets dictate your eligibility. And although the VA doesn’t impose credit score minimums, most lenders do — NerdWallet's analysis shows 620 is a common floor.

It’s also important to note that a VA loan’s 0% down isn’t free. VA loans carry a funding fee based on how much you’re putting down and whether it’s your first or subsequent use (certain disabled veterans are exempt). You can pay this up front at closing or roll it into the loan amount; again, your team of professionals should be able to walk you through your options.

“I think there's the assumption that this is what we do: We get our VA home loan and we go buy a home,” says Julie Soares, financial readiness director for the Wounded Warrior Project. When we’re given access to a benefit that offers discounts and perks, it can be hard not to take advantage of it. But this is why financial awareness is critical.

There are a lot of conversations happening right now around homeownership and whether it’s the right choice, especially as affordability remains challenging. So if you haven’t heard it recently, here’s a reminder: Buying property is not the only way to build wealth or create financial security.

“The financial readiness program at Wounded Warrior Project is really leaning into getting further upstream in those conversations,” Soares says. Having a clear understanding of your benefits before you need to use them allows you to make more informed choices.

While it's helpful to understand your entitlement and what you may be eligible to borrow, you don't have to know every little detail about mortgages, or navigate the process alone. Sometimes, the most important thing to know about VA loans is where to turn for help.

If you are unsure of your eligibility or want more information about home loans, you can call the VA's home loans hotline at 877-827-3702.

“Please reach out to Wounded Warrior Project, reach out to the VA, reach out to any service organization,” Soares says. “Not one organization is gonna be able to fix or solve all of these problems. We have to do this as a group.”

#3072. Coincheck: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Management's plan treats weak retail trading as a test of whether core operations can fund growth without new capital.

On its second-quarter fiscal 2027 earnings call, held on Nov. 9, 2026, Coincheck (CNCK -11.90%) detailed a three-phase operational plan to turn its core trading platform into a self-funding cash engine. Long-term investors need to evaluate whether the business can build lasting enterprise value as retail trading activity slows. Three takeaways from management's comments illustrate how the business plans to navigate this market shift.

Management is setting strict margin benchmarks across its core marketplace and asset management units so cash flow directly covers new product rollouts. This disciplined framework reduces reliance on external debt or equity dilution while retail trading volumes remain depressed.

The company is winding down Aplo, its European institutional brokerage subsidiary, to cut costs and integrate its trading technology into 3iQ, its Canadian digital asset management subsidiary. Reallocating those assets enables 3iQ to launch a separately managed account (SMA) product line for institutional clients in the first half of calendar 2027.

To offset lower spot crypto trading volumes in Japan, management launched contracts for differences (CFDs), which are leverage-based derivative products, in September 2026. Expanding into leveraged products and crypto-as-a-service partnerships positions the platform to capture demand from both institutional and retail investors as Japanese regulations evolve.

Trading activity across the crypto market remains muted, but management isn't waiting for market sentiment to recover before executing its long-term plan. The company holds key regulatory licenses in Japan, strong distribution partnerships with major financial firms, and an established institutional asset manager in 3iQ. Management focuses on strict margin benchmarks and efficient capital allocation across every operating unit.

By connecting its Japanese distribution network with 3iQ's institutional asset management capabilities, the business aims to create a repeatable platform that expands internationally once local operations generate consistent cash flow.

#3071. First Look: Inside United’s New Club at Washington Dulles

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

The airline’s newest lounge will be the second-largest in the network, offering flyers an upgraded experience at Dulles.

You’ll no longer have to go to the basement to get into a United Club at Washington Dulles International Airport.

The brand-new United Club in the new IAD Concourse E spans nearly 40,000 square feet across an upstairs floor and can seat 630 passengers, making it a significant upgrade to the carrier’s existing lounge portfolio at the airport. It’s the second-largest United Club after its newest lounge in Houston, which opened just weeks earlier.

The new lounge by gates E14 and E16 opens to customers Nov. 10, 2026. United invited me to a media preview of the lounge, and here’s what you can expect inside.

The lounge features several nods to D.C.’s iconic landmarks and monuments, as well as the region’s natural beauty.

When you first enter the lounge, you’ll need to scan your boarding pass and then head up the escalators (stairs and elevators are also available) to get to the lounge.

One of my favorite design features as a former D.C. resident is that escalator ride up, which has been recreated to feel like you’re inside a Metro station.

Thankfully, the escalator ride is not as long as at some stations. Looking at you, Dupont Circle!

Once you're upstairs, you’ll walk into the heart of the lounge, with two buffet stations, a centerpiece bar and plenty of seating options, whether you’re traveling as a group or by yourself. (More on food and beverage options later.)

It’s impossible to miss the soaring ceilings in this lounge, especially compared to the bunker-like atmosphere at some of the existing United Clubs.

Don’t forget to look up, where you’ll see a cherry blossom sculpture, an homage to one of the region’s most popular attractions.

There are also two additional lounging areas on both sides of the central area. One is inspired by the Blue Ridge Mountains, which you can see in the distance.

The other has a cherry blossom theme, with light fixtures that branch out from the ceiling.

Massive windows run throughout the lounge, allowing in tons of natural light and offering fantastic views of the airfield. United has a major presence at IAD, so expect to see plenty of United planes.

The United Club has a full kitchen, allowing it to offer upgraded food options compared to its existing lounges at IAD.

You can expect to find sandwiches, salads and hot protein dishes. The lounge also had a ramen bar and a chili bar set up during my visit.

The two buffet stations are located right next to each other, with a dessert station in between.

If you want something to drink, you can head to the bar or one of the hydration stations throughout the space.

There’s plenty of seating at the bar, which has a double archway design. There are also large TVs if you’re trying to keep up with one of Washington’s many sports teams.

The United Club has eight phone booths for those who need to make a call. They’re split between the two lounging areas in the club.

You'll also find a single-stall private restroom, a wellness room, and dedicated men’s and women’s restrooms.

There are no showers in this lounge. To access a shower in a United lounge, you’ll need to head to the Polaris Lounge, reserved exclusively for eligible business class passengers flying on United and select partner airlines.

You can access the United Club by holding elite status, a lounge membership, the right credit card or an eligible premium cabin ticket.

If there’s availability, United sells day passes at the door for flights departing within three hours. You’ll also need a valid same-day boarding pass on a United or Star Alliance flight.

Holding the right credit card is probably the easiest way to get in. United’s most high-end card comes with a United Club membership, while others might offer one-time passes.

If you’re looking for a United lounge, the newest club in Concourse E is where you want to be. It offers more space, lots of light and elevated food options compared to other United Clubs.

Its massive footprint could also ease overcrowding, a common complaint with other lounges.

One thing to consider is location. The concourses at IAD are spread out, and you may need to take a mobile lounge or train to get there. If you want to check out the lounge, make sure you give yourself enough time to get to your gate.

#3069. Bad News: There's a New Red Flag Ahead of the Anthropic IPO

The bond market has a can’t-miss warning for investors in artificial intelligence (AI) stocks.

Five months ago, on June 12, Elon Musk's Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +0.72%), better known as SpaceX, rewrote Wall Street's record books with its $1.77 trillion initial public offering (IPO). In mere weeks, the developer of the Claude large language model (LLM), Anthropic (Nasdaq: ANT), may challenge SpaceX for the top spot with its own IPO.

Anthropic filed its public registration statement (S-1) on Oct. 26, starting the 15-calendar-day clock until it markets its upcoming debut and (likely) kicks off an IPO roadshow. With a lightning-fast growth rate that had Anthropic generating $65 billion in annual run rate sales as of July, there should be plenty of excited retail investors.

But skepticism is also building ahead of this monumental IPO. On top of a laundry list of historical headwinds, a new red flag has emerged ahead of Anthropic's debut.

Though history is an ally of long-term-minded investors, it can be the foe of IPOs and hyped tech trends, at least over shorter timelines.

For instance, every game-changing tech innovation since the mid-1990s has navigated an early-stage bubble-bursting event. These bubbles form and subsequently burst because investors overestimate how quickly game-changing tech innovations will be optimized. Consistently overshooting the mark leaves hyped technologies, including artificial intelligence (AI), prone to a bubble-bursting event.

Moral of the story - do NOT chase hot IPOs Year-1 average drawdown = 55% Year-1 median drawdown = 54% Table: Truist

Additionally, tech-focused IPOs don't have the best track record early on. Data from Truist Financial (TFC -0.14%) shows that the average year-one maximum drawdown for the 30 hottest tech-driven IPOs since May 2012 is 55%! SpaceX, which debuted after Truist released its data set, has endured a max drawdown of 54%!

History also says that premium valuations aren't well tolerated by Wall Street. For example, a trailing 12-month (TTM) price-to-sales (P/S) ratio above 30 can alert investors to an unsustainable bubble. Anthropic's TTM P/S ratio should land between 75 and 100 at its IPO.

The bad news for retail investors is that historical precedent isn't the only problem for Anthropic. The bond market is increasingly signaling that trouble lies ahead for AI hardware providers, neoclouds, and hyperscalers.

While some companies are financing the AI infrastructure build-out and the development of LLMs with cash on hand, most are relying on debt in some capacity. When public companies issue debt, they do so at a par value of $1,000, which is commonly expressed as $1 on financial websites. When bond traders are confident a company will make good on its debt obligations, the bond price hovers around par value.

Credit markets are flashing WARNING SIGNS across the AI trade: Oracle, $ORCL, 5-year credit default swap (CDS) spread has surged to ~263 basis points, while SpaceX's has risen to ~203 basis points, both at record highs. At the same time, CoreWeave's $CRWV, 5-year CDS is up to a record 982 basis points. This comes as tech companies have issued nearly $500 billion in new debt this year to finance AI infrastructure, with 9 US investment-grade bond deals worth at least $25 billion each. The growing debt burden is colliding with uncertainty over whether AI revenues will justify the enormous spending. As a result, investors are increasingly demanding higher compensation to take on credit risk. Oracle's market-implied 5-year default risk has risen above 20%, compared with around 16% for SpaceX and more than 7% for Nvidia. The pressure could intensify further, with Goldman Sachs projecting hyperscaler debt issuance to jump from $250 billion in 2026 to a massive $420 billion in 2027, as 35% of next year's capital spending is expected to be debt-funded. The AI boom is now testing credit markets, as investors begin repricing the risks behind Big Tech's unprecedented borrowing spree.

What we're seeing is several mid- and long-duration bond prices for AI companies plunging. The 2056 note SpaceX issued at par less than five months ago is trading at an all-time low of $0.83. Even Nvidia's (NVDA -5.47%) 2060 notes are trading at just $0.63.

The rout we've witnessed in bond prices, which has driven up yields, indicates tangible skepticism about the ability of AI companies to meet their debt obligations.

Given that Anthropic's operating model isn't yet time-tested or proven, this bond market malaise can sink Wall Street's hottest upcoming IPO before it has any chance to gain traction.

#3068. This Fast-Growing Insurance Stock Is About to Do Something It's Never Done Before

Lemonade expects its first quarter of positive adjusted EBITDA, and its growth is speeding up at the same time.

For most of its time as a public company, the knock on Lemonade (LMND -1.73%) has been simple. It grows fast, but it loses a lot of money doing it. That's about to change, at least by one important measure.

In its third-quarter earnings report, Lemonade said that it expects to generate positive adjusted EBITDA (earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization) in the fourth quarter for the first time. This is a major milestone, and it's one that shareholders have been waiting for. With that in mind, here's a rundown of Lemonade's numbers, its profitability claims, and what investors should keep in mind as we head into 2027.

The best measure of Lemonade's growth is in-force premium, the annualized value of all the insurance policies it has in place. In the third quarter, this figure grew 34% year over year to $1.55 billion, an acceleration from growth in the low 30s over the previous four quarters. Other growth metrics look excellent as well. For example, Lemonade now has 3.52 million customers, 23% more than a year ago.

Lemonade's revenue grew even faster, up 73% year-over-year to $336.1 million. However, a big part of that came from Lemonade passing less of its business to reinsurers, which means it keeps more premium (and more risk) on its own books. That's one big reason why it's smart to focus on in-force premium instead.

The car insurance business is a big part of the momentum. This is both Lemonade's largest growth opportunity (by far), as well as one of its big risk factors, as auto insurance is highly competitive. But the news was great. In-force premium from Lemonade Car rose to $265 million from $163 million a year ago, and the product is now available in 14 states.

Lemonade's adjusted EBITDA was a loss of $20.4 million in the third quarter, but management expects a positive $5 million to $9 million in the fourth quarter and its first full year of positive adjusted EBITDA in 2027. The plan is to grow marketing and other growth spending more slowly than in-force premium from here, so this figure should get significantly larger in the future.

It's also worth noting that Lemonade already generates cash. Adjusted free cash flow was $27 million in the third quarter and $63.2 million through the first nine months of the year. But adjusted EBITDA is a more widely recognized metric of profitability, and helps provide an easier comparison with other companies that also report this figure.

To be sure, adjusted EBITDA isn't the same as profit, and Lemonade still reported a net loss of $0.56 per share. A big reason why is the company's stock-based compensation, which is on the high end and has been for Lemonade's entire publicly traded history. Interest expense has also been elevated recently.

Plus, Lemonade's gross loss ratio, the share of premiums paid out in claims, rose to 65% from 62% a year ago, which is still significantly below Lemonade's 75% stated target. Over the past 12 months, it was a much better 60%, but insurance results can swing from quarter to quarter, especially when catastrophes hit. Keeping more risk on its own books also means a bad quarter would hurt more than it used to. And car insurance is a tough, competitive business.

Having said all that, Lemonade is growing faster while spending less to do it, and it's on the verge of the profitability milestone that many skeptics said it would never reach. I'm holding my shares, and the fourth-quarter results will be the first test of whether this turnaround is for real.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews of their products or services. Here is a list of our partners.

Prediction markets are booming, but that doesn’t make them a sound investment strategy.

The investing information provided on this page is for educational purposes only. NerdWallet, Inc. does not offer advisory or brokerage services, nor does it recommend or advise investors to buy or sell particular stocks, securities or other investments.

Around this time every year, we conduct NerdWallet’s annual broker review process. We assess what’s changed in the industry, discuss which brokerage features matter most (and which ones now matter less), and finally, we do the work by opening accounts to test them.

Prediction markets, which have made their way into some of the most popular investing apps, came up more than we expected in testing this year.

At first, we decided to simply collect information on who does and doesn’t offer access to them, without intending to reward or penalize a broker either way. It would be a neutral presentation of facts, not something that would factor into how we scored a broker.

But one afternoon, all together in the office, we confronted an uncomfortable question: “Do prediction markets, and such easy access to them within an investment app, go against NerdWallet’s editorial house views?”

After careful consideration, we all agreed: they do.

So we started to configure our 2027 rubric with a firmer stance against this trendy feature, penalizing brokers that actively market sports and entertainment event contracts, and whose prediction markets come with few, if any, guardrails.

We’ve distilled our discussion down to three main points:

These products are being marketed alongside investments.

We fear they could be discouraging long-term investing.

Let’s start here: Many aspects of trading could be seen as gambling-adjacent. Options, futures, highly leveraged single-day equity bets and even crypto can all easily fall into the “highly speculative” category. So why are we picking on prediction markets in particular?

In our view, a key differentiator is a much lower barrier to entry with few in-app guardrails in place.

Most of the products we mentioned above require some level of fluency — and at times even permission from the broker — to dabble in. To trade options, for example, you need to answer questions about your investing proficiency, your finances and more. And even after approval, the terminology required to trade can be so intimidating that the language itself can become an indirect deterrent.

Event contracts, which form the backbone of prediction markets, are largely presented in the vernacular, and our testing showed approval to trade them is completed with a few taps from the home screen. Moreover, their content is often unrelated to finance — according to a May 2026 report by the Pew Research Center, around 80% of volume on the prediction market platform Kalshi was for sports events[1]Pew Research Center. Trading Volume on Prediction Markets Has Soared in Recent Months. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources .

Answering a binary question (yes/no; this/that), posed in everyday language about a familiar topic — something like, “Will the Knicks beat the Spurs on Sunday?” — can be far less daunting than figuring out what an options chain is or understanding forex pairs.

In other words, what makes prediction markets accessible to a wider audience is also what makes them problematic, especially when housed within an investing account. Which brings us to our next point.

On the platforms that feature them, we found that prediction markets almost always appear alongside stock and ETF trading. We think this is a mistake. What has made these mobile-first investing apps so good for so long (making it easy to move, manage and invest money through a beautiful interface) is what’s now making us nervous.

For example, most investing platforms let users do any number of smart things with idle cash. They could open an Individual Retirement Account (IRA), a custodial account or a robo-managed portfolio all through a single app. But now, they can dip into that same pot of money to bet on whether Beyoncé or Alicia Keys will play the Super Bowl halftime show.

But is anyone actually trading these event contracts? Let’s use data from publicly traded brokers that offer prediction markets to explore this.

In Q3 2026, Robinhood’s revenue from event contracts rose more than 600% year over year to $173 million, while the number of event contracts traded grew more than sevenfold to 16.4 billion[2]Robinhood. Robinhood Reports Third Quarter 2026 Results. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources . This was the second-highest source of transaction revenue behind options trading. In a forecast reported on by Cointelegraph, Bernstein analysts projected Robinhood’s prediction markets could drive $1.7 billion in annual revenue by 2028[3]Yahoo Finance. Bernstein Sees Robinhood Prediction Markets Reaching $1.7 Billion in Revenue by 2028, Topping Crypto. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources .

Coinbase's Q1 2026 quarterly report called prediction markets “one of its fastest-scaling products ever, driven by strong retail interest[4]Coinbase. Coinbase Q1 Financial Results Show Resilient Financial Performance Driven by New All-Time High Crypto Trading Volume Market Share. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources ."

In a call discussing Webull’s Q2 results, U.S. CEO Anthony Denier noted trading volume in Webull’s nascent prediction market product grew about 70% quarter over quarter[5]Gloom. Webull (BULL) Q2 2026 earnings call transcript. Accessed Nov 3, 2026. View all sources .

Gemini’s Q2 report shows prediction market volume rose 93% quarter over quarter[6]Gemini. Shareholder Update Q2 2026. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources .

So, now that we’ve established that these markets are growing in popularity — and investing platforms appear to have a large incentive to lean into them — how are all those event contract traders faring? That brings us to our final point.

Is all this even worth it? Several studies show how hard it is to actually turn a profit in prediction markets. Here are a few:

One study, conducted by researchers at ESSEC Business School, HEC Montréal and the University of Toronto's Rotman School of Management, covered 2.4 million Polymarket accounts across $67 billion in trading volume between November 2022 and March 2026, and found that 69% of users lost money[7]SSRN. Who Wins and Who Loses In Prediction Markets? Evidence from Polymarket. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources . Moreover, the top 1% of users who made a profit accounted for about 76% of all gains. Translation: Even if you did turn a profit, there’s a good chance you didn’t make much.

A Bloomberg analysis supports this: From January 2025 to April 2026, more than 100,000 Polymarket accounts lost at least $1,000[8]Bloomberg. Most Prediction Market Traders Are Losing Money While Bots Rack Up Gains. Accessed Nov 3, 2026. View all sources . Of the 2 million active wallets studied, almost half made or lost less than $10 during the period, while far fewer accounts collected larger profits.

A separate Wall Street Journal analysis echoed the same trend: 67% of Polymarket profits go to 0.1% of accounts, and more than 70% of Polymarket users lost money[9]The Wall Street Journal. Why Almost Everyone Loses—Except a Few Sharks—on Prediction Markets. Accessed Nov 3, 2026. View all sources .

While there is far less data available on Kalshi trades (Polymarket runs on a public blockchain, making it easy to extract and analyze data), a Kalshi spokesperson confirmed in the Wall Street Journal analysis above that one month of data in 2026 showed there were 2.9 unprofitable users to each profitable user[10]Wall Street Journal. Why Almost Everyone Loses—Except a Few Sharks—on Prediction Markets. Accessed Nov 10, 2026. View all sources .

The first study above discusses how event contract trading is binary and zero-sum: If one trader wins, another loses. This means traders allocating money to these instruments could be missing out on aspects of equity markets that have helped create long-term wealth for previous generations.

“Unlike equity markets, there is no equity premium, no dividends, and no passive investment options. Their short maturities naturally encourage short-horizon trading, which is precisely what the behavioral finance literature has suggested is most destructive to wealth accumulation. Given the rapid growth of these platforms and their role as a potential entry point for young investors, understanding who bears the losses has direct implications for investor welfare and market regulation.”

As a reminder, the vast majority of Polymarket users bore the losses.

At NerdWallet, our mission has always been to help people make smarter financial decisions, so we’re sticking by that. Starting with our 2027 ratings, brokers that offer easy access to sports and entertainment event contracts will lose points in our scoring system because we believe this new product has the potential to harm long-term wealth building and doesn’t belong in investing platforms.

So, what’s a better way forward? Bring back the friction and make prediction markets a separate app. Financial institutions, businesses and individuals can still explore legitimate use cases for prediction markets, such as forecasting and hedging. And we’re not here to stop anyone from trading event contracts for entertainment with extra money. We just don’t want these markets so easily conflated with the legitimate investing vehicles these apps already offer. And our reviews will reflect that.

#3066. Alphabet or CoreWeave: Which AI Stock Is Better to Buy in a Market Crash?

Alphabet and CoreWeave are both positioned to benefit from AI, but one has a much stronger financial foundation.

A stock market crash can create opportunities to buy great businesses at discounted prices. But in the artificial intelligence boom, which stock should investors consider: Alphabet (GOOGL -0.27%) (GOOG -0.38%) or CoreWeave (CRWV -0.51%)?

Both companies stand to benefit from growing demand for AI. However, they offer very different investment opportunities.

Alphabet owns some of the world's most valuable technology businesses and is investing heavily in AI. CoreWeave provides the computing infrastructure needed to develop and run AI models.

If I could choose only one during a market crash, I would favor Alphabet. It offers exposure to AI through several established businesses, providing investors with a stronger financial foundation amid uncertain economic conditions.

Alphabet is more than a search engine company. Google Search, YouTube, and Google Cloud provide several revenue streams. These businesses also give Alphabet an enormous customer base through which it can distribute AI products and services.

The company's latest results demonstrate this strength. In the third quarter of 2026, revenue increased 25% year over year to $127.6 billion. Operating income rose 30% to $40.6 billion, while Google Cloud revenue more than doubled to $30.7 billion.

The important point is that Alphabet isn't relying entirely on AI to grow. Google Search and YouTube already generate substantial revenue, while Google Cloud benefits as businesses adopt AI. This gives Alphabet several ways to participate in the AI opportunity. It can monetize AI through advertising, cloud computing, subscriptions, and its own AI models.

There are risks, of course. AI could change how people search for information, potentially challenging Google's traditional advertising business. Alphabet is also spending heavily on data centers and computing infrastructure, which could put pressure on cash flow if returns disappoint.

Nevertheless, its established businesses provide a financial cushion that many smaller AI companies lack.

CoreWeave is a different kind of AI investment. Instead of developing consumer products or search engines, it provides specialized cloud infrastructure that allows businesses to train and run AI models.

As AI adoption grows, demand for computing power could increase substantially. CoreWeave wants to capture a portion of that demand. Its second -quarter 2026 results were impressive. Revenue increased 112% year over year to $2.6 billion, while its revenue backlog reached approximately $104 billion. That backlog suggests strong customer demand, although it represents future contracted business rather than cash already received.

Still, the biggest problem is that building AI infrastructure requires enormous amounts of capital. CoreWeave's capital expenditures reached approximately $9.4 billion in the second quarter alone. It also recorded an adjusted net loss of $567 million. This creates a financial vulnerability.

If AI customers reduce spending or financing becomes more expensive, CoreWeave could struggle to fund its expansion. Its equipment also requires continual investment as newer generations of chips become available.

In short, the company could become a major winner if AI demand continues expanding. But investors must recognize that its growth depends on substantial capital investment and continued access to financing.

The difference between these companies becomes particularly important during an economic downturn. Imagine that businesses become more cautious about AI spending. Both companies could experience slower growth, but their financial positions would give them different levels of flexibility.

Alphabet has established businesses generating substantial operating profits. It can continue investing in AI even if demand temporarily weakens. CoreWeave has a more concentrated business model and much higher financing requirements relative to its scale. A slowdown could affect not only its growth prospects but also its ability to fund new infrastructure.

That doesn't mean CoreWeave will fail. Its enormous backlog and relationships with major customers demonstrate meaningful demand. But the company's financial risks are considerably higher.

For investors, this distinction matters. A market crash could make either stock cheaper. But the objective during a market crash shouldn't simply be to buy the stock that has fallen the most. It should be to identify businesses capable of emerging from the downturn stronger.

Both Alphabet and CoreWeave could benefit enormously from the AI revolution. But I would approach them differently. Alphabet is my choice for investors seeking long-term AI exposure with a stronger underlying business. CoreWeave is a more speculative opportunity for investors comfortable with substantial financial and execution risks.

A market crash could make either stock cheaper. But investors should still examine their valuations rather than assume a large share-price decline automatically creates a bargain. When investing in a promising industry, sometimes the best opportunity isn't the company with the most exciting growth. It's the company with the financial strength to keep winning when conditions become difficult.

#3063. Interactive Brokers Group vs. Mastercard: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Interactive Brokers trades at a lower forward P/E than Mastercard, but their balance sheets and margins tell very different stories.

Investors often weigh high-growth companies against established giants. Comparing Interactive Brokers Group (IBKR +0.03%) and Mastercard (MA +0.46%) reveals two different ways to potentially build wealth in the global financial markets.

Interactive Brokers Group focuses on low-cost execution for sophisticated, active traders and institutions around the world. Mastercard operates one of the largest payment networks, facilitating trillions of dollars in annual transaction volume. Both companies are central to modern finance, but they serve different roles for retail and institutional clients.

This company provides direct-access trade execution and clearing services for a diverse group of clients. It serves approximately 4.4 million cleared customer accounts, including hedge funds, mutual funds, and individual investors. Interactive Brokers focuses on automation and low costs to attract active participants as one of the leading financial stocks in the global market.

In fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $6.2 billion, representing growth of approximately 19.7% compared to the previous year. This performance helped the company generate net income of roughly $984 million. Interactive Brokers achieved a net margin of close to 15.9%, up from about 14.6% in the prior fiscal period, reflecting its ability to scale its low-cost model effectively.

According to its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is 0, indicating the company has no debt relative to its shareholders' equity. The current ratio, which measures the ability to pay short-term obligations with short-term assets, sits at approximately 1.1. Free cash flow for fiscal 2025 was nearly $15.7 billion, representing cash from operations minus capital expenditures.

This payments leader operates a technology-driven network that connects consumers, merchants, and financial institutions in more than 220 countries and territories. It earns fees by processing transactions and providing value-added services like data analytics and security. Mastercard relies on deep partnerships with banks and digital companies to facilitate its global reach.

In fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $32.8 billion, a 16.4% increase over the previous year. This robust top-line performance translated into net income of about $15 billion. The company maintained a high net margin of approximately 45.6%, which highlights its ability to convert a significant portion of every dollar earned into profit.

As of Mastercard's December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is roughly 2.5. The current ratio is approximately 1, indicating its short-term assets are roughly equal to its short-term liabilities. Free cash flow for the fiscal year ended in 2025 reached nearly $16.4 billion.

Interactive Brokers Group faces risks from its concentrated ownership structure, as Chairman Thomas Peterffy holds voting control. The business is heavily dependent on its proprietary technology and automation, making it vulnerable to technical failures or systemic market events. It also faces global regulatory scrutiny and competition from other digital brokerage platforms.

Interactive Brokers Group appears more attractively priced based on its forward P/E, which compares stock price to future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

How a stock has performed in the past does not necessarily predict how it will perform in the future. Still, I do think it's worth reviewing. Interactive Brokers and Mastercard went public almost exactly a year apart, in 2007 and 2006, respectively.

Mastercard shares have left both Interactive Brokers and the S&P 500 in the dust. The credit card giant also pays a modest dividend, which may appeal to some investors.

What I find most attractive about Mastercard is its high net margin. The ability to convert more than 45% of revenue into profits is impressive and would be the envy of most companies.

To be clear, I don't think Interactive Brokers is a bad stock. I just think the secular trend toward increasing use of digital payments is something Mastercard (and other payment specialists) will continue to benefit from. Because of these tailwinds, Mastercard gets my vote here.

#3062. Mortgage Rates Today, Tuesday, November 10: A Little Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Rates fell today, but not by enough to change your mortgage math.

Mortgage rates are a little lower today. It probably won't last, thanks to new inflation data that has investors even more confident that the Federal Reserve will hike rates next month. You can read more on that below the charts.

Today, the average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 6.9% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is eight basis points lower than yesterday and 17 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This morning the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) dropped the October Consumer Price Index, which shows that inflation rose another 0.4% last month. This outpaced expectations: Economists had forecasted an increase of 0.3%. CPI is one of two key measures of inflation, which the Federal Reserve aims to keep in check relative to the strength of the labor market. (The Fed looks more closely at a different inflation measure, the Personal Consumption Expenditures Index, which comes out Nov. 25.)

Raising the federal funds rate is the Fed’s main tool for bringing inflation down, and futures traders are banking on a rate hike when the committee meets Dec. 8-9. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but an anticipated rate hike can put upward pressure on mortgage rates, which respond to investors’ expectations for the economy and Fed policy.

Central bankers raised the federal funds rate in September, but it hasn’t been enough to pull inflation closer to the Fed’s target of 2%. Investors aren’t sure that one more hike in December will do the trick, either. According to CME FedWatch, further rate hikes may be in store for January.

» MORE: How the Federal Reserve affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.4% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

The rate you see advertised is a sample rate — usually for a borrower with perfect credit, making a big down payment, and paying for mortgage points. That won't match every buyer's circumstances.

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.
