#3061. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for their solid growth potential

Investors are closely monitoring earnings releases of artificial intelligence companies amid concerns about the durability of high spending and demand. While quarterly results provide key insights into a company's performance and execution, it is prudent to focus on long-term prospects and look beyond short-term fluctuations.

Top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick stocks with attractive long-term prospects as their assessments are backed by in-depth research of a company's fundamentals and growth opportunities.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

AI-powered data analytics provider Palantir Technologies is this week's first pick. The company reported market-beating second-quarter results. Moreover, Palantir raised its full-year guidance and now expects U.S. commercial business revenue to grow by at least 134%.

Following the Q2 print, Bank of America analyst Mariana Perez Mora reiterated a buy rating on PLTR stock with a price target of $255. Mora said that Palantir's performance reflects the success of its AI strategy, which focuses on delivering the right infrastructure to customers and ensuring better outcomes.

"The main driver continues to be the U.S. commercial business, which now represents almost 40% of total (vs. 30% a year ago and just 20% a couple of years back)," noted Mora.

The five-star analyst highlighted the 149% growth in Palantir's U.S. commercial business in Q2 2026 and the expansion in its customer base and engagement levels, with clients pursuing more integrated relationships. Mora noted that the U.S. commercial customer count increased 35% year-over-year to 653, with trailing twelve months revenue per customer surging 76% to $3.5 million in the second quarter.

Citing strength across Palantir's commercial and government segments, Mora increased her sales and earnings per share estimates for 2026-2028. The analyst attributed the increase in her estimates to the growth in the commercial business, backed by stronger total contract value in the second quarter.

Mora ranks No. 444 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 32.6%. See Palantir Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

E-commerce and cloud computing giant Amazon impressed investors with its strong second-quarter results. Revenue from the company's Amazon Web Services (AWS) cloud business surged 37%, marking the unit's fastest growth since 2021.

In reaction to the Q2 2026 performance, JPMorgan analyst Doug Anmuth reiterated a buy rating on Amazon stock and raised his price target to $365 from $330, saying that it remains the firm's Best Idea. Anmuth highlighted that the company's forex-neutral top-line growth accelerated to 20% in Q2 2026, the fastest in 20 quarters. He added that the top line gained from acceleration across both AWS and stores.

The five-star analyst noted that AWS growth of 37% was the fastest in 18 quarters, with backlog jumping nearly 2.5x year-over-year and 36% sequentially to $496 billion. Amazon's backlog gained from robust demand across both core workloads and AI adoption. Anmuth also observed the strength in AWS' AI and chips businesses, Graviton revenue commitments, and AWS' core business.

Notably, Anmuth believes that the core AWS business is highly correlated to AI revenue, and he expects further strengthening of this relationship as more AI workloads move into full-scale production and fuel higher demand. Overall, the analyst is more optimistic on Amazon following Q2 results and raised his 2026 and 2027 sales estimates by about 1% to 2% and operating income by about 5% to 7%.

"Importantly, management emphasized that the [return on invested capital] on its AI investments is compelling and it has a clear line-of-sight to strong financial returns," said Anmuth.

Anmuth ranks No. 717 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.5%. See Amazon Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Lam Research, a supplier of semiconductor manufacturing equipment and services. Thanks to AI-driven demand, the company delivered better-than-anticipated fiscal fourth quarter results.

In reaction to the solid results, Oppenheimer analyst Edward Yang reaffirmed a buy rating on LRCX stock with a price target of $400. Yang stated that Lam Research exceeded June-quarter expectations despite the high bar, driven by strong performance of the Customer Support Business Group and doubling of NAND revenue from the prior quarter. He also cited the improvement in Q4 FY26 gross margin.

The five-star analyst highlighted that Lam Research's September-quarter revenue and EPS outlook exceeded the Street's expectations by mid-teens percentages. Yang added that the company increased its wafer fabrication equipment spending outlook to the low-$150 billion range, up from its prior $140 billion estimate.

Importantly, Yang noted that Lam Research called calendar year 2027 "extraordinary," citing persistent supply shortages and the addition of 8 to 10 new fabrication plants. The analyst raised his 2027 revenue and EPS estimates by 7% and 9% to $33 billion and $8.88, respectively. Yang also increased his 2028 revenue and EPS estimates by 7% and 8% to $36.6 billion and $9.89, respectively.

"It's the cleanest way to play AI-driven 3D scaling across memory, foundry/logic, and packaging," said Yang about LRCX.

Yang ranks No. 256 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 56.4%. See Lam Research Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#3059. If I Were in My 20s, I'd Buy This AI ETF After Its Recent Crash and Hold It Forever

Adding artificial intelligence stocks to a diversified portfolio can supercharge its returns over the long run.

Buying an exchange-traded fund (ETF) that tracks a diversified index like the S&P 500 is one of the surest ways to build wealth in the stock market over the long term. It's a strategy every investor should consider, no matter their age.

However, young investors in their 20s have time on their side, so they might be comfortable with a little more volatility in exchange for the opportunity to earn even higher returns. Having more exposure to companies developing revolutionary technologies like artificial intelligence (AI) could supercharge their portfolio over the long term.

The Roundhill Generative AI and Technology ETF (CHAT -1.12%) exclusively invests in the world's top AI stocks. It's down 35% from its June all-time high (as of the market close on Nov. 6), and if I were in my 20s today, here's why I'd take this opportunity to add it to my diversified portfolio.

The Roundhill Generative AI and Technology ETF is actively managed, which means rather than simply tracking an index, a team of professional portfolio managers regularly buy and sell stocks based on what they believe will produce the best returns. But they adhere to a criteria, investing only in companies developing the hardware infrastructure, software, and platforms powering the AI revolution.

The Roundhill ETF currently holds just 36 stocks, and it's quite top-heavy, because its five largest positions represent 31.5% of the value of its entire portfolio.

Data source: Roundhill Investments. Portfolio weightings are accurate as of Nov. 8, 2026, and are subject to change.

Nvidia is the world's largest semiconductor company. Its graphics processing units (GPUs) for the data center are the best chips in the industry for processing AI workloads, and it continues to experience more demand than it can supply. In fact, chief executive Jensen Huang says every frontier AI model company plans to adopt the new Vera Rubin GPU systems.

Alphabet, Microsoft, and Amazon operate the three largest cloud computing platforms in the world, where they give enterprise customers access to data center capacity, ready-made foundation models, and other tools that are essential for deploying AI software. Each of these companies also developed its own AI assistant, which it integrated into legacy software systems to unlock new revenue streams.

Meta Platforms is the parent company of the Facebook, Instagram, and WhatsApp social media applications. However, it launched a personal AI assistant called Muse in September which already has over 10 million users. It can do everything from managing the user's emails to handling their shopping, so it could significantly expand Meta's addressable market beyond social media.

Some of the other AI powerhouses in the Roundhill ETF include Advanced Micro Devices, Micron Technology, CrowdStrike, and Elon Musk's Space Exploration Technologies.

The Roundhill Generative AI and Technology ETF was established in May 2023, so it doesn't have a very long track record. However, even after losing one-third of its value over the last few months, the ETF has still returned 160% since its inception, more than twice the 75% return of the S&P 500 over the same period.

I think it will trend higher over the long term because the AI boom is still in its infancy. While chips and data centers are the primary drivers of value right now, technologies like robotics and autonomous vehicles could be orders of magnitude larger in the future. But given its highly concentrated exposure to the AI industry alone, investors shouldn't bet the farm on the Roundhill ETF.

Had an investor parked $20,000 in an S&P 500 index fund in May 2023, they would have roughly $35,000 today. But had they put 70% of that money in the S&P and the other 30% in the Roundhill ETF, they would be sitting on $40,100 today instead. That demonstrates how an investor can use the ETF alongside a traditional index fund to supercharge their returns.

But remember, that strategy will come with added volatility. Not every investor would be comfortable with seeing a particular asset decline by 35% in just a few months, so while I would buy this ETF with the intention of holding it forever if I were in my 20s, it certainly isn't for everyone.

#3055. Car Insurance Soars for Drivers With Poor Credit; Here’s What to Do

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

While building your credit, shop around for auto insurance quotes. Some insurers are more lenient than others.

Lead Writer & Licensed Insurance Specialist

Your driving record may be spotless, but if you have poor credit, car insurance companies view you as a risk — and they’re significantly raising your rates.

Over the past three years, drivers with poor credit have seen auto insurance premiums surge 37.7%, according to NerdWallet’s 2026 Auto Insurance Rates Report. These price hikes were even steeper than those faced by drivers with good credit who caused a crash or got a DUI.

In January 2023, someone with poor credit and a clean driving record would pay a median of $2,864 per year for full-coverage car insurance, NerdWallet’s analysis found. By January 2026, premiums for this driver had risen to $3,944 — an increase of $1,081 per year. By comparison, the same driver with good credit saw an increase of only $576.

“Credit scores are one of the most harmful things that results in you paying higher auto insurance premiums,” says Michael DeLong, a research and advocacy associate with the Washington, D.C.-based nonprofit Consumer Federation of America. “The penalties are extremely severe.”

While higher premiums after a wreck or DUI may not be a surprise, DeLong says many drivers aren’t aware their credit scores will affect their car insurance rates. To further complicate things, building credit can take months.

But there are ways to get relief more quickly. DeLong suggests comparing car insurance quotes from multiple companies, because some insurers penalize poor credit less than others. Raising your deductible, evaluating your coverage and avoiding small claims can also help.

NerdWallet’s analysis found that insurers raised rates more sharply from 2023 to 2026 for drivers with poor credit than for those with good credit — even if the poor-credit driver had a flawless record while the good-credit driver had a DUI, speeding ticket or at-fault crash.

When our sample driver with poor credit also had a recent DUI or speeding ticket, rate increases were even greater. Prices for a full-coverage policy went up by more than 40% for drivers with poor credit and a recent DUI, the biggest increase of any profile we analyzed.

Drivers often think it’s unfair for credit to affect car insurance prices, DeLong says, since it isn’t directly linked to driving behavior. Some states agree; California, Hawaii and Massachusetts don’t allow credit as a factor in setting auto insurance rates.

However, insurers say credit scores can indicate the likelihood that a driver will file a claim.[1]National Association of Insurance Commissioners. Credit-Based Insurance Scores. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources They use what’s called a credit-based insurance score — similar to the typical credit scores used by lenders but weighted differently.

“It helps in predicting expected claims activity and loss costs more accurately,” said Norman Niami, a member of the American Academy of Actuaries' Automobile Insurance Committee, in an email.

The practice has been controversial, with critics arguing it disproportionately hurts low-income and minority drivers. But a Federal Trade Commission report to Congress in July 2007 concluded that credit-based insurance scores were strong predictors of risk even when controlled for income, race and ethnicity.[2]A Report to Congress by the Federal Trade Commission. Credit-Based Insurance Scores: Impacts on Consumers of Automobile Insurance. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources

The explanation may be that some people simply tend to take risks, according to a statistical analysis published in the North American Actuarial Journal in 2016.[3]North American Actuarial Journal. Empirical Evidence on the Use of Credit Scoring for Predicting Insurance Losses with Psycho-social and Biochemical Explanations. Accessed Nov 2, 2026. View all sources Researchers concluded that risk-taking behavior can affect credit scores as well as insurance claims. Their analysis found that average losses per policy were 64.6% higher for drivers with the lowest credit scores than for those with the best credit.

Working to build your credit over time may help lower your car insurance premiums, but it’s not a sure thing. A credit-based insurance score is just one of many factors (along with things like location, age and driving history) that insurers use to rate a person’s risk.

Building credit “can produce a noticeable premium decrease in some cases, while producing little or no change in others,” Niami said, depending on whether the new score causes an insurer to move you into a better rating tier. Each insurer sets its own score thresholds, so a score viewed as “poor” by one insurer might be considered “fair” by another.

At the same time, consumer advocates argue it’s difficult for many people to take the steps needed to strengthen their credit scores.

“Most people probably have some idea that you can improve your credit score by trying to pay your bills regularly and reliably, and using different kinds of financial resources and credit cards,” DeLong says.

“But again, that’s easier said than done," he says. "If you’re struggling at a low-wage job and trying to juggle bills, or if you don’t easily have access to a credit card, that advice may not be especially helpful."

Drivers with poor credit or no credit may be able to take other steps to lower their premiums.

Comparing car insurance quotes from different companies is one of the best ways to ensure you have the cheapest rate possible. Insurers set their own pricing formulas, and some are more forgiving of poor credit than others. A NerdWallet analysis of October 2026 rates found that in about half (49.3%) of U.S. ZIP codes in states that allow credit scoring, the cheapest insurer for a driver with poor credit was a different company than the cheapest for a driver with good credit.

Although you can’t improve your risk profile overnight, “there’s a bunch of small things you can do that over a two- to three-year span make you look a lot better” to insurers, says Bradley Flowers, founder of Portal Insurance agency in Mobile, Alabama.

Consider a higher deductible. Raising your car insurance deductible — the amount of a future claim that you agree to cover yourself — lowers your premium and signals to insurers that you’re less likely to file a claim. But be sure you can afford to pay the higher deductible if you have to make a claim.

Adjust how you’re billed by insurers. By signing up for automatic payments and ensuring you don’t miss insurance payments each month, you’ll get a small discount while improving your risk profile for insurers.

Avoid minor claims if you can. Flowers recommends thinking twice about filing minor claims, such as windshield cracks. “When you’re in one of these situations, let’s save our insurance for the really bad stuff,” he suggests.

Lastly, be sure your coverage fits your vehicle. If you could afford to replace your car on your own, consider dropping collision and comprehensive coverage to bring your premiums down. Your trimmed-down policy would still provide you with liability coverage after a crash, while leaving you to cover damage to your vehicle.

NerdWallet's 2026 Auto Insurance Rates Report is based on an analysis of more than 1.4 billion rate estimates provided by Quadrant Information Services for all 50 states and Washington, D.C., from January 2023 to January 2026.

Quadrant derives its rate data from modeled estimates based on carrier rating algorithms; figures do not reflect every available carrier or every consumer's individual rate. Liberty Mutual does not participate in Quadrant's rate data and is not represented in this analysis.

Quadrant’s figures are modeled rate estimates produced by running a fixed driver profile through carrier rating algorithms. They represent both new business and renewal rate estimates, and they are not a record of what actual drivers paid.

NerdWallet anchored its analysis to a 3-year old Toyota Camry LE, ensuring each analysis period reflected rates for the same-aged car.

The primary rate analysis is based on a 35-year-old single driver with good credit, no recent at-fault accidents or driving violations, and a full-coverage policy with $1,000 deductibles for collision and comprehensive coverage. Rates are pooled across male and female drivers.

In states where drivers must carry other types of insurance not included in our full coverage profile specifications, we added the minimum amount of coverage required by those states. Some policies include additional coverage at the insurer’s discretion.

In addition to our base profile driver with good credit and a spotless driving history, we analyzed rates for the following driver profiles:

Drivers with an at-fault crash. We added a single at-fault crash causing $10,000 in property damage.

Drivers with a speeding ticket. We added a single speeding ticket for driving 16 mph over the speed limit.

Drivers with a DUI. We added a single drunken-driving violation.

Drivers with poor credit. This is the same as our regular base profile, except the driver has poor credit instead of good credit. We also analyzed rates for drivers with poor credit and each of the three incidents above.

NerdWallet applied a strict comparability filter before calculating year-over-year figures. Only coverage variants and carrier/state combinations present in all four years of data were included in median calculations. This ensures reported changes reflect genuine pricing shifts among stable carriers, rather than differences caused by insurers entering markets, exiting, or going insolvent during the analysis period.

#3046. Breakfast News: Unity Software – Measure Twice!

Why a Unity buyout deserves a higher valuation, CoreWeave shrugs off debt jitters, and more…

Silver Lake and Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund have reportedly held talks to acquire Unity Software (U -2.12%), and we understand why.We first recommended Unity ("The U") in Hidden Gems at $17.09 in September 2024. We've made 22 additional recommendations and investments since then and have not sold a share. Today, the stock is at $50.34.Let's remind ourselves what Unity Software does and then consider the buyout together.

Unity makes the software that developers use to build video games, especially on phones. It also runs an ad business that helps those games find players.

CEO Matt Bromberg (a throwback all the way to America Online in 1999 – Motley Fool memories!) joined in May 2024 and inherited a company at war with those developers. He fixed that first, scrapping the controversial runtime fee and winning them back. He then rebuilt the company's ad business around Vector, an AI engine.Last quarter, these efforts produced Unity's first GAAP profit since going public in 2020, on top of 32% revenue growth. Its Vector ad engine grew 126% year over year.

Vector's edge comes from data. Roughly three billion people play Unity-built games each month, and what happens inside those games now trains Vector's AI. More than 90% of new Unity projects opt in to sharing in-game data. Few rivals see inside the game itself. That advantage is a multiplier.

And that's what attracts this investor consortium. It already owns major gaming businesses like Electronic Arts and Scopely. Owning Unity could deepen that integration, improving game development, player acquisitions, and advertising profitability across the broader portfolio.Silver Lake has an excellent reputation as a long-term partner. The firm made its first investment into Unity Software in 2017, and its CEO Egon Durban joined the company's board then as well. (Of course, that board seat also complicates this negotiation.)Early reports suggest a first valuation of $25 billion (around $55 per share). To our eye, that doesn't adequately reflect the value emerging in Unity's advertising business.What buyout price would we support? $75.04 per share.Vector is taking market share in a competitive environment, and its existing advertising customers are increasing their spend. Unity Software has a path to generating gross margins in the mid-80s percent alongside returns on invested capital in the mid-teens.

You could, therefore, in just a few years, find this company driving double-digit top line growth with rising margins and capital efficiency, generating over $2 billion in free cash flow. Our ballpark $33 billion price is more valid than the aforementioned $25 billion.And as long-term business owners in Fooldom, we're very happy to keep holding our shares in an independent U!

The artificial intelligence (AI) trade just got two very different signals. CoreWeave’s (CRWV -0.51%) debt-fueled expansion, a brutal sell-off in its stock and bonds, and questions about whether AI demand can justify the spending rattled investors. The AI cloud provider’s latest earnings offered some reassurance, sending Nasdaq futures up 1.4% this morning. CoreWeave popped over 10% in pre-market trading.

But there's a wrinkle: while demand for AI computing remains red-hot, investors appear less willing to pay absolutely anything for a piece of the boom.

#3043. UnitedHealth Just Raised Guidance for the Third Time This Year. Where Will the Stock Be in 3 Years?

The health insurer's turnaround is lifting earnings. Here's what annual growth between 13% and 16% could mean for its stock by 2029.

UnitedHealth Group (UNH +1.89%) has raised its full-year earnings guidance for the third time this year as its turnaround gains momentum. And after a challenging stretch, its latest earnings report on Oct. 13 gave investors more reasons to be optimistic.

Now, with earnings showing signs of recovery, here's a look at where UnitedHealth could be over the next three years.

For much of the past two years, UnitedHealth Group has been dealing with operational setbacks, rising medical costs, and pressure on its Medicare Advantage business. But these days, it is beginning to show signs of a meaningful turnaround.

The clearest proof is its improving earnings outlook. When management initially issued its 2026 forecast in January, it expected adjusted earnings per share (EPS) of at least $17.75. That figure was raised to more than $18.25 following its first-quarter results, then increased to between $19.50 and $20 after the second quarter.

Then, in October, management raised its outlook for the third time this year, setting its adjusted EPS target to at least $20.50. More importantly, the upward revisions came alongside stronger operating performance, suggesting the company is making real improvements in its business.

Behind those gains, it has been working to simplify its operations, strengthen its leadership team, and refocus on its core U.S. healthcare business. The company has also been exiting certain international businesses, while making changes to its operating structure.

These moves suggest that management is focusing on areas where the company has stronger competitive advantages and better opportunities. Plus, UnitedHealth has another major advantage beyond its insurance operations.

Optum, the company's healthcare services and technology business, provides exposure to several areas of the industry. That includes pharmacy services, healthcare technology, and direct patient care. This diversification is important because it gives the company more ways to generate earnings beyond collecting insurance premiums.

Here's how it has shown up in the numbers. In the third quarter, Optum Health generated $735 million in operating earnings, compared with just $41 million in the same period last year. That translated to its operating margin improving to 3.2% from 0.2%, reflecting better medical cost management.

Optum Insight is another area worth watching as the business provides technology, analytics, and administrative solutions to healthcare providers and insurers. In fact, its artificial intelligence (AI) enabled products could become a strong growth driver as healthcare organizations seek to improve efficiency. Management said it remained on track to invest nearly $1.5 billion in AI-related initiatives in 2026, with applications aimed at improving productivity and making healthcare easier to navigate.

Looking ahead, with its turnaround gaining traction, the question is how much earnings growth it can deliver through 2029.

Management said its 2027 earnings growth target starts at the lower end of its longer-term target range of 13% to 16%. From there, assuming a 2026 adjusted EPS of $20.50 based on the company's latest guidance, these growth targets could lift it to between about $29.60 and $32 by 2029. Shares closed Nov. 9 at $397.62, a forward price-to-earnings ratio (P/E) of about 17.

In a more conservative scenario, UnitedHealth could reach around $444 per share, assuming a 15 P/E multiple. That reflects 13% annual growth, or $29.60 in adjusted EPS, and implies roughly 12% upside.

Meanwhile, the base case puts the stock at $554, using an 18 multiple. It reflects 14.5% annual growth, or $30.80 in adjusted EPS, and implies roughly 40% upside. The 18 multiple roughly matches today's forward P/E ratio.

Lastly, if the company's initiatives exceed expectations, a 21 multiple could push shares to around $672, reflecting 16% annual growth, or $32 in adjusted EPS, implying roughly 70% upside.

Despite the improving outlook, it still faces regulatory scrutiny, including investigations into its Medicare Advantage practices. The company is also exposed to rising medical costs and changes in government reimbursement.

Even so, UnitedHealth is clearly making progress, and its third guidance increase this year suggests its turnaround is accelerating. The next three years could be rewarding for investors if earnings continue to climb and the company delivers on its plans.

#3038. Black Rock Coffee Bar: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Nearly all store leaders were promoted from within, a pipeline management credits for keeping turnover low as the chain expands.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, Black Rock Coffee Bar (BRCB -4.78%) announced that revenue grew 30% to $67 million. Sustained customer traffic and rapid store expansion show that the coffee chain's operating model is scaling efficiently into new geographic regions. Three things management said stand out.

Expanding physical footprint is the main engine driving multiyear revenue growth. By accelerating store openings while maintaining high store profitability, the business expands its overall market presence.

Expanding sales beyond morning hours boosts overall store productivity and revenue per location. Fresh food additions and extended operating hours create new reasons for customers to visit later in the day.

Retaining experienced store managers helps maintain high operational standards and protects store profitability, which is near 30%. Promoting leaders from within secures a steady talent pipeline as new store locations open.

Customer traffic is building into the fourth quarter as guest engagement initiatives take hold. Drive-through convenience, flexible site design, and high store-level profit margins position the business to capture white space in new markets while management tracks store build-out costs.

Store location portability allows entry into diverse geographic areas through both new builds and site conversions.

#3030. Grocery Outlet: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Slowing new store openings in favor of infill sites is a bet that the company can grow profitably without leaning on promotions.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, Grocery Outlet (GO +1.08%) announced a return to positive comparable store sales, which measure sales at stores open at least a year. The discount grocer is refocusing on its core model of buying deeply discounted surplus inventory to draw budget-conscious shoppers. Three things management said stand out for long-term investors.

Management phased out synthetic broad promotions and refocused on buying surplus brand-name products at deep discounts. That shift expanded customer visits to stores as inflation continued to squeeze household budgets.

After applying a targeted playbook to revitalize surplus grocery items, management repeated the process in frozen and deli. Replacing standard items with higher-margin opportunistic inventory accelerated sales growth across both departments.

To improve returns on capital expenditure, which means money spent on building stores, management is slowing store openings and prioritizing infill sites. Building within existing markets provides predictable returns and lowers execution risks.

Sales momentum accelerated entering the fourth quarter as customer traffic offset smaller basket sizes. Management is monitoring freight costs and maintenance expenses while leaning into established store footprints.

Management expects gross margins to expand year over year as higher-margin surplus deals replace artificial promotions. Capital allocation discipline will guide store expansion and supply chain efficiency into 2027.

#3029. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The stock market continues to grapple with volatility amid geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and concerns about the sustainability of the AI boom. Given this uncertainty, investors looking for steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick the right dividend stocks, as these experts assign their ratings after an in-depth analysis of a company's financials and ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Downstream energy company Phillips 66 (PSX) is this week's first dividend pick. With a quarterly dividend $1.27 per share (annualized dividend of $5.08), PSX offers a yield of 2.25%. The company recently reported solid Q2 earnings, as the Middle East conflict affected global supplies and drove up refining margins.

Following the Q2 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Phillips 66 and raised his price target to $255 from $240, reflecting higher 2026 earnings expectations and lower interest expense next year.

Gabelman highlighted the quarter-over-quarter reduction in Phillips 66's net debt, noting management's optimism at achieving its estimated $15.5 billion net debt target one year ahead of schedule. The 5-star analyst expects the company to end 2026 with a net debt of $14.6 billion.

"The [balance sheet] improvement could re-establish PSX as a go-to defensive refiner," said Gabelman.

Management agreed that PSX's payout ratio lagged year-to-date, but expects buybacks to increase in the second half. Gabelman said Phillps indicated the possibility of a larger dividend hike following an annual increase of 5% over the past two years.

Gabelman ranks No. 554 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Phillips 66 Statistics on TipRanks.

Crescent Energy (CRGY) is an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian and Uinta Basins. Earlier this month, the company reported better-than-expected Q2 earnings and announced a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share, payable on August 31. At an annualized dividend of $0.48 per share, CRGY boasts a dividend yield of about 4%.

In reaction to Q2 results, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reaffirmed a buy rating on Crescent Energy stock with a price target of $18. He noted continued strength in the company's performance, with second-quarter oil production and cash flow surpassing the Street's expectations.

"CRGY's cash flow exceeded expectations by 10%, reinforcing its trajectory of capital efficiency," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that Crescent raised its full-year oil production guidance, citing the effective integration of the Vital Energy acquisition. Specifically, Crescent tripled its synergy target from the Vital Energy deal to as much as $300 million, significantly reducing the effective purchase price. This reflects Crescent's solid execution following the acquisition, Richardson said.

Additionally, the analyst noted that Crescent's capital spending is and heading toward the lower end of management's prior guidance, indicating disciplined financial controls.

Richardson ranks No. 579 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.5%. See Crescent Energy Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Finally, Viper Energy (VNOM) is effectively controlled by Diamondback Energy. Viper owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests in oil-weighted basins, mainly the Permian in West Texas. Viper recently announced its Q2 2026 results and declared a 32% hike in its base dividend, effective in the third quarter of 2026. The new base dividend indicates a 4.5% annualized yield.

Viper also removed its quarterly commitment to return at least 75% of cash available for distribution. The company believes that this move will ensure increased flexibility to make opportunistic share repurchases and pursue accretive mergers and acquisitions.

Following the Q2 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper, slightly raising his price target to $59 from $58. Bilkoski said Viper's solid Q2 performance supports his view that the strength in oil prices and rising operator activity are driving higher production.

"Viper has delivered, and we forecast will continue to deliver, one of the highest production per share growth profiles in our royalty universe" through the end of 2027, said Bilkoski. He believes Viper deserves a premium valuation, given its above-average growth.

The 5-star analyst said that while the biggest surprise in the quarter was Viper's revision to its shareholder return framework, he doesn't believe that the move indicates a significant change to its underlying strategy. Under the new framework, a larger portion of excess free cash flow is expected to be allocated to share repurchases than to variable dividends, Bilkoski said.

Bilkoski ranks No. 719 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Viper Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#3026. Here's What $1,000 Invested in SpaceX Could Be Worth by the End of 2027

SpaceX's situation for the medium term looks complicated, to say the least.

In this inflation-riddled economy, $1,000 might not seem like a lot. But if that money is invested in the right company at the right time, it could create a foundation for life-changing returns over the long haul. Many investors are curious whether Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +0.72%) has what it takes to go the distance after its record-setting $1.77 trillion initial public offering in June.

SpaceX has a lot going for it. CEO Elon Musk is no stranger to innovation and proving his naysayers wrong. And the company's spectacular recent results suggest he hasn't lost his Midas touch.

That said, the company still faces significant uncertainty, especially considering its sky-high valuation and the changing political climate after the Democrats swept the midterm elections. So what might a $1,000 investment might be worth by the end of next year?

Usually, large companies offer investors relative stability at the expense of high growth. But SpaceX flips this dynamic on its head. The space and artificial intelligence giant is growing like a weed, with third-quarter revenues soaring by 161% year over year to $14.1 billion, driven by the company's artificial intelligence segment, where revenues rose 830%. The AI segment, which SpaceX now calls its "super intelligence" unit, makes most of its money by accumulating data center hardware and renting out compute to clients that need to run and train large language models (LLMs).

The company has secured several deals with major AI companies like Anthropic and Alphabet, which is paying SpaceX a whopping $920 million per month for access to AI compute capacity. That said, it's difficult to know how long these types of deals will last, because many of SpaceX's clients have an incentive to invest in building their own data centers (and designing their own chips) rather than relying on third-party solutions. And most of the deals can be cancelled with a few months notice by either party.

SpaceX's management seems to be aware of that problem, and the company aims to bolster its moat by deploying a constellation of data center satellites in Earth orbit. If the technology works, it could offer significant advantages over ground-based compute due to abundant and essentially free solar energy, easier cooling, and perhaps less regulatory pushback. That last point may be more important than ever considering recent political developments.

While most business leaders avoid partisan politics to avoid the risk of potential brand damage, Musk has taken a different approach. He went all-in for Republicans and President Donald Trump in both 2024 and again in the 2026 U.S. midterm election, when he spent $474 million to support GOP candidates, including a whopping $224 million in Texas alone, where SpaceX has its headquarters, as well as significant launch and manufacturing facilities.

Musk's campaign contributions are constitutionally protected free speech. However, investors would be naive to assume that his actions can't have negative consequences for his companies.

Every Musk-backed Republican lost their election. Meanwhile, the Democrats are promising to impose more regulations on data center construction, which could slow the pace of AI expansion and drive up costs. On the other side of the business, SpaceX's Texas launch operations have already faced significant pressure from environmental groups. These headwinds are likely to increase over the coming years.

While SpaceX is a rapidly growing business, it faces substantial challenges from political uncertainty. And trading at a price-to-sales multiple of 55 (compared to the S&P 500's average ratio of just 3.6), its speculative valuation doesn't leave much room for error.

The potential for a mega-merger with Musk's other public company, Tesla, could make the problem even worse by saddling the combined company with a stagnating electric vehicle business.

With all this in mind, I don't see an investment in SpaceX growing substantially over the next 14 months. A $1,000 position opened today will probably still be worth around $1,000 in a base case scenario.

#3020. Coinbase Global vs. Toast: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Coinbase's crypto exchange and Toast's restaurant software are growing fast, but their valuations tell very different stories for investors.

Choosing between Coinbase Global (COIN -2.92%) and Toast (TOST -0.72%) requires pitting high-growth digital assets against the steady digitization of the restaurant industry. Which path leads to better long-term portfolio growth for investors today?

Coinbase Global operates as a primary bridge between traditional finance and the crypto economy. Toast provides a comprehensive technology stack that manages everything from point-of-sale to payroll for restaurants. Both companies represent the shift toward digital-first financial services, making them frequent candidates for growth-oriented investors looking toward 2027 and beyond.

Coinbase Global generates revenue by facilitating the exchange of digital assets for retail and institutional clients. The company also offers subscription and services revenue through its partnership with Circle (CRCL -3.82%) for the creation and redemption of the USDC (USDC +0.01%) stablecoin. By providing secure storage and developer tools, it aims to be the primary gateway for the crypto economy.

In its latest annual report for fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $7.2 billion, representing growth of approximately 9.4% over the prior period. The company reported net income of close to $1.3 billion, resulting in a net margin of roughly 17.6%. This net margin reflects the portion of total revenue remaining as profit after all expenses are paid.

As of Coinbase's December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio, which compares total debt to shareholder equity to assess financial leverage, is approximately 0.5. The current ratio, which measures liquidity, is nearly 2.3, while free cash flow reached close to $2.4 billion in fiscal 2025. Note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 34.6% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation, since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Toast provides an integrated platform that serves as the operating system for the restaurant industry. The company offers software, payment processing hardware, and financial services to more than 170,000 locations. It often partners with third parties, such as Amazon (AMZN +1.48%), to maintain its cloud infrastructure and deliver services to small and medium-sized businesses. This is a common strategy among tech stocks that focus on vertical software solutions.

In its latest annual report for fiscal 2025, Toast reported revenue of nearly $6.2 billion, a growth rate of approximately 24.1%. The company achieved net income of close to $342 million, a significant swing from its historical performance. This resulted in a net margin of roughly 5.6% for the year.

Toast's balance sheet as of December 2025 shows a debt-to-equity ratio of 0. The current ratio is approximately 2.8, and free cash flow for fiscal 2025 was nearly $608 million. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 36.6% of operating cash flow.

Coinbase Global faces significant revenue concentration in Bitcoin (BTC -0.49%) and Ethereum (ETH -1.84%) trading, making its results highly sensitive to price volatility. The company is also navigating intensive regulatory scrutiny, including investigations by the New York Attorney General regarding marketing practices. Furthermore, operational risks like cybersecurity threats and platform outages could affect its brand and financial stability.

Toast carries risks related to its reliance on a single bank partner for its working capital loan programs. A lawsuit from the FTC alleging deceptive pricing practices also poses a threat to its reputation and bottom line. Additionally, the company is concentrated in the small business restaurant market, which is vulnerable to inflation and broader economic downturns. Toast also relies on Amazon for platform hosting, creating a dependency on the stability of third-party services.

Toast appears cheaper based on its forward P/E and P/S ratio. These reflect future earnings estimates and sales over the past 12 months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Coinbase Global starts at a disadvantage here because I am a crypto skeptic. Crypto has no inherent value -- it doesn't produce earnings or cash flow and has no real-world utility. Buying cryptocurrency as an investment is entirely a bet that someone will later buy it from you at a higher price.

Furthermore, Coinbase Global's fortunes are inseparable from those of crypto:

Look at how closely Coinbase's stock mimics the price movements of Bitcoin and Ethereum (except worse). To me, Coinbase seems like a second-order play on crypto, and not a very good one, to boot.

Toast has a more reasonable valuation and also recently flipped from reporting a loss to a profit. Its POS systems are ubiquitous in the restaurant industry; it feels like every restaurant either runs on Toast or Square nowadays. I don't think crypto is a legitimate investment, and Coinbase is inextricably linked to the crypto market. Toast gets my vote here.

#3003. Standard Chartered Sees 50x Gains Ahead for This Surging Crypto. Here's Why It Could Be Worth a Closer Look.

This under-the-radar cryptocurrency could be one way to get exposure to the asset tokenization trend.

Real-world asset (RWA) tokenization remains one of the fastest-growing market opportunities within the crypto industry. In turn, this rapid growth is opening up all sorts of new investment options for investors.

One of the most promising options is Arbitrum (ARB -2.98%), a cryptocurrency that launched in 2021. It's growing by leaps and bounds right now, and Standard Chartered predicts it will soar to $10 by 2030. Based on its current price of approximately $0.20, there's the potential for an eye-popping 50x gain.

The tokenized asset market is currently worth $340 billion, but Standard Chartered sees this number ballooning to $4 trillion by 2030. There will be a massive land grab, as traditional finance firms rush to bring their financial assets on-chain.

And that's where Arbitrum enters the picture. Standard Chartered is calling it "the blockchain for TradFi (traditional finance)" for good reason. Wall Street firms and financial institutions that want to bring traditional financial assets (such as stocks and bonds) on-chain will turn to Arbitrum, a Layer-2 blockchain built on Ethereum.

The good news here is that Arbitrum collects 10% of net protocol revenue. So the more firms that sign up for real-world asset tokenization initiatives, the more money it will collect. We've already seen this dynamic in action with Robinhood Markets, which recently launched Robinhood Chain in partnership with Arbitrum.

The launch of Robinhood Chain immediately led to a surge in transaction volume, and Arbitrum has been making money hand over fist as a result. In September alone, Arbitrum pocketed more than $5 million in fees. It's gotten to the point where some investors are now talking about the "Robinhood Chain Effect." Other finance firms could follow Robinhood's lead, creating even more revenue opportunities for Arbitrum.

This might sound like a fantastic business model, but a lot can still go wrong. For example, the pace of real-world asset tokenization may cool off, which will directly impact Arbitrum's future growth prospects.

Moreover, Arbitrum has plenty of potential rivals. Its nearest competitor is Base, the Layer-2 blockchain from Coinbase Global. So it's not like Arbitrum has a monopoly on real-world asset tokenization.

Finally, it's still unclear whether investors prefer investing in Layer-1 blockchains, such as Ethereum, or in Layer-2 blockchains, such as Arbitrum. Currently, all the value is flowing to Layer-2 blockchains, but that could change.

Investors can pick up ARB at an unbelievable 92% discount to its all-time high of $2.40. Even if Arbitrum doesn't reach $10 by 2030, it could still deliver 10x gains by reclaiming the $2 price level. That type of upside potential should make Arbitrum worth a closer look heading into 2027.

#2991. Ray Dalio says Bessent move is sign that a debt crisis is getting closer; recommends gold and bitcoin

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's debt buyback announcement this week fits into a broader pattern that can signal a forthcoming crisis, according to billionaire investor Ray Dalio.

The Bridgewater Associates founder said Bessent's plan to increase government debt purchases may portend trouble for the U.S. economy. Coupled with the Japanese government reducing its U.S. bond market exposure and surging long-dated American bond yields, Dalio said investors may want to prepare their portfolios for increased risk by owning cryptocurrencies and gold.

"I am confident that the government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote in a LinkedIn post published Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Dalio noted that the Treasury Department has "only limited capacity" to buy back bonds. Bessent told CNBC on Thursday that his team was going to "make a market" and that the purchases would likely top $4 billion.

The U.S. is spending about 40% more than it's bringing in, resulting in the burgeoning budget deficit, Dalio said. The U.S. budget deficit topped $432 billion in July, but Bessent told CNBC that it's likely peaked under President Donald Trump's administration.

Bessent said in the interview that a team was looking at ways to shrink spending by hundreds of billions of dollars. But Dalio said there is "very little ability" to do so given that it is either committed or considered essential.

After years of overspending, Dalio said total debt now dwarfs what the U.S. brings in each year. If the U.S. government was a business, Dalio said debt service payments would come in at roughly $11 trillion — about 200% of annual revenue.

Dalio, 77, warned the cost of repaying principal and servicing the debt will only grow over time.

To solve this, Dalio said the U.S. needs to carefully employ a three-part strategy to get the budget deficit down to 3% of gross domestic product.

First, Dalio said the U.S. government needs to reduce its spending. Second, he said tax revenue needs to be raised. Finally, the New York native and Harvard Business School alumni said lower interest rates were needed.

"All three need to happen concurrently so as to prevent any one from being too large," Dalio said. "If any one is too large, the adjustment will be traumatic."

Dalio cautioned against ramming through these adjustments by "force." For instance, "it would be very bad if the Federal Reserve unnaturally forced interest rates down," he said.

Dalio said it's important to to take steps now, while the economy is healthy. An economy in recession requires increased government spending, he said.

Dalio said the exact timing of a debt crisis can be swayed by variables ranging from military conflict to political change. On its current trajectory, the U.S. could enter such a crisis in as early as one year or as late as five. "My guess, which I suppose will be a bad one, is that it will come in three years, give or take two, if the course we're on is not changed."

To prepare, Dalio recommended investors are underweight debt assets, such as bonds.

As much as 10% to 15% of a portfolio could land in gold, as well as "a bit" of bitcoin, the hedge fund founder said.

Dalio's post came at the tail end of a volatile week for U.S. financial markets. Rising long-term Treasury yields have pressured stocks, leading the S&P 500 to snap a a three-week-long advance.

#2990. StubHub: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Rapid deleveraging cut net leverage from 4.5 to 2.5 times EBITDA, but the open distribution push is still unproven at scale.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, StubHub (STUB -3.42%) announced an increase in its full-year gross merchandise sales, representing the total dollar value of tickets sold on its platform, guidance to 11% to 13% growth. Operating efficiency drove adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) margin up 10 percentage points year over year to 25%. Debt repayments of $1.1 billion since its initial public offering have lowered borrowing costs and strengthened its balance sheet. Three takeaways from management's comments stand out.

Sales and marketing costs fell nine percentage points year over year to 45% of revenue in the third quarter. Management attributes this efficiency to established market leadership, allowing it to capture repeat customer traffic without spending heavily on acquiring buyers.

Net leverage dropped from 4.5 times adjusted EBITDA at the end of 2025 to 2.5 times at the end of the third quarter of 2026. The company paid down $100 million of debt in July and extended term loan maturities to October 2033, creating balance sheet flexibility for strategic investments.

Management is expanding beyond traditional resale through open distribution, which allows primary ticket sellers and content owners to list inventory directly on the platform without exclusivity. Concurrently, the platform is testing sponsored listings, allowing sellers to pay for elevated visibility as event dates approach.

Live event demand remains steady across sports and music categories, and international concert travel continues to expand the customer base. StubHub benefits from network effects and a long operational history handling high-demand ticket fulfillment, while management watches the timing of major 2027 concert ticket releases and updates to search engine algorithms.

With leverage down and term loan maturities pushed out to October 2033, the company is shifting focus toward driving free cash flow per share over the long term.

#2984. Stock Market Sell-Off: The 5 Best Stocks to Buy in November

The strong business models of these five companies can offer peace of mind even amid broad sell-offs.

It's only a week into November, and the markets were off to a rough start, with the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.38%) falling 3.1% from Nov. 2 to Nov. 6. The reasons are plentiful, as investors weigh what might be next for the artificial intelligence (AI) trade after the elections, and whether December will feature a rate hike after a strong jobs report, showing the United States added 165,000 jobs in October compared to expectations of 65,000.

The stock market showed a bit of a rebound on Nov. 9, but those gains could still be short-lived, as plenty of uncertainty remains. And while no stock is immune to uncertainty, some can hold up better than others during sell-offs thanks to their durable business models. And unlike speculative stocks, these investments help investors achieve greater peace of mind, making it less likely they'll sell out of them during a downturn and miss out on a longer-term price run higher.

With the markets looking rocky to start November, these are the five stocks I would consider adding to a portfolio: Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA +2.60%) (BRKB +1.41%), Costco Wholesale (COST +0.00%), Walmart (WMT -0.09%), Coca-Cola (KO +0.92%), and Monster Beverage (MNST -0.66%).

New CEO Greg Abel has quickly made his mark on Berkshire. With less than a year in the role, Berkshire has acquired the homebuilder Taylor Morrison, loaded up on Alphabet stock, and built an 8.9% stake in Bending Spoons, an Italian technology conglomerate that acquires other companies.

That said, even if some of the investments may look different or the pace of investing seems more aggressive, Berkshire is still an anchor for any portfolio. For its recent 2026 third-quarter earnings results, operating profits fell 5.8% to $12.7 billion from the prior-year period. Still, the key there is operating profits of $12.7 billion, as this company is an expert at having cash constantly flowing in. Also, its cash holdings increased to $377.4 billion for the quarter, giving it plenty of runway to stand steady during market turbulence.

Costco offers the kind of bulk deals people look for as shoppers increasingly try to stretch their budgets as far as possible. But it's the membership required to access those deals that gives Costco its strong foundation. Its members are also extremely loyal, with a 92.3% renewal rate in North America.

I also like its ability to reward long-term shareholders through three different mechanisms that work together to increase total returns. It's served as a growth stock over the last five years, with the stock price climbing 103.7%. In comparison, over the same period, the S&P 500 is up 57.5%.

The second mechanism is special dividends, which the company typically pays every few years; it last paid a special dividend of $15 per share in 2024. Finally, it pays a quarterly dividend that yields 0.5%.

Walmart has a similar appeal to Costco. It doesn't offer the same type of bulk deals, but it stands out in the crowded retail sector by attracting shoppers through its lower prices. And like Costco, it's even more handily outperformed the S&P 500 over the last five years, climbing 154.8%.

One thing that separates the two, however, is its greater leaning toward technology. While shopping at Costco largely involves visiting a warehouse, Walmart leans more into online sales, launching an AI shopping assistant, Sparky, that is showing positive results. It's also using drone deliveries and is even building out an advertising business, making its revenue sources even more robust.

Coca-Cola and Monster don't offer must-have products. But given the habits people form and the price points both companies offer for their products, most people aren't going to stop reaching for a soda or energy drink if that is part of their usual routine.

Both can fit into a single portfolio, though depending on risk tolerances and concentration risk, an investor may want to choose one over the other.

With Coca-Cola, you're getting a Dividend King, as it's increased its payouts for 50 or more consecutive years. To be exact, it has increased its payout for 64 consecutive years, and its dividend currently yields 2.2%.

Monster isn't fully a growth stock, as it is a more mature business. But it still has sales growth opportunities, including expanding its international presence and building out an alcoholic beverage segment. For the third quarter of 2026, international net sales totaled $1.2 billion, an increase of 28.1%.

#2980. Did the Midterms Just Put the AI Data Center Boom at Risk?

New government leaders could potentially put tighter regulations around AI development.

Data centers have existed for some time to store digital data for cloud computing, fintech, and more. But they've gone into overdrive to supply the compute needed to fuel artificial intelligence (AI) development, with substantial power required for the training and inference that enable AI to respond quickly and accurately to queries and commands.

Companies have been building more data centers to keep development going, but not everyone is on board. People whose neighborhoods are seeing data centers go up are complaining about the rising energy costs that come with them, and with Democrats taking over Congress and several state governorships, the build-out might be at risk. Here's why, and how it impacts investors.

Forecasted data center revenue for this year is $572 billion, according to Statista, and according to the World Economic Forum, global data center investment could reach $7 trillion by 2030.

However, new regulations may impede further growth in the near term. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has pledged to halt data center development until the government brings residents to the table to hear their concerns. Texas accounts for 25% to 30% of planned U.S. data centers, more than any other state, according to banking data compiled by tech analyst Dan Ives. There are new Democratic governors in Ohio, Georgia, and Michigan as well, all states with high data center potential, and they all plan to follow her lead.

Hyperscalers like Amazon (AMZN +1.48%), Microsoft (MSFT -2.21%), Meta Platforms (META +0.00%), and Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +0.72%) are building the data centers, and SpaceX is even renting out its compute power to competitors like Anthropic and Alphabet (GOOG -0.38%)(GOOGL -0.27%).

These companies weren't too adversely affected by the tech sell-off last week, since they're already the leaders in the area and have compute power to drive their businesses right now. The consensus is that even if some data center construction is halted, these companies already have ample compute power to drive development. Investors are also happy that these companies may be forced to slow down, since AI spend has skyrocketed.

Beyond the state-specific moratoriums, there could be tighter safety and environmental protocols that data center builders must meet, which could increase costs and lead to longer timelines. Several companies said they're going to get better at building bridges with local governments, and ChatGPT's parent company, OpenAI, said it's going to work with local residents to get their input and explain how data center development could benefit their neighborhoods.

While the hyperscalers seem to have escaped negative market reaction, that wasn't the case for the data center chip suppliers like Nvidia (NVDA -5.47%) and Broadcom (AVGO +3.91%), whose stock prices fell after the elections. These "pick and shovel" stocks could slow down if data centers are built at a slower pace, and that could negatively impact their stocks in the near term.

#2977. Top Wall Street analysts believe in the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Geopolitical uncertainty in the Middle East and soaring bond yields weighed on global markets this past week. And investors are closely assessing the valuations of artificial intelligence stocks amid concerns about the sustainability of high spending on AI infrastructure.

Despite those continued challenges, top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the prospects of several stocks due to their long-run growth potential and exposure to lucrative business lines, including AI.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Cybersecurity company CrowdStrike Holdings (CRWD) is this week's first stock pick. The company is scheduled to announce fiscal second-quarter results on August 26.

Ahead of Q2 FY27 earnings, Truist analyst Junaid Siddiqui reiterated a buy rating on CrowdStrike stock and raised his price target to $245 from $187.50. Following CrowdStrike's impressive fiscal first-quarter performance and guidance raise, Siddiqui expects another strong quarter.

The 5-star analyst's optimism is backed by continued strength in Falcon Flex adoption and encouraging large deal activity. Siddiqui also cited continued momentum across emerging products. With the FY27 outlook assuming persistent strength in CrowdStrike's net new annual recurring revenue (ARR), Siddiqui is more focused on the longevity of key growth catalysts such as Falcon Flex adoption, platform consolidation and AI-led demand.

The analyst highlighted that talks with partners were encouraging, with pipeline generation and momentum in large deals remaining healthy in the fiscal second quarter despite tough, year-over-year comparisons.

"We continue to view CrowdStrike as one of the primary beneficiaries of platform consolidation, though we expect investors to remain focused on competitive dynamics and whether current growth levels represent a new baseline or a period of demand acceleration," the analyst said.

Siddiqui ranks No. 226 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 80% of the time, delivering an average return of about 40%. See CrowdStrike Financials on TipRanks.

Shares of personal computer maker Dell Technologies (DELL) have rallied 251% this year, driven by strong demand for AI servers. Recently, Evercore analyst Amit Daryanani reiterated a buy rating on DELL and raised his price target to $550 from $500.

The 5-star analyst contends that while Dell's AI compute segment is getting investors' attention, the company's Storage business is underappreciated and well-positioned to drive revenue and profit acceleration. Daryanani expects the storage business to see higher demand once enterprises start to bring workload back on-premises.

The analyst highlighted that Dell's Storage business, estimated to account for only 10% of FY27 revenue, is one of the factors that could help improve profit margins, given its higher profitability compared to AI servers. Daryanani estimates that the Storage business's operating margin is currently in the low-20% range, while margins for the AI server and traditional server businesses are in the mid-single-digit and mid-teens range, respectively.

Overall, Daryanani views the Storage business as an important catalyst for three reasons: Dell has one of the broadest storage portfolios in the industry; Storage has a significantly higher margin profile than AI servers; and the shift toward proprietary, software-defined storage and AI-focused platforms.

"We think DELL's storage business is becoming increasingly strategic as AI infrastructure deployments move beyond GPU procurement and toward full stack + AI-ready architectures," said Daryanani.

Daryanani ranks No. 19 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 38.70%. See Dell Technologies Statistics on TipRanks.

SanDisk (SNDK) is benefiting from AI-driven demand for its memory and storage products. Following the company's 2026 Investor Day event, J.P. Morgan analyst Harlan Sur resumed research coverage of SNDK with a buy rating (it was earlier, "Not Rated") and a price target of $2,250.

The 5-star analyst believes SanDisk is well-positioned to capitalize on a structural inflection reached in NAND demand, driven by strong growth in AI inference. Sur's bullishness is based on three key factors.

First, Sur believes that SanDisk's New Business Model (NBM) framework (or long-term agreements) has enhanced its margin profile and reduced cyclicality. SNDK's eight signed NBMs represent a total contract value of $94 billion and a gross margin of about 80%, even at the floor pricing.

Second, Sur highlighted the growing NAND market and expects the total addressable market to surge from about $70 billion in calendar year 2025 to over $300 billion in 2026 and about $500 billion in 2027. Importantly, the analyst noted that data centers are the largest driver of NAND growth, as hyperscalers increasingly deploy flash for AI inference workloads.

Finally, Sur is optimistic on SanDisk due to its technology dominance. The company's BiCS10 technology is sampling ahead of schedule and has 65% more bits per wafer than BiCS8, the analyst said. Also, Sur expects SanDisk's High Bandwidth Flash to become a differentiated memory platform for AI inference.

"Layered on top, mgmt's commitment to returning 100% of excess cash flow to shareholders should drive an aggressive share count reduction trajectory that further amplifies EPS growth on a per-share basis," said Sur.

Sur ranks No. 15 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 43.20%. See SanDisk Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2966. Believe it, Lakers are NBA championship contenders

Luka Dončić is playing as though he's on a revenge tour and every opposing player has former Mavericks general manager Nico Harrison's face superimposed on it.

Austin Reaves is playing as though he were shortchanged by signing a four-year, $180 million deal over the summer, the richest contract in NBA history for a player who went undrafted.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic (77) shoots against Atlanta Hawks guard CJ McCollum during an NBA game, Monday, Nov. 9, 2026, in Atlanta. AP Photo/Mike Stewart

Walker Kessler is playing as though he's hellbent on fulfilling his vow to "run through a wall" for the Lakers after they emptied their treasure trove of draft capital to acquire him in July.

And the Lakers' role players are playing as though they deeply understood the assignment when they joined a 17-time championship franchise.

The Lakers have the league's best record at 10-2. They've won six games in a row.

That isn't a sentence we thought we'd write heading into this season. Not with nine new players. Not with so many question marks around their perimeter defense. Not with their lack of depth at backup center.

But now, Lakers general manager Rob Pelinka looks like the Albert Einstein of basketball rosters and coach JJ Redick looks like a superstar whisperer.

The Lakers last won a championship in 2020, their first title in 10 years.

They could snap their six-year drought this season.

Dončić is on a historic tear, averaging 39.3 points (49.8% shooting from the field, 40.7% from beyond the arc), 7.8 rebounds, 7.9 assists and two steals per game.

In his first nine games of the season, Dončić scored 361 points, surpassing Michael Jordan's 347 points in 1986-87 for the most points scored through that period in modern NBA history.

But what really made the Lakers look like contenders is how they played without Dončić (lower back tightness) on Sunday against the Pistons.

Austin Reaves (15) of the Los Angeles Lakers reacts after a late second-half 3-point basket against the Detroit Pistons in Detroit, Sunday, Nov. 8, 2026. Getty Images

Without the league's current front-runner for the MVP Award and against a Pistons team with the third-highest defensive rating, Reaves scored a season-high 43 points in the Lakers' 121-116 win.

He reminded everyone of what he can do when he's unleashed.

Reaves has quietly been putting together a spectacular season. He's averaging 25.3 points on a very efficient 50.8% from the field and 41.3% from beyond the arc.

He's the 15th leading scorer in the league, and of those ahead of him, only Giannis Antetokounmpo, Anthony Davis and Victor Wembanyama have higher field-goal percentages than Reaves.

And only Jamal Murray and Steph Curry are shooting at a higher percentage from beyond the 3-point line.

Dončić and Reaves are the NBA's highest-scoring duo by a mile, averaging 64.6 points, 12.6 more than the second-leading duo of Steph Curry and Yaxel Lendeborg, who are averaging a combined 52 points per game.

Then there's Kessler, who's averaging a double-double with 14.9 points and 10.8 rebounds per game.

When Reaves was out for Monday's 124-114 win over the Hawks because of an illness, Dončić got help from the Lakers' reserves, with Collin Sexton having an 18-point, six-assist performance and Sandro Mamukelashvili pouring in 23 points and grabbing eight rebounds.

Los Angeles Lakers center Walker Kessler (14) shoots against Atlanta Hawks center Jock Landale (31) during an NBA basketball game, Monday, Nov. 9, 2026, in Atlanta. AP Photo/Mike Stewart

The Lakers have the highest offensive rating in the league at 121.5. And even though they're 19th in defensive rating (113.8), they rank second in the league in steals per game (10.8), second in total deflections (240) and third in blocks per game (6.4).

Kessler leads the NBA in contested shots (140).

Heck, even Dončić, who has notoriously been slammed for his defense throughout his career, is showing a lot of effort on the less glamorous end of the court. He leads the league in defensive loose balls recovered (nine) and is tied for second in charges drawn (four).

Entering this season, it was presumed that the Lakers needed time to jell. It seemed as though they had major holes. They weren't ready to compete with the league's elite teams, such as the Thunder, Spurs or Knicks.

The Lakers have sprinted past their doubters, proving that they're a team to watch.

#2962. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.
